# Creative Science

Creative as the primary performance lever: diversity, fatigue, volume economics, testing structures, hooks.

Part of the [[00-Codex|Advertising Science Codex]]. Claims follow the tier system (T1 docs, T2 shown test, T3 practitioner, T4 theory).

## Claims

## Why creative now decides delivery

### CR-001 · Meta built Andromeda explicitly to absorb a rapidly growing ad-creative volume driven by Advantage+ automation and GenAI creative tools
Tier: T1 · Status: active
Meta's engineering post states Advantage+ automation increases the number of eligible ads, and GenAI creative generation is expected to grow creative volume significantly: more than a million advertisers used its generative AI tools to create more than 15 million ads in a month. The retrieval engine was scaled so this larger, more diverse candidate pool can be personalized per person. This is the system-level reason creative diversity gives an account more surface area in retrieval.
Sources: Engineering at Meta, Meta Andromeda post, 2024-12-02
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-002 · Meta reports +22% ROAS for advertisers turning on Advantage+ creative features and +7% conversions from GenAI image generation
Tier: T1 · Status: active
In the Andromeda post, Meta cites a +22% increase in ROAS for advertisers who turn on Advantage+ creative features and a +7% increase in conversions from its image generation tool. These are Meta's own stated numbers for letting the system vary creative per person.
Four more of Meta's own figures, all read off in-product surfaces rather than the engineering blog, and all materially smaller than the +22%. Inside the AI business assistant's recommendations: standard enhancements 3% lower cost per result, generative AI creative variety 10% higher click-through rate, maximize value of conversions 7% higher ROAS. At the media upload step, where Meta auto-generates image variations from your uploaded image, the on-screen copy claims an 11% CTR lift and an 8% commercial rate lift. Record two things about these. They are marketing copy inside a product surface, with no methodology, no population and no date attached, so they are Meta's claims rather than Meta's evidence. And the gap between +22% for the bundle and +3% for standard enhancements is large enough that nobody should quote the 22% as the expected return on flipping a toggle. Heath's own read of the auto-generated image variations, which is the operator layer on top: colour and background variants of your own image are usable, variants that swap the person or the body shape are not.
Sources: Engineering at Meta, Meta Andromeda post, 2024-12-02; Ben Heath, How Good Is Meta's New AI Business Assistant?, 2026-06-03; Ben Heath, How To Use Creative Enhancements on Your Facebook Ads, 2026-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-003 · Semantic content features from foundation models let Meta rank new ads with no engagement history (cold start)
Tier: T1 · Status: active
Meta's multi-stage ranking architecture integrates semantic content features from foundation models specifically to support cold-start scenarios, meaning new ads or advertisers with limited historical engagement data. A fresh creative is scored partly on what it IS (content understanding), not only on accumulated engagement.
Sources: Engineering at Meta, From User Sequences to Scaling Laws, 2026-08-05
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-004 · Meta's stated GEM roadmap is a multimodal whole-ecosystem model that ranks organic content and ads together and understands creative across text, image, audio, and video
Tier: T1 · Status: active
Future direction in the GEM post: learning from Meta's entire ecosystem including user interactions on organic and ads content across text, images, audio, and video, plus a unified engagement model that can rank both organic content and ads, inference-time scaling, and agentic advertiser automation. Creative that competes with organic content quality is the implied bar.
Sources: Engineering at Meta, Meta's Generative Ads Model (GEM), 2025-11-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

## Avatar and persona positioning

### CR-005 · Niching the same product's creative to one untapped avatar sharply cuts acquisition cost and unlocks scale
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Nick Theriot showed a handbag account where generalized women-targeted creative at just under $1,000/day produced a $73 cost per purchase; the same product niched to women who crochet/knit at the same spend produced $35, roughly half. Fraser Cottrell describes the same mechanism (a heavy-duty soap repositioned from builders to dirt bikers) as giving Meta clean signals into an uncontested delivery pool. Nick's caveat: the blue-ocean avatar must still have a large TAM or the win does not scale.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16; Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-006 · Repositioning a product onto a higher-value avatar or a new desire can multiply profit several-fold without changing the product
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Breath-spray client repositioned from college kids to cigar smokers (more disposable income, product reads as cheap to them): monthly sales went from ~$270k to $472k and monthly profit from ~$28k to ~$90k, with the avatar shift moving profit from ~$30k to over $100k/month within 30-60 days; dashboards were shown. A separate client whose anxiety-positioned product plateaued at $100k/month scaled to $1M/month after repositioning as a fun girls' night activity, a simultaneous avatar and desire change (numbers told, not shown). Retold 2026-06-15 with fuller figures and one inconsistency worth carrying. The product is paint-by-numbers, monthly revenue went from $130,000 to $1.1 million and monthly profit from $19,000 to $198,000, and the only lever named is the desire. The two positions verbatim: "POV you're my emotional support art project" against "this is my girlies who need a girls' night soon." His plateau figure moves between tellings, $100k in one and $130k in the other, no dashboard was shown either time, and the case is described as "a while back", so treat the multiple as directional and the profit figures as recalled. Blue Sense adds the downstream argument from a Gruns teardown: one SKU (daily greens gummies) is run as at least six distinct products (gut health for bloating, GLP-1 support, fiber for digestion, hair health, cognition and focus, multivitamin for business professionals), and each persona unlocks an offer that would be irrelevant to 90% of buyers under generic positioning (a GLP-1 partner discount, a free extra 30 days justified by a 120-day hair-health study). Repositioning is therefore the precondition for offer differentiation, not only a creative lever, and its contribution-margin impact is zero or positive. Competitor teardown, asserted, no performance data shown.
The messaging consequence, which is the step operators skip after picking a new segment: the BENEFIT you lead with has to change with the segment. Ben Heath's rule for a high-net-worth segment is to drop price competitiveness entirely and lead on time saving, convenience or status, "that's not who you're going after, they don't care about that as much." His own worked case is a subtraction rather than an addition: if he targeted only ecommerce businesses above $10m of revenue, he would remove lead quality from all of his messaging, because that segment does not care about it. Repositioning is therefore a deletion exercise as much as a rewriting one, and the audit is which of your current proof points the new segment would skip.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; It Took Me 10 Years to Learn This Secret Facebook Ads Scaling Strategy, 2026-07-24; I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads, 2026-08-14; Blue Sense Digital, How 8 and 9 Figure Brands Build Offers Without Discounting, 2026-06-08; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-007 · Niche one level deep only; TAM size caps what any creative can scale
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Winning avatars are one broad attribute (cigar smokers, women who crochet), never stacked qualifiers. His illustration: ~1.2 million US doctors vs ~400 neurosurgeons; a hook calling out neurosurgeons has at most 400 qualified viewers and can never scale regardless of quality. Validate the avatar has real online mass before committing: multiple TikTok videos on the niche with 25K+ views, ideally 100K+. Blue Sense adds the operating consequence: persona specificity sets a maximum daily spend ceiling for the ad, so a hook calling out "e-commerce CFOs in Australia owning over $100 million" addresses maybe 1,000-2,000 people and $10,000/day behind it just hits the same thousand repeatedly. His prescription is not broader callouts, because broad callouts demand creative good enough to resonate with everyone. Stay hyper-specific, find the persona's daily spend ceiling, hold spend there and rotate creative into it; personas that churn (marathon trainees) refill. He puts genuinely broad top-of-funnel unaware ads out of reach below roughly $50M revenue.
The same operator argued the opposite five months earlier and the inconsistency should lower confidence in his number. On 2026-01-22 he graded a persona callout ("are your gym sessions under 60 minutes?") as commercially wrong because "that ad won't be able to spend $4 million, it's going to tap out particularly in the Australian market", and put the scaling hook at a belief the whole market holds instead ("drinking three litres of water a day doesn't mean that you're hydrated"). On 2026-06-15 he told operators to stay hyper-specific and live inside the ceiling. Both positions accept that specificity caps spend; they disagree on whether the operator should accept the cap or write past it, and no spend curve was shown either time. The market-wide belief challenge is the reconcilable version: it is still one narrow claim, it just picks a belief with a large holder pool rather than a demographic with a small one.
The same operator restated the view-count validation on 2026-06-15 with the desire, rather than the avatar, as the unit being sized: search the desire on TikTok and YouTube and read the view counts, where "a couple videos with a thousand views" means there is no market and "a whole bunch of videos with multiple millions of views" means a large desire. It is a free pre-commit check with no calibration behind it, and it does not distinguish a desire that is large from one that is merely entertaining to watch. Same method as [[Creative Science#CR-156|CR-156]] pointed at market size instead of at format.
The Blue Sense inconsistency is now three-way and the earliest instance is 2025-10-30, which pushes it back another seven months. There he ties avatar WIDTH directly to spend capacity and prescribes the opposite of hyper-specificity: "is also not just speaking to one particular avatar, not just a mum who's aged 35 with some skin problems. You need to speak to a very wide avatar so it scales on top of funnel so it reaches a bunch of people and can hold a quarter million a month at ad spend." So across ten months the same operator has argued wide (2025-10-30), wide (2026-01-22) and narrow (2026-06-15), always from the same premise that specificity caps spend. He has never shown a spend curve. Take the premise, which all three tellings share and which is independently supported by the cluster-saturation mechanism in [[Creative Science#CR-028|CR-028]], and treat his prescription as unsettled.
Ben Heath argues the narrow side for SMALL BUDGETS specifically, which is the one framing that makes the disagreement tractable: write off 99% of the market deliberately and build everything for the remaining 1% or less. "We're not going to touch 99% of this market that technically could buy our product or service. Instead, we're going to be hyper-specific for this 1% or sometimes even less that we can tailor everything towards." His argument is competitive, not statistical: a small advertiser cannot out-spend, out-brand or out-produce the large one, so it out-specifies. That reconciles most of the fight, because the spend ceiling specificity imposes is only a problem for an advertiser large enough to hit it.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Sending Cold Traffic on Meta to PDPs, 2025-10-30; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-008 · Self-target with scenario dissection at the psychology level, not demographic callouts in the hook
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Pre-Andromeda hooks like "hey, you a woman 30 to 60" are too broad now. The working method dissects a familiar situation ("It's 11am, you're starving, you grab a $5 protein bar, 300+ calories, you eat half and it goes stale in your bag") so only matching sub-personas recognize themselves and keep watching. That both filters viewers and feeds Meta precise delivery signals. Blue Sense states the same rule harder across two videos: "Persona call outs are super overrated and it's because no one wants to hear themselves called out. People want to hear the problem that they're actually dealing with." He reports first-person that ads opening "agency owners who need X, listen up" get skipped, while a specific problem agitation holds him. Second, delivery-side reason: Meta auto-transcribes every ad, so a literal persona callout routes delivery to that persona even when the rest of the creative has nothing to do with them, wasting the impression. Two caveats he does not resolve. In the same transcript a VSL breakdown praises a persona-callout opener ("You have IBS, leaky gut, or bloating?"), so the rule is not absolute. And it sits against [[Creative Science#CR-070|CR-070]], which puts the avatar callout as job one. The reconcilable version: a callout does route delivery but underperforms as a hook, so put the callout inside the problem framing rather than in a literal "if you're a mom aged 30-35" opener.
The same operator supplied the mechanism that closes his own contradiction on 2026-01-29. His four-beat hook is persona, problem, pattern interrupt, promise, and the third beat exists precisely because of the callout's cost: "once you've called out a persona and you've problem agitated, you're going to lose a lot of people cuz they might know that an ad's coming." So a callout is usable when a pattern interrupt is built to catch the drop it causes, and unusable when it stands alone. Hard constraint attached: casting must match the callout, since a 50-year-old woman cannot open with a callout to 20-year-old men without breaking relevance and trust.
Two operators independently reject demographics as an avatar definition. Alex Cooper: put a brand on the spot and it answers "male, 35 to 55, suburbs, $100k a year", which is not a persona. A persona states what the customer believes, fears and values and the exact words they use, and his test is whether you can answer what they believe, what they have tried and failed, and what they fear. Ezra Firestone shows why the band is useless with a hair-colour brand doing tens of millions across CVS, Sally's, Sprouts and Target on a nominal "women 25 to 55": inside that one band sit gray root touch-up for women in their 40s and 50s dyeing back to natural, and balayage support for brown-haired women adding blonde, while a second brand of his sells the opposite promise, embracing the silver grow-out, to women in their 60s and 70s. Same category, same broad demographic, three different problems. The promise attaches to the lived experience and the problem, never to the age bracket.
B2B is the standing exception and it is stated as a flat rule by a search-side team: put the buyer's JOB in the first five words, using the verbs their employer pays them to perform, reduce, automate, ship, track, restock, forecast, qualify, generate, prevent, reconcile. "put the buyer's job, what it is that they do in the first five words. This is immediately going to create relevance for them. They are going to see themselves in your ad." Their named failure mode is the self-referential opener, introducing, all-in-one, next-gen, revolutionary. Note what is actually being called out: a job is a set of tasks and problems, not an age bracket or a job title in isolation, so this is closer to the problem-first rule above than it first reads. The genuine tension is with the delivery-side objection, that a literal callout routes delivery to the named group whether or not the rest of the creative serves them. T4, asserted, no data, and it comes from search rather than from a personalised feed, where the delivery objection does not apply.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Andrew Faris, Ezra Firestone: Nothing Has Changed In Ecom In 20 Years, 2026-05-15; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-009 · Three ecom competition strategies work in 2026: bought social proof, improved product, or same product for a new avatar; only the avatar play is cheap
Tier: T3 · Status: active
IM8 scales on celebrity social proof (~850k monthly visits, reported ~$10M/mo revenue, $1B growth financing commitment July 2026). Gruns scales on an improved-mechanism product (greens as gummy; founder exit reported over $1B; $6M/$11M/$35M rounds). Black Girl Vitamins scales commodity products for one avatar (4.2M monthly visits, no reported funding). The avatar strategy requires only research.
IM8's creative execution of the social-proof play is worth copying independently of the celebrity: it runs a green-screen third-party-style business breakdown of itself, through its own page, opening on "$100 million ARR in 11 months" and then arguing that the David Beckham co-founder link is NOT the reason for the growth. Citing your own revenue as the proof point, then disowning the obvious explanation, uses Beckham's status while reading as objective analysis. The ad never says buy and never claims best product; it moves to studies, then reviews, then testimonials, then other celebrity endorsements around the 40-second mark. Two weeks live with 2,400 likes, which is an engagement proxy for spend, not spend.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-010 · In a sophisticated market with no unique mechanism, avatar trial-and-error takes about 3 months before one unlocks scale
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A client in an oversaturated category with no unique mechanism needed ~3 months of avatar testing (6-7 month engagement total) before one avatar unlocked the business. Contrast: the cigar-spray client's avatar disconnect was obvious on audit and fixed within 30-60 days. The clearer the creative-avatar mismatch, the faster the fix.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads, 2026-08-14
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-011 · Effective TAM equals the number of category entry points a product supports
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Beyond raw buyer headcount, TAM widens with the number of distinct value propositions a product can carry (the Mars Man hormone supplement spans brain health, heart health, training, strength). A product with many entry points scales faster because message variety, the core creative lever, is structurally available; a niche product like a $200 baseball-glove wallet caps out regardless of execution.
Nick Theriot states the same structure with DESIRE as the unit and gives the operating trigger the Faris version lacks: one product carries many desires that vary in size, so a scaling plateau is a signal the current desire's market is exhausted rather than a delivery problem. His escalation order is to try new ways to communicate the same desire first, and only after roughly six months stuck at the same ceiling to go looking for a different desire entirely. That converts a plateau into a diagnosis instead of a fatigue argument, and it is the mechanism underneath the repositioning case in [[Creative Science#CR-006|CR-006]]. Model, no data attached to the six-month figure.
Sources: Andrew Faris, You're Working Hard On The Wrong Problem, 2026-07-27; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-136 · Mass desire cannot be created, only transferred, so a campaign whose job is to educate a market into wanting the outcome is misbuilt
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Cited from Eugene Schwartz and used as a gate before any campaign: "If you're trying to educate your market, just give up. Instead, you should channel the existing desire into your product." If the ad's job is to convince people they should want the outcome at all, the problem is the offer, not the copy. Operationally this kills category-education campaigns for small advertisers and pushes them toward an existing high-intensity desire the product can be attached to. It sits upstream of the awareness-stage rules in [[Creative Science#CR-021|CR-021]] and [[Creative Science#CR-022|CR-022]]: awareness stage decides how you talk, desire transfer decides whether you should be in the market at all. It also pairs with [[Creative Science#CR-083|CR-083]], where the same desire varies in intensity by season. Asserted as a principle from a 1966 book, no test attached.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-148 · Two ads pointing at the same page recruit different buyers: the same jacket returned $178 AOV from a discount-led static and $320 from a styling ad
Tier: T3 · Status: active
On a women's clothing account, two ads sending traffic to the identical page produced an AOV of $178 and $320. Mechanism: the creative selects the buyer, so a price-led ad recruits price-conscious single-item buyers and a styling or problem-solving ad recruits multi-item buyers. He verified the composition with custom conversion events showing one ad sold only the jacket while the other sold the jacket plus two or three items. Numbers asserted with the mechanism named, not shown on screen.
Operator move: report AOV and basket composition per ad, not only CPA and ROAS, and treat AOV divergence between ads as an audience-quality signal rather than noise. This is transaction-level evidence for MD-001 and [[Creative Science#CR-005|CR-005]], that the creative is the targeting, and it is a reason a lower-ROAS ad can be the better one to fund.
The general mechanism, from two operators. Charley T: "every ad you run effectively creates its own lookalike audience based on who responds, if you run five different creatives, they will all find different pockets of people", so once targeting is broad the instruction is to load multiple creatives and read who bought from which ad rather than pre-defining segments with filters and exclusions. Blue Sense names the four inputs Meta reads to decide who to serve, which is the actionable version: the copy, the creative, the TRANSCRIPT (the voice inside the video), and the landing page. Change the words and you change the audience. He notes the same four are what Meta scans to detect exploitative health targeting, so the mechanism cuts both ways. Both asserted from reasoning, no test.
The cost side nobody budgets for, from Charley T: every new ad brings a different type of person into the store, and every decision the store has already made on email, search, CRO and pricing was tuned to the customers you previously acquired. A new ad producing a cheap first sale from someone far less likely to buy again makes the business progressively more dependent on first-purchase economics. That is a real argument against uncapped creative volume ([[Creative Science#CR-026|CR-026]]) that does not rely on delivery mechanics at all, and it is checkable: segment repeat rate by acquiring ad. His tightest statement of it, 2026-01-21: "the more ads you run, the more dissimilar all of your traffic will look. So, the less likely anybody is to convert on your landing page. And all of the decisions you made for CRO and search and email today are completely undermined if all of your ads look different tomorrow." Note the prediction it makes, which nobody has checked: site conversion rate should FALL as live-ad count rises, holding spend constant. That is directly measurable in any account that has changed its ad count materially.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The BEST Facebook Ads Strategy for 2026 Post Andromeda, 2025-12-27; Professor Charley T, Do these 5 things each day to scale Facebook Ads, 2026-02-21; Professor Charley T, Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies, 2026-04-15; Blue Sense Digital, How Machine Learning Works in Meta Ads (2025), 2025-03-25; Professor Charley T, If You Run Facebook Ads... ROAS Doesn't Matter in 2026, 2026-01-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

## Hooks

### CR-012 · How much of a hook is the visual versus the copy?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Nick Theriot: for photo ads the hook is the image and the text hook together; for video it is what people see AND hear in the first 3 seconds. In his $130-watch example the text hook is only about 25% of the hook and the visuals (luxury box, lighting, slow-mo close-ups) are 75%, so judging a hook on copy alone misses most of the stopping power. Mark Builds Brands inverts the weighting: "roughly the copy is going to be about 80%. The next 15% is going to be the scroll stopper, and the final 5% will be the audio." He calls audio the smallest needle mover and treats the human-versus-AI voiceover choice as near-irrelevant. Neither side showed comparative data; both are practitioner assertions. The split may be category-dependent, since Theriot's example is a visually-demonstrable ecom product and Mark's accounts lean lead-gen. Mark's visual-selection test is usable regardless of the weighting: a scroll stopper qualifies only if a naive viewer says out loud "what did I just watch" after seeing it.
A third position sits between them and it is an EFFORT split rather than a contribution split, which is what keeps it from counting as a vote. Matt Shiver puts 80% of his own effort into the creative asset and 20% into the copy. Nothing was measured; he is describing where his hours go. Read it as a production-allocation habit, not as support for the visual side of the argument above.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Dr. Matt Shiver, Facebook Ads Tutorial for Beginners Updated for 2026, 2026-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-013 · A scalable hook needs four properties: calls out the ideal customer, intrigues curiosity, addresses a large TAM, and differs from every competitor
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Stop the scroll of the ideal customer, create curiosity to keep watching, target a desire held by a large total addressable market, and differentiate visually or verbally from competitors. Hooks that copy the niche's standard pattern do not work even when well executed. One example ad meeting all four spent almost $200,000.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-014 · The highest-leverage 2026 hook pattern is desired outcome WITHOUT the mainstream solution's hated symptom
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Across $4.3M spent in 30 days, the lead hook archetype calls out what the audience wants but cannot have due to a symptom of the current mainstream solution: "If you're over 40, here's how to lose weight without needles" (vs Ozempic's needle fear). Formula: [outcome] + without + [the specific thing people hate about the dominant solution], peeling off the segment blocked by that objection.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spent $4.3M on Facebook Ads Last Month, 2026-08-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-015 · An 18-archetype hook library is scaling in 2026: problem/solution, failed-solutions list, David-vs-Goliath, desired state, news-style, testimonial, before/after, mechanism-question and more
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The scaling set includes: problem then solution ("Cigar breath, never again with this"); list of failed solutions then yours; David vs Goliath ("four cigars later and she doesn't smell a thing"); show desired state; offer and offer-ending FOMO; problem as question; problem as news story; testimonial; before-and-after; "not hard if you have the right system"; current solution's flaw; competitor superiority for stage-4 sophistication; ditched-mainstream-solution; speed to result ("30 seconds and she can't smell a thing"); new mechanism plus superiority; mechanism-question ("what would happen to your acne if the cells fueling it were shut down?"). Compiled from ads that scaled during a $4.3M month.
Two more templates from the same operator on 2026-08-19, both carried by ads whose spend was shown live in Ads Manager, though neither hook is isolated from the other changes in those ads: the rejected-solution confession, "why I'll never use [X solution] to [X activity] in [X place] again", with the payoff deliberately withheld to the caption; and the situational summons, "watch this if you [activity] at [verb moment]". Both are curiosity gaps rather than claims, which is why they pair with the withheld-payoff loop device in [[Creative Science#CR-157|CR-157]].
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spent $4.3M on Facebook Ads Last Month, 2026-08-10; Nick Theriot, How To Test Facebook Ads in 2026 When You Can't Find a Winner, 2026-08-19
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-016 · Making the viewer FEEL an emotion through a story beats naming the emotion; deliberate rage-bait hooks produced some of his best ads ever
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The failure mode is selling to an emotion directly ("do you feel sad that dogs are in shelters?"); the working version shows a story that induces the feeling, then introduces the product as the resolution. He uses an emotion-wheel prompt to generate hooks that intentionally upset the ideal customer ("Most cigar guys think their wife is overreacting. She's not."), then gives the solution.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-017 · Polarizing belief-based hooks self-select the audience, then a trusted proxy executes the belief change
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A sunscreen street-interview ad opens "sunscreen is worse than the sun", keeping anti-sunscreen viewers and scrolling everyone else away, cleaning the audience Meta optimizes into. Because the viewer identifies with the interviewee, his on-camera flip to accepting the natural-ingredient product transfers the belief change. Under-education kills the same structure: a dandruff-shampoo ad failed because the antifungal mechanism got 2 sentences instead of the ~15 extra seconds it needed.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale (12 Real Examples), 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-018 · A low hook rate does not mean a losing ad; judge winners on CPA and CTR, not thumbstop
Tier: T2 · Status: active
His Aloha problem-solution ad had only a 21% hook rate but a 2.2% click-through rate and a strong CPA, because the fewer people who stayed were precisely the interested ones. An ad can deliberately filter hard at the top and still acquire customers cheaply; hook rate is not the winner/loser metric.
Blue Sense reaches the same conclusion from the delivery side and states it harder: "having the highest hook rate possible isn't actually the objective of creative... you want to hook the right people so that the algorithm reinforces into that as a soft metric and understands who to prioritize serving the ad to." His worked failure is a jerky ad opening on an unidentifiable product being eaten, which manufactures intrigue and would score a high hook rate. Vegetarians and everyone else stop equally, so the early soft signal is noise and the system cannot work out who to prioritise while purchase data is still thin. Hook the right people, not the most people. Both are practitioner readings; neither ran a hook-rate-versus-CPA regression.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-019 · Reframe price against what the customer already spends, not against competing solutions
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Anchor the price to an existing approved purchase: "for less than the cost of one premium cigar, never hear you smell like an ashtray again." The reframe converts an unfamiliar price comparison into a familiar, already-approved spend.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-020 · A specific exaggerated claim beats a generic category claim
Tier: T3 · Status: active
When every competitor says "lose fat", saying "lose 18 lb in 4 weeks" makes yours the only concrete ad and the consumer's choice becomes trivial. Escalation applies inside creative styles too: when everyone shows a streamer reacting, the next winner smashes the computer with a bat, because the baseline reaction is stale.
A cheap version of the same lever is counting the claim. Rewriting a jerky hook, Blue Sense names the word "two" as the load-bearing copy in "we found the same two problems in all of them": a counted claim bounds the time commitment the viewer is agreeing to, so they decide they can sit through it, while "we found problems in all of them" reads as a sales setup and fails on specificity. He predicts a worse hook rate for the uncounted version and did not test it. Cheapest application of this claim: put a number on any hook that currently carries a vague quantity.
Sources: Nick Theriot, It Took Me 10 Years to Learn This Secret Facebook Ads Scaling Strategy, 2026-07-24; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-021 · For problem-aware markets, lead with the new mechanism, not the problem, and introduce the mechanism before naming the product
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Nick: the acne market already knows it has acne; the creative leads with "new viral laser treatment that vanishes acne in 30 minutes", never "do you struggle with acne?". Blue Sense: naming the product before explaining the mechanism (the Medicube ad) weakens the sell; explain the ingredient/mechanism, then reveal the product. Blue Sense adds a compliance note: full-screen before/afters draw higher CPMs or disapproval on Meta, but a small flashing corner before/after slips through, and an anti-sell ("works so well it has side effects if misapplied") is a strong credibility device.
The bridge he grades highest runs the mechanism as a GENERIC solution first and lets the product enter as packaging convenience: state the generalised fix (rebalance the bacteria, calm the inflammation), name the four ingredients that do it, explain what each ingredient does, then close on "rather than having to go and buy four different tablets from four different retailers, we've combined them all." The product is never positioned as the answer, only as the easy way to get the answer. The contrast case he grades as the failure point of an otherwise strong ad is a bridge that pivots to "that's why everybody's been calling this tank the celebrity", which converts the whole asset into an obvious pitch in one sentence.
Sources: Nick Theriot, It Took Me 10 Years to Learn This Secret Facebook Ads Scaling Strategy, 2026-07-24; Blue Sense Digital, Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale (12 Real Examples), 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-022 · For unaware markets and stage-5 sophistication, the hook must sell watching the video, not the product
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Stage-5 sophistication opens the same way as an unaware market: the hook's only job is earning the next seconds of attention. His cat-toy example asks "who has a better life, cats or dogs?", magnifies a problem (58% of indoor cats show depression signs), sets up a new mechanism, then adds origin story, authority, twist and objection handling; the product intro is deliberately delayed. Analysis of a third-party ad he admires, not one he ran.
Sources: Nick Theriot, Top Creatives I'm Running For 30K Days In July 2026, 2026-07-29
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-023 · Solution-aware headlines have two legal moves: call out the desire, or lead with the product and bridge to desire, chosen by market sophistication
Tier: T4 · Status: active
From Breakthrough Advertising: at lower sophistication call out the desire ("how to lose 47 lb in 30 days or your money back"); in a more sophisticated marketplace lead with the product/solution ("new wonder drug for losing weight") then bridge to desire. Book-derived framework, no test data.
Sources: Nick Theriot, NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-123 · Grade every hook on five criteria (clarity, relevance, novelty, specificity, credibility) and accept a couple scored well rather than all five
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Definitions as given. Clarity: a stranger understands the subject inside 3 seconds, not a deliberate confusion play. Relevance: problem agitation, explicitly not a persona callout (see [[Creative Science#CR-008|CR-008]]). Novelty: "purple ocean" white space, a repositioning that is adjacent-but-novel rather than a true blue ocean with no proven demand, tested by asking whether the hook has been done a million times. Specificity: numbers, names, quantified outcomes, which is where authority comes from. Credibility: usually carried by the visual, a domain-relevant figure appearing on screen rather than a generic lab coat, not by the copy. A good hook does not need 10/10 on all five. Stated across two videos by the same operator; no scoring data shown, so this is a rubric, not a measured model. This is the same five-criteria set the workspace hook scorecard runs, and it is a distinct framework from the four properties in [[Creative Science#CR-013|CR-013]].
Stated a third time on 2026-01-29 with a priority order the later videos drop: the five are listed in order of importance, and specificity and credibility are explicitly second tier, "because fitting credibility into a hook within the first 3 to 4 seconds is difficult to do, not really a priority, but if you can do it nice." So clarity and relevance are the pass/fail pair and the last two are upside. His shortcut for buying credibility without spending hook seconds is casting: a known influencer or famous person supplies it on sight.
Extension of the credibility criterion from the same review: tie the benefit to a status figure the buyer wants to resemble. A fashion ad spends its 4-second to 15-second window connecting tank-top fit to Channing Tatum, Brad Pitt, Mark Wahlberg and David Beckham. The mechanism is that an unknown creator faces a standing trust objection, who is this person and why would I take clothing advice from him, and anchoring on people the target already wants to look like converts the claim into a desire the viewer holds. The same device doubles as a hook, because four named people with an unstated commonality opens an information gap.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-124 · How much of an ad's outcome does the hook carry?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Two linked mechanics from the same operator. Production allocation: about 80% of viewers never watch past the hook, and the spread available from swapping hooks is wider than the spread from swapping bodies, so at equal production time 2 bodies x 15 hooks beats 5 bodies x 2 hooks. His caveat is explicit: if the body scripting is weak, one body may accidentally be the winner and the leverage read is wrong. Three hook layers are independently testable, visual, audio, and overlaid copy including primary text. Shoot six hook takes at recording time; it costs a few extra minutes. Delivery mechanic: a new hook gets its own creative ID, so Meta will not bundle it with the source asset the way it bundles near-duplicates, and it reaches a novel audience despite the remaining 87 seconds being identical. His reported result: ads that fatigued after $50-100k of spend absorbed another $100k at the same efficiency after 50 new hooks were rotated on, and the 50 hooks took about an hour to shoot. Numbers asserted, no dashboard shown. Second use: rescue an ad that failed on hook rate while its body scripting was sound. The bundling side of this is [[Creative Science#CR-028|CR-028]] and MD-003.
His hardest version, 2026-01-29: "the hook is 80% of performance. Why? Well, because 80% of people never make it past the hook." Both 80s are rules of thumb with no retention curve behind them, and the second does not license the first. Same video gives the three-lever production arithmetic: visual scroll stopper, audio (a function of the script), and copy burned onto the creative, which is separate from the headline and primary text fields. Crossing the three yields 20 to 30 hook variants from a handful of scripts, and he claims adding one exaggerated overlay line such as "this transformed my life" doubled hook rate and creative performance on an otherwise mediocre hook. No before-and-after shown for the doubling. Charley T supplies the cheapest corroboration available: he published a video, watched it become his worst launch in 18 months, pulled it after two hours, changed only the hook and thumbnail, relaunched the identical body and it performed. That was YouTube organic with no view numbers, so treat it as a re-hook-before-you-write-off rule rather than a paid finding.
Contested by Andrew Faris, who puts the ceiling on this. "The message is the most important part of the ad, and hooky replacements for the message only get you so far." He uses it as a first-principles constraint on how much team time goes to AI hook generators, says he would change his mind on data showing otherwise, and says he does not see that data. Jeremy Haynes runs the same position operationally and further: his 25 to 30 ads per ad set are 25 to 30 separately filmed reasons to buy, and he is explicit that they are NOT a different hook attached to a shared body and CTA.

THE BEST CASE ON RECORD, 2026-01-08, and it names the ceiling instead of denying it. A video testimonial launched 27 April with 2 bodies and 9 hooks became the account's best ever ad, "about 5x better than anything else", then fatigued at 6 to 7 months. Adding 4 newly shot hooks across the same 2 bodies returned it to the account's top two ads at the same daily spend throughput. The load-bearing detail is WHAT THE SWAP WAS: "we didn't just take more hooks from the existing content and repurpose it onto the existing content. Instead, what we did is we actually shot completely new hooks of a different person." The stated mechanism is market exposure, since the audience already saw that video with that hook and skipped it, and a rewrite does not clear that: "often just a change in wording can get a lot of people in, but it's not going to get everyone in. We do need a change in visuals." So the swap with a real revival behind it is a NEW SHOOT with a NEW AVATAR, which sits between a near-free rewrite and Haynes's separately filmed arguments and is closer to Haynes than the near-free framing implies. Ad set shown on screen with variants and dates; the 5x and the throughput claim are stated from memory with no CPA, ROAS or spend, so T3.
His cost arithmetic and the gap in it. On a 75-second video, "majority of the effort in shooting and editing is actually in the last 73 seconds here. So, we can go and make 10 ads for the same effort as making one body." That counts EDIT effort only. It does not cover the shoot day a different person on camera requires, which is exactly what his own revival needed. Read 10-for-1 as an editing ratio, never as a production ratio. Four levers he rotates on a fatiguing winner, in his order: angle, offer, avatar, format. This revival moved angle and avatar only, holding offer and format for the next one. One concrete format-lever build: replace the first 3 seconds with a static or motion-graphic card, for example B-roll under a written testimonial, that then cuts into the existing body. Floor under variant count with no hit rate attached: "the odds that you're going to get a really high performing hook when you're only testing three is actually pretty low." Boundary the same operator sets against himself: hook variations are for reviving an asset that ALREADY earned scale, never for rescuing a starved ad set, where the instruction is "ideally this is diverse creative. This is nothing like what we launched here. We're not doing slight hook variations."
Nick Theriot lands on the new-argument side, and the disagreement is partly definitional. His hook swap IS the three variants inside one ad idea, where "the text hook is the same, the script, the voiceover, literally everything's identical. The only difference is the visual hook, the first three to five seconds" (see [[Creative Science#CR-035|CR-035]]). Under that definition a hook swap can never be a new argument, because the argument is held constant by construction. His actual breakout on 2026-08-19 changed four things at once against the account's history, a new avatar, a new creative style, a curiosity text hook and an unusual filming location, and the campaign moved from a $98 cost per purchase to $51 on the ad's first day live and to about $45 across the following Friday to Sunday on $2,000 of spend, read off Ads Manager (T2 for the account numbers, T3 for attributing them to any one of the four changes). Set that beside Blue Sense's "completely new hooks of a different person" and the two describe THE SAME MOVE under different names: one operator calls a new avatar on camera a hook swap, the other calls it a new argument. Record that plainly. The roster's disagreement on this law is partly a vocabulary problem, and the empirical question that survives it is narrower than the debate: does re-cutting the first 3 to 5 seconds of an EXISTING shoot revive a fatigued winner? Blue Sense says no, from a case where he chose not to try it. Nobody has run it.
The delivery objection, both sides, because the whole claim rests on it. Ben Heath, 2026-03-03: "post-Andromeda Meta will see ads with different hooks as the same ad and only enter one into the auction, which can happen", and his workaround is Meta's creative testing tool ([[Creative Science#CR-113|CR-113]]). Blue Sense rebuts it directly from account observation: "it might count the bodies as the same ad and group them together. Well, I can tell you it doesn't because this is still working incredibly well in our accounts." Neither shows delivery data. Same question as MD-003 near-duplicate collapse; read it there before acting on either.
FOLKLORE, kept so nobody relearns it. The percentages in circulation are 90% ("the hook is really 90% of the importance of a creative because 90% of people don't make it past the hook", Blue Sense 2026-01-08), 80% from the same operator on 2026-01-29, and 80% again from Nick Theriot ("creative hook is responsible for 80% performance of that particular creative"). Not one is attached to a retention curve, a shown test, or a study anyone quoting it has read. The nearest identifiable origin is David Ogilvy's print-headline observation, that roughly five times as many people read the headline as the body copy and that writing the headline spends eighty cents of the dollar, which is a magazine readership figure restated decades later as a video retention statistic. Ben Heath's 8% to 33% hook-rate example, which he presents as illustrative and not from a named account, is the same argument with invented numbers. All of it is T4. Our own thumbstop and retention data can test it directly and should.
PRACTICAL RESOLUTION, revised 2026-08-19, and it replaces the old wording. Split the term before using it. A rewrite or re-cut of an existing shoot is near-free, should still be exhausted before an asset is retired, and has no evidence behind it as a revival move. A new-avatar reshoot of the first seconds is the swap with a real case attached, it is not free, and it is a smaller version of a new argument rather than an alternative to one. A genuinely new argument is what wins when volume against one angle has stalled. Nobody on either side ran a controlled hook-swap-versus-message-swap test, so the ordering above is inference from two accounts, not a measured result.

THE OPEN QUESTION WAS SEARCHED FOR AND CAME BACK EMPTY, 2026-08-20. The narrow empirical question is whether re-cutting the first 3 to 5 seconds of an EXISTING shoot revives a FATIGUED winner. Three reading batches went looking for it across 25 transcripts, and nobody has shown it. Do not re-search this corpus for it. The three nearest misses, all recorded here so they are not mistaken for the finding later:
1. Nick Theriot's visual iteration off a 7-day creative-learnings review, which became a top-spending ad. "we actually did the creative learnings on an on an ad, and then we came up with this idea for a visual iteration and basically what we want to change here. And then boom, this basically took like top spending ads." He never states the parent ad was fatigued and never says only the opening seconds changed, so it is not a revival case.
2. Theriot's sale-period move, which is a re-skin rather than a re-cut: "we'll also take our top-performing ads, we'll duplicate them, but we'll put a banner on those creatives as well." Duplicating a live top performer with a holiday banner is a calendar tactic, not a fatigue response.
3. Matt Shiver re-expressing a winning static as a green-screen talk-over: "I just took the static ad and talked about it in a green screen format. So, now I'm going to get in front of a whole new audience of people just by taking the same exact winning ad and literally talking over it." That is NEW footage shot around an old asset, which is the same category as Blue Sense's new-avatar reshoot, not a re-cut of existing footage.
Adjacent and useful even though it does not answer the question: Ben Heath's entry point for a hook transplant is a metric split rather than a fatigue signal. High CTR with low ROAS means "the hook's great. So the initial 3 seconds or so is really good on this one, but the body of the video ad might not be as good. So maybe we could take the the main body from other video ads that convert better, combine it with this hook." That is the transplant run in the opposite direction, a proven hook onto a proven body, and it is also untested. See [[Creative Science#CR-159|CR-159]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-02-28; Andrew Faris, Why Are Early AI Adopters' Businesses Performing So Badly? (With Patrick Coddou), 2026-05-08; Professor Charley T, Meta Ads expert reacts to $1M/month Strategy, 2026-02-14; Blue Sense Digital, Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda, 2026-01-08; Blue Sense Digital, What To Do When 1 Ad Gets All The Spend, 2025-12-03; Nick Theriot, How To Test Facebook Ads in 2026 When You Can't Find a Winner, 2026-08-19; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10; Ben Heath, Copy This Meta Ads Testing Strategy for Better Results, 2026-03-03; Nick Theriot, How Many Ads Should You Be Testing a Week?, 2026-06-26; Nick Theriot, Your Ads Results Are Doomed If You Don't Do This Now!!!, 2026-06-19; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16; Ben Heath, How Good Is Meta's New AI Business Assistant?, 2026-06-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

**WARNING 2026-08-25: the operator we cite for the re-cut revival argues AGAINST re-cuts three months earlier, and this is the same failure mode as the Ben Heath "always a suggestion" case at MD-013.** Four more Fraser Cottrell transcripts were read in full. He never re-asserts the revival, and twice states the opposite as a general rule.
1 February 2026: "No longer can we go into an ad account, just change the hook and expect to see completely different performance. It was a really simple win a couple of years ago and could boost performance. And don't get me wrong, it still does work sometimes, but the majority it's not."
22 February 2026: "Simple hook changes or headline swaps simply don't count as creative testing in Meta's eyes anymore."
His prescription in both files is to expand the winning angle into NEW FORMATS rather than re-cut it: "when you find a winning angle, instead of recreating the ad 10 different times, you want to expand on that angle, go a bit deeper, change the format."
**So the codex has been holding his single undated revival assertion and none of his general position. Record the general position as the stronger of the two, because it is stated twice, in two files, as a rule, while the revival claim was one sentence attributed to a guess and sat beside a failed hook swap on the same body.**
**The one revival case in the 2026-08-25 batch is NOT a re-cut and must not be counted as one.** His Aloha example is an ad that was never edited: it launched with a poor hook rate, was left running unchanged, and later became an evergreen top spender. "at first, it wasn't a very good ad. The hook rate wasn't very high... so they left it on. Now, this is an evergreen winner for them." That is a patience story. No before, no after, no curve, no spend.
**A genuine re-cut mechanism did arrive this pass, from a different operator, and it is a format change rather than a hook change.** Charley T on a fatiguing video winner: "take a few still frames from that video and build a 322 image ad. That flex ad will be shown to the people who saw the video. Now you're retargeting with creative. It capitalizes on the attention the video already built." T3, no numbers, never measured. It is consistent with MD-043, because the media type changes.
**The surviving empirical question is unchanged after three passes and is now the longest-open question in the codex: does re-cutting the first 3 to 5 seconds of an EXISTING shoot revive a fatigued winner? Two operators assert it, one of them contradicts himself, and nobody has shown a curve.**
Also from this pass, a third instance of the 80% folklore at the top of this claim. Nick Theriot: "keep in mind that the hook does 80% of the work here." No source, no test, no retention curve, same as the other two.
Added sources: Fraser Cottrell, Copy This NEW Meta Ads Strategy Post Andromeda, 2026-02-01; Fraser Cottrell, Static Ads vs UGC in 2026, 2026-02-22; Fraser Cottrell, Ive Made 10000 Ads Heres Why Yours Dont Win, 2026-05-03; Charley T, I spent 1 million on Meta ads in 30 days, 2026-02-24; Nick Theriot, The Psychology of Facebook Ads that Scale, 2026-06-29
Last touched: 2026-08-25

### CR-145 · Naming the live platform trend inside the hook lowers CPM and cost per lead, and the angle is perishable
Tier: T3 · Status: active
He runs the trend of the moment as one angle slot inside a standing batch, same 60-second script structure across five pain points or angles, one of which is the current trend (his examples: "yapping videos" and Claude/AI). His ads with "Claude" in the hook are outperforming the rest of the account on CPM and cost per lead. Claimed mechanism is the same as organic trend-riding: familiarity buys cheaper attention, and it shows up in the CPM column first. No dashboard shown, single account. Caveat surfaced in the same conversation and not by him: he and his co-host both describe audience saturation and active "not interested" tagging on AI content, so the window is short. Treat the trend slot as a rotating angle, not a permanent one.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-157 · Manufacture the curiosity gap twice in the first frame: put the subject somewhere the activity makes no sense, and withhold the hook's payoff to the caption
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Two devices from the same breakout ad, both aimed at the same thing, giving the viewer a question they cannot answer without watching. Visual device: place the subject in a setting where the activity is illogical. His build showed a woman using the product in an AIRPORT, "it made no sense of why she would be doing that in the airport, but it created a lot of curiosity to get people in". The frame is the question. Copy device: write the text hook so the payoff sits in the caption rather than on screen, his template being "why I'll never use X solution to X activity in X place again, and then details in caption". His claimed mechanism for the copy half is mechanical rather than persuasive: on a five to ten second video the reader looks away to the caption, the video loops, and play rate rises, which he says the system reads as a positive signal. That last step is asserted with no play-rate figures and should be treated as T4 inside this claim.
The ad carrying both is the 2026-08-19 breakout that took the campaign from a $98 cost per purchase to $51 on its first day live, with the account on screen, but it changed four things at once (new avatar, new creative style, curiosity text hook, unusual location) so neither device is isolated. Both are cheap enough to test on their own. Related but distinct from what is already here: [[Creative Science#CR-013|CR-013]] names curiosity as a required hook property without saying how to build it, [[Creative Science#CR-053|CR-053]] is a twist in the MIDDLE of an ad rather than the first frame, and [[Creative Science#CR-057|CR-057]] is category-level differentiation rather than a shot instruction. The incongruent-setting device also travels well into categories where the product is boring to film, since the setting carries the interest instead of the product.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Test Facebook Ads in 2026 When You Can't Find a Winner, 2026-08-19
Last touched: 2026-08-19

## Creative volume and fatigue

### CR-024 · How many net-new creatives does a spend level actually require?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Fraser Cottrell's brackets: $10-20k/month needs only 3-4 net-new creatives monthly (ads fatigue slower at low spend; overloading starves every ad). $20-50k: 5-6/month, adding TOF educational formats. $50-70k: the pivotal bracket where a winner lasts only 4-6 weeks, requiring 10-15 net-new concepts monthly plus variations. $70-100k: creative is the main scaling lever (bidding and budget mechanics matter far less), ~20 concepts monthly, offer testing starts. $100-250k: ~30/month floor, and poor quality now triggers a delivery penalty spiral (bad ads get served to fewer people, CPA rises). $250-500k+: hard floor of high-40s to 50+ net-new monthly with fully systematized creative ops.
Blue Sense Digital's rule runs roughly 5-10x higher at every tier: one new ad per $1,000 of monthly spend in AUD, so $30k/month means 30 new ads, $100k means 100, and $250-500k means 250-500. His derivation back-propagates from revenue: required creatives = new-customer revenue target divided by (mean lifetime spend per ad x average 7-day-click ROAS). Worked example: 500 ads over a year against $500k spend gives $1,000 mean spend per ad; at 3x ROAS the expected value per ad is $3,000; a $300k monthly new-customer revenue target therefore needs 100 creatives per month. He names two known gaps in his own model. It ignores time-to-realise, since an ad may take four months to spend its $1,000. And it averages winners and losers together, so a real forecast has to model both separately. Volume should flex month to month with the revenue forecast rather than sitting at a fixed number.
An earlier formulation from the same operator constrains volume from the opposite side: creative tests per month = (Meta budget x testing allocation %) / (3 x AOV), where 3x AOV is the minimum spend a single creative needs before a verdict. That caps how many creatives a given budget can honestly evaluate, and it cannot be reconciled with the 1-ad-per-$1,000 rule at low AOV without raising the testing allocation.
Andrew Faris's agency sits between them at 200 to 400 pieces of creative per client per month and says it is pushing that number up, with the condition that the brand supply a clear identity and message because undirected quantity is the failure mode. His floor version for a sub-$1M brand that cannot fund a $40k/month creative agency: roughly 20 new ads a week, product seeded to about 10 creators a month, two new product releases in the year, and four to eight good marketing-calendar moments, starting from whatever the founder is personally good at. He also names a structural reason the number must rise over an account's life: every winning ad becomes the benchmark, so beating it is by definition harder than clearing the previous bar, and a creative program sized for last year underfeeds this year.
Two live-market numbers worth carrying. Blue Sense's own portfolio in December 2025: 8 and 9 figure clients up 58% year on year while 7 figure clients grew far less, dragging the average to 40%, and his stated hypothesis for the gap is that the large accounts received 300 to 400 ads a month through October and November. He flags it as reasoning, not measured attribution. Second, the arbitrage is still open: talking to 10 or 20 brands in market, "the majority have never thought about launching more than 10 ads a month", including large spenders, with brands at $30,000/month on Meta unaware the landscape had changed.
Nick Theriot's ladder is the fourth set and it indexes on REVENUE rather than on spend, which nobody else does: 5 to 6 new ads a week at $0-30k/month revenue on $50-100/day of spend, 10 to 20 new ads a week at $30-60k, 20 to 40 a week at $60-200k. His agency-delivery version indexes on retainer instead: 5 concepts at 3 creatives each, so 15 creatives a week, to a $10k/month client, and four times that at $40k/month, so 80 concepts and about 240 creatives a month. Both are LAUNCH rates, per client, and neither is a live count ([[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]). His stated abandon condition on the whole exercise: once an account is already launching high volume and cost per link click will not move, "the likelihood of you just going to test another ad and bringing out cost per link click is probably going to be low", and the higher-probability lever moves to AOV (upsells, added products) or conversion rate (listicle, advertorial, quiz funnel). That is the only diminishing-returns condition anyone in this claim attaches to their own number.
The Blue Sense divide-by-3x-AOV formula, dated 2025-02-26 with the missing coefficient filled in: "take your advertising spend times by 70%, call that your testing budget to new assets, and then divide it by your average order value times three", output in tests per month. He never runs an account through it, and his own worked example does not obviously agree with it: at $2,000 monthly spend he calls five tests a month "probably makes sense" and puts the physical ceiling at "20 to 30 things", while the formula returns roughly 9 tests at $50 AOV and roughly 23 at $20 AOV. Treat the formula as a heuristic ceiling, not a target. His blanket read from 1,000-plus audits is that 99% of accounts are not testing enough, and his named example of far-too-few is five tests a month at $100,000 of spend.
Two more ladders, both LAUNCH rates, added 2026-08-21. Sam Piliero's "Rule of 10,000": one new ad per week for every $10,000 of MONTHLY spend, floor of two ads per week. Note the unit mismatch built into it, ads per WEEK indexed to spend per MONTH, and note his floor conflicts with his own formula between $10k and $20k a month, where the formula returns one or two and the floor returns two. Eleven items later in the same video he also says "If you think you can get away with just quantity of creative and launching a boatload of ads, your ads are going to get lost into the void", which sits against prescribing any fixed cadence at all. Matt Shiver's is flatter and carries a stated ceiling on its own logic: 5 to 10 new ads per week built from 3 to 5 unique concepts plus variations, and under $100k a month better ads beat more ads. Both asserted, no account data on either.
Neither side showed a controlled comparison. The positions do not reconcile on the number: Fraser's $250-500k floor of 50+ is five to ten times below Blue Sense's 250-500 for the same spend. What both agree on is that volume must be derived from spend rather than fixed by habit.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, Creative Volume | How Many Ads You Actually Need at Each Spend Level, 2026-08-02; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure Your Meta Ads for Profit (Free Live Webinar), 2025-02-19; Blue Sense Digital, Google Ads in 2026 (w/ Austin from Grow My Ads), 2025-12-15; Andrew Faris, The One Part Of Your Business Where You Should Be Wasting More Money, 2026-06-15; Andrew Faris, The 4 Cylinder Marketing Engine Powering My $10M+ Ecom Clients' Success, 2026-05-04; Nick Theriot, Every Level of Scaling an ECOM Brand ($0, $30k, $100k, $1m, $10m), 2026-06-10; Nick Theriot, How Many Ads Should You Be Testing a Week?, 2026-06-26; Nick Theriot, Best Meta Ads Columns Setup for Ecommerce (2026), 2026-07-03; Blue Sense Digital, 5 Step Checklist to Increase Meta Ad Performance Overnight, 2025-02-26; Sam Piliero, 21 Facebook Ad Tricks to Improve Your ROAS INSTANTLY, 2026-05-15; Dr. Matt Shiver, If I Wanted to Make My First 100k mo in 2026 Id Do This, 2026-06-02
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-025 · Run creative as a fixed-cadence production pipeline, not ad-hoc creation
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Ben Heath: at high spend, lock ~3 proven content types and produce ~12 new creatives/week to prevent everything fatiguing at once (ROAS at a quarter of two weeks prior). Nick Theriot: 5 new ad ideas per week x 3 creatives = 15 creatives per client per week, on a continuous 2-week loop where the next ~15 concepts are being built while the current batch tests. The pipeline, not any individual ad, is the fatigue defense.
Jeremy Haynes adds a shelf: keep filmed but unlaunched ads parked so a duplicated ad set can be topped up the same day. His justification is production latency, since even a fast team needs 24 to 48 hours to film and edit, and that is 24 to 48 hours of the new ad set running under-loaded. Charley T's open objection is that Haynes never specifies what the shelf ads should be ABOUT, so an unbriefed shelf is a stack of lottery tickets rather than a set of directed hypotheses. Bank the shelf, brief it against named angles.
The pipeline stated as a schedule, with the lag named, which is the part that determines when a cadence actually starts producing: a 4-week rolling cycle at a 3-week brief-to-live lag. "week one, um CS writes the ad... And then week two, our video production team makes the ad. And then week three, our media buyer launches the ad... And then week four, our CS basically goes ahead and does the um learnings on the ad." After the first cycle new ads go live every week, because the strategist writes every week. Format sets the lead time inside that: UGC needing a creator runs 2 to 3 weeks, AI-produced ads run 48 to 72 hours. Plan the cadence backwards from the slowest format in the mix, not the average.
Sources: Ben Heath, $100 vs $100,000 Facebook Ads Strategy, 2026-08-12; Nick Theriot, My Full Process For Scaling Facebook Ad Clients In 2026, 2026-07-20; NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05; Professor Charley T, Meta Ads expert reacts to $1M/month Strategy, 2026-02-14; Nick Theriot, How Many Ads Should You Be Testing a Week?, 2026-06-26
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-026 · Raw net-new volume vs multiplying proven winners
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Sam Piliero (80+ brands, $30M+/month): raw creative volume does not matter; his agency stopped chasing volume and instead multiplies quality, using AI to produce ~20 iterations per run from the top five highest-spending image ads plus competitor inspiration, which yields hit rate AND volume simultaneously. Fraser Cottrell and Ben Heath hold the opposing emphasis: high spend requires large net-new concept counts on a fixed cadence (Fraser: ~30/month at $100-250k, 50+ at $250-500k; Ben: ~12/week at $100k/month), because at scale everything fatigues fast regardless of quality. The positions partly reconcile (iterations are still launches), but the prescriptions differ on whether net-new concept count is the KPI.
Blue Sense Digital offers a third position that reconciles them through a quality floor. His leverage hierarchy, stated identically in two videos, is volume first, then concept quality, hook strength, editing quality, UGC talent, and storytelling last. The argument: hand ten senior creative strategists five objectively good ads and ask which one won, and none can beat guessing, they will only rationalize. Since prediction above the bar is impossible, define the bar (his shorthand is a 7/10 ad), then maximise volume above it. He rejects the naive version explicitly: he observed two accounts launching 2,000+ ads a month while barely reaching $4M/month spend and staying unprofitable, because that volume cleared no concept, hook or format standard. Operator action under this position is to invest in defining and teaching what the bar looks like, then remove throughput constraints. Asserted, no controlled test.
Professor Charley T holds the extreme opposite end and it is the most decision-relevant version of the disagreement, because he is not arguing for less volume, he is arguing volume is actively harmful. An account has a finite creative absorption capacity and exceeding it degrades performance; the working alternative is replacing only the worst-performing creative when replacement is needed, and he reports rarely needing to. Asked how many new ads he launches per month he answers "on average, it would be less than one", says he has not added a creative to his existing campaign in months, and spends about ten minutes twice a week in the account, down from 5 to 10 hours a week under his previous approach. Scope that honestly: it is his own lead-gen account peaking around $40k/month, self-reported, no dashboard. His rule is conditional rather than absolute: "if you've solved the problem of being able to spend more money, the worst thing you could do is launch new ads", and asked directly by a viewer whose first winner had absorbed all the Black Friday spend whether that meant testing more, he said no, flatly. He reconciles his position with Meta's public volume guidance by scoping it: most advertisers have launched four or five ads ever, so more volume is correct advice for them, and per a Meta employee he knows the guidance covers organic content, not paid ads alone.
Andrew Faris takes the volume side without qualification and names volume as the variable he will not trade: "we know that most of the ads that we put into the ad account are not going to work, but we build a system to create a large volume of ads, then generally keep trying to tweak it over time to get the best performing ones to work, but we're never going to sacrifice the volume." His target phrase is a high volume of high quality, highly diverse ad creative, which is Blue Sense's quality-floor position stated from the other direction. He also supplies the boundary condition nobody else offers: one brand ran the aggressive content investment for a year and it did not work, appeared to hit a local maximum, and pulling back was the right call. The strategy assumes growth is limited by content rather than by product or addressable market, which links this to [[Creative Science#CR-080|CR-080]] and [[Creative Science#CR-007|CR-007]].
Charley T's sharpest statement of his position, 2026-02-07, and it names replication specifically rather than volume generally: "Why are we adding new winners every week? This is something I never understood... If you can scale the budget, why would you add a new creative? ... Plus, these new ads are going to come at the expense of the old one. So, you're manufacturing ad fatigue when you already solve the problem." The mechanism he attaches is cannibalisation of delivery rather than entity collapse: "as soon as something works, you're building something else that basically wins for the same reason, but they're not reaching different people... You're robbing Peter to pay Paul. You're not getting any more money like this." That is adjacent evidence for MD-003, not a direct answer to it. It is also conditional in the same way his earlier statement was, the precondition being that budget can still be raised on the incumbent.
Nick Theriot supplies the standard that the volume camp needs and rarely states, and it does not require a count: an ad should only be launched if it was BUILT TO BEAT the current top spender. "the whole reason we're launching and testing more ads is to beat our top spending ad. So like if you spent this much effort and attention to build your top performing ad, and now you're trying to beat it with ads that you spent this much effort and attention, it makes no sense why you're even launching that right there." He reports operators testing 50 to 200 ads a week with no hypothesis and no post-launch review, and diagnoses the creative rather than the account. That is Blue Sense's quality-floor position with a concrete pass condition attached, and it is checkable against a written hypothesis per ad ([[Creative Science#CR-041|CR-041]]).
The strongest available case that raw volume is the wrong axis, and it is a real account rather than reasoning: Nick Theriot reports inheriting an account testing hundreds of ads per week against one angle with nothing working, and its first winner in six months came from a single new ad carrying a different angle, positioning and avatar. No numbers, dates or spend given, so T3. It does not settle the volume debate, because the winning move was still a launch, but it does bound it: unlimited variants inside one argument produced nothing for half a year. Sam Piliero states the same constraint from the structure side, "We absolutely do need more ads, but we only need those ads if they are positioned properly", which makes structure the prerequisite and volume the multiplier rather than the two being alternatives. Meta's own position, from the executive who owns retrieval, is that a reasonable diverse candidate set is enough and an extremely large number adds nothing over reasonable diversity, with no threshold named (see [[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]). Three voices now say the binding variable is difference, not count, and none of them ran the comparison.
Sources: Sam Piliero, Do THIS and the Meta Andromeda Algorithm Will LOVE You!, 2026-08-14; Fraser Cottrell, Creative Volume, 2026-08-02; Ben Heath, $100 vs $100,000 Facebook Ads Strategy, 2026-08-12; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-02-28; Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Andrew Faris, The 4 Cylinder Marketing Engine Powering My $10M+ Ecom Clients' Success, 2026-05-04; Andrew Faris, The One Part Of Your Business Where You Should Be Wasting More Money, 2026-06-15; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10; Sam Piliero, The BEST Facebook Ads Campaign Structure for 2026, 2026-07-05; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07; Nick Theriot, How Many Ads Should You Be Testing a Week?, 2026-06-26
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-027 · Does audience exhaustion actually happen at normal spend?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Ben Heath: on accounts spending over $100k/month, ads fatigue within days; the driver is market dynamics (small target audience, high spend), not creative quality, so "better ads will last longer" is explicitly rejected. Every high-budget account runs into fatigue and the fix is a constant refresh pipeline, not better individual ads.
Professor Charley T denies the population-exhaustion version outright across two videos. First: "I used to spend over a million dollar a day for single accounts. And even at that level, it took a week to reach 85% of Americans just two and a half times. Back then, CPMs were 10 times cheaper than they are today. You would need to spend $10 million a day to truly burn through your audience." Second: exhausting a one-million-person audience with a single ad would take roughly $500,000 per week, every week, though he hedges the figure himself ("I think you would need something like") and shows no working. His conclusion is that what operators call audience fatigue is misallocated effort, too much spend at the bottom of the funnel and not enough at the top, and that interfering slows the system's discovery of new people. His operating advice follows: stop diagnosing "fatigue" or "saturation" and look for operator error first (displacement, killed top-of-funnel ads, seasonality).
Neither side showed data. The two are partly reconcilable because they measure different things: Charley argues about the total addressable population, while Ben and [[Creative Science#CR-028|CR-028]] describe saturation of a targeted embedding cluster, which is a far smaller pool than "85% of Americans".
