The unresolved part is whether a real account at $100k/month is spending into a cluster or into a population.
Charley's positive mechanism, stated twice in February and April 2026 and worth testing because it is falsifiable: fatigue is manufactured by the operator, because every new ad is validated first against the users the system already understands, which are the warmest and also the cheapest conversions currently feeding the incumbent winners. Each launch therefore strips low-hanging fruit from the ads carrying the spend, and the resulting cost rise reads as fatigue on the dashboard. "99% of ad fatigue that isn't built around just you raised the budget too much too fast is because you launch a new ad." The 99% is rhetorical. The prediction that makes it testable: an account that stops launching and stops duplicating ad sets should stop showing fatigue.
Restated 2026-02-07 in the form that makes it checkable, and it is a delivery-cannibalisation claim rather than an entity-collapse one: "as soon as something works, you're building something else that basically wins for the same reason, but they're not reaching different people. Just this thing wins by taking this thing's lunch." Under that reading the new winner does not expand the pool, it moves spend, so the incumbent's decline is manufactured by the launch. Same falsifiable prediction as above, and still nobody has run it.
Andrew Faris supplies the third position and it is the one that fits both camps. Meta has got materially better at putting the right ad in front of the right person faster, so an ad reaches its best audience sooner AND exhausts it sooner. Faster burnout is a feature of better delivery, not an error and not a ghost in the machine. His observed spend shape: an ad going $5,000/day, to $15,000/day a day or two later, to $50,000/day, then back to $5,000, within days. In apparel he saw ads rip for three or four weeks then fade with the demand cycle. No account, date range or CPA attached to the curve. If Faris is right, Charley's operator-error diagnosis and Ben's market-dynamics diagnosis are both describing the same acceleration from different ends, and the actionable consequence is Ben's, not Charley's: volume becomes mandatory rather than optional.
Sources: Ben Heath, $100 vs $100,000 Facebook Ads Strategy, 2026-08-12; Professor Charley T, The NEW BEST Meta Ads Andromeda Course to Scale in 2026, 2026-01-24; Professor Charley T, Meta advertisers: We've got a big problem, 2026-01-17; Professor Charley T, Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies, 2026-04-15; Professor Charley T, Meta Ads expert reacts to $1M/month Strategy, 2026-02-14; Andrew Faris, I've Managed Over 100 Meta Ads Accounts. Here's Why Yours Is Broken., 2026-05-27; Andrew Faris, The 3-Part Creative Diversity Framework I Use To Scale Past $10M On Meta Ads, 2026-05-01; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07

### CR-028 · Creative fatigue is embedding-cluster saturation: a hyper-specific ad exhausts its cluster, then frequency climbs or delivery bridges to worse-fit users
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A persona callout like "business owners doing $10M+" finds and saturates its cluster quickly; after roughly $10,000 of spend (illustrative) there is no one new. Meta then either re-serves the same pool (frequency up) or expands to adjacent clusters that are no longer the ICP (ROAS down). This is the mechanistic reason specific ads fatigue faster than broad-relevance ads.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How The Meta Ads Algorithm Works in 2026, 2026-07-28
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-029 · Does injecting iterations around the fatigue point extend a winner's life, or accelerate its death?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Sam Piliero's default pattern: an ad spends, fatigues, tails off, dies. Instead, once an ad proves out (high ROAS plus high spend), inject iterations of that concept as fatigue would start: proven hook x new bodies, proven body/offer/CTA x new hooks. Performance sustains and the decline takes significantly longer; the money is made in the gap between the sustained line and the fatigue line. Judge winners after 7-14 days of data.
Professor Charley T holds the opposite. He does not push winners to the point where performance drops, and when it does drop he says replacing the ad guarantees a faster decline. His read is that a drop means the platform has changed how it uses that ad in the journey, diagnosable through his 4PI analysis, and the right response is to exploit the opportunity that shift created rather than to feed the slot. His named alternatives sit outside Meta entirely: spike the Google search budget, or get aggressive on email, where he claims 99% of brands are not aggressive enough. Reasoning, no data shown, and it rests on the same mechanism as [[Creative Science#CR-027|CR-027]], that new launches cannibalise the incumbent's cheapest conversions.
Neither ran the comparison. The narrow disagreement is whether an iteration of the winner counts as a new ad that steals its cheap conversions or as an extension of the same asset reaching new people. That is directly testable by injecting iterations on one winner and holding a matched winner untouched, then reading account-level cost per acquisition rather than ad-level.
Sources: Sam Piliero, Easiest Way to Scale Facebook Ads in 2026, 2026-08-03; The Only Facebook Ads Video You Need in 2026, 2026-07-26; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-030 · The sweet spot is 4-8 ads per concept pack, new packs every 1-3 weeks, winners judged after 7-14 days
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
More than 8 ads per pack is too many, fewer than 4 is not enough swings. Packs ship on a consistent weekly-to-triweekly drumbeat so a new pack is always in its test window. The flywheel analyzes launches after 7-14 days using incremental attribution, pulls top-spending KPI-hitting ads as winners, iterates them, and mines competitors' repeatedly-run templates for inspiration.
Professor Charley T rejects the fixed judging window outright and names it: asked whether he turns ads off on a 7-day interval, he says no interval exists, the decision is a feel for the account's pulse and depends on what other moves are running at the same time, and "anybody that tells you it's seven days is full of it." He offers no replacement rule, which makes his position unusable as an operating instruction even if it is right. The defensible middle is that a fixed window is a convention for making decisions at all, not a claim about when signal arrives, and it should stretch when conversion volume is thin. Neither side showed how verdicts made at 7 days compare with verdicts made at 21.
Sources: Sam Piliero, The Only Facebook Ads Video You Need in 2026, 2026-07-26; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-031 · Animating a winning static counts as a genuinely different entity post-Andromeda; the format is resurging and AI collapses production to 5-10 minutes
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Because video and image are different media types, Meta is very unlikely to tag the animated version as the same entity ID as the source static, unlike headline-swap variations which now get flagged. Animated statics produced many winners early, died, and are now resurging, so adopt before re-saturation. Via the Arcads MCP in Claude (Kling 3.0 Pro, start frame locked to the exact static), a ~2-hour designer-plus-animator job collapsed to about 5-10 minutes; he chose 9:16 as safest for text accuracy. He hedges the entity-ID claim himself and the demo was sponsored.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, Claude + Arcads MCP | Fastest Way to Make Iterations Meta Ad Creatives, 2026-08-09
