### CR-142 · Each ad element biases where Meta places the ad in the customer journey, so a flexible ad can be assembled to correct a diagnosed funnel-position problem
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Premise: the primary text, the headline and the creative each independently pull the ad toward upper, mid or lower funnel, so a 322 can be built surgically against a diagnosis instead of assembled at random. Worked example: a creative eating budget at low frequency, low CPM and a terrible conversion rate is diagnosed as too upper-funnel, and the fix is attaching two headlines that sit lower in the funnel to drag the assembled ad down. The element-level test procedure he calls "Golden Bear" is paywalled inside his own program, so the mechanism is described but neither the procedure nor any validating data was shown. Bank as a hypothesis: element-level funnel position is testable by holding two of the three element types constant.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The BEST AD ON META after Andromeda, 2026-01-10
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-143 · One 30-second video yields six assets and therefore two flexible ads, with the statics claimed to serve to people who already watched the video
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The asset arithmetic answers low creative volume on small budgets: one 30-second shoot gives 30s, 20s and 15s cuts plus three stills pulled from the footage, which fills two 322 flexible ads. The load-bearing and unverified half is the sequencing claim: "because the videos are attracting the attention and then the images are being shown to the people that watch the videos because that's how Andromeda shows customer journeys, you now have a full funnel inside of an adset targeting just the most qualified individuals." Nothing was shown to support it. It is directly testable by checking impression overlap between the video and static permutations. Consistent with the sequencing logic in SC-021, applied inside one ad set rather than across five concepts.
Ben Heath states the same sequencing rule as a standing production instruction, independent of the 322 arithmetic: "I always recommend make image variations of your best-performing videos, because images will follow people around, so they see the video, it's in their brain, and then you have statics." His economic argument is that images are far cheaper to produce in volume and carry the same idea across more placements once the video has planted it. Charley T endorses it with the offline analogue, Flo from Progressive appearing on both TV and billboards. Two operators, same reasoning, still no impression-overlap data from either, so the mechanism remains the untested half. The production rule is cheap enough to run regardless: every video winner should generate matching statics.
Sources: Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Low Budget (joint session with Ben Heath), 2026-02-28; Professor Charley T, Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies, 2026-04-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-151 · Put the same creative set into every ad set so ads can be compared across audiences; a creative that only wins in one pocket is fragile
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Applied to a catalog/DPA account running four or five ad sets, each with different ads against different audiences, which made cross-audience comparison impossible. After consolidating the same creatives into every ad set, the best ad hit everywhere and carried 50% higher AOV and 2x the gross profit per transaction of any other ad. The generalisable rule: scalability is defined as performing across audiences, so a creative test that runs one ad in one pocket cannot answer the scaling question at all. Numbers asserted in this video; the account was shown in a different one. Complements SC-014 (hyper-segmentation fragments data) from the measurement side.
Told a week earlier with the causal detail and one more number. The account came from a cost-cap agency with three or four different ads in every ad set; his first move was to put the same ads into every audience, after which specific ads carried a higher AOV or a lower CPA consistently across every audience they appeared in, which he calls a direct, immediate and transferable effect. One catalog ad delivered 2x the gross profit per transaction of any other catalog ad in the account. Its only distinguishing feature: the copy told the story of the brand while the catalog tiles carried nothing but product name and price, where the competing ads padded the tiles with descriptions. So on catalog and DPA, the copy wrapper is the variable, and creative testing belongs at the ad level inside one ad set rather than across duplicated ad sets. Real spend, no dashboard shown.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The NEW SIMPLE EASY WAY to Scale BIG with Meta Ads, 2026-02-07; Professor Charley T, Copy this Meta Ad strategy to BLOW UP your business. (After Andromeda, Clothing Niche), 2026-01-31
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-153 · A separate testing campaign measures the wrong thing, because a winner on a protected $50/day budget has never had to compete for spend
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Three objections, stated across three videos by one operator. First, a creative that wins on low data at $50/day tells you nothing about its behaviour at $100 or $1,000. Second, the testing campaign competes in-auction against your main campaign, so the test result carries extra competition at a lower budget and is not comparable to the destination. Third, reading the result requires trusting attribution. The compounding problem is that the winner then has to be retested in the destination campaign anyway, which makes the first test wasted motion, and in the meantime a false positive from a protected budget is used to justify replacing ads that are winning under real competitive conditions.
The framing he attaches goes further than the structure: "no ad set is testing or scaling, all ads are always testing." Under that model the testing-versus-scaling split exists only in the media buyer's head, and converting an ad set into a "scaling ad set" signals the whole model is wrong. Everything runs in one campaign.
His positive replacement, stated 2026-02-07, and it is the constructive half this claim previously lacked: run the challenger inside the SAME CBO as the control ad set, and read whether the challenger takes budget away from the control rather than whether it hits a target in isolation. "I'm doing this style testing with one flex ad in one adset and one 32 in the other ad set specifically to do an AB test around do these concept get spend from my winner from my control ad set and if they do which one does a better job and out of my control ads set that they're competing against because it's a CBO which ad sees less spend." The win condition is displacement, which also tells you which incumbent to retire. It carries the cost he does not mention: a challenger that displaces the control cannibalises it, which is exactly the mechanism he blames for manufactured fatigue in [[Creative Science#CR-027|CR-027]]. Reasoning and one described setup, no result shown.
Reasoning throughout, no data shown, and note his position is not neutral, since the same operator holds the lowest launch-volume position in this file ([[Creative Science#CR-026|CR-026]]). The falsifiable part is narrow and worth testing: whether a creative's rank order at $50/day predicts its rank order at $500/day in the same account.
Sources: Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13; Professor Charley T, How to Scale Profits with Meta Ads, 2026-01-24; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-154 · How many ads should be live and eligible for spend at once?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
The single widest live disagreement in this file, and one operator contradicts himself across four dates inside it, which should lower confidence in his number specifically. Professor Charley T: 4 to 6 eligible for spend on any given day, 5 to 8 maximum (2025-12-06); "it doesn't matter your AOV, doesn't matter your budget, I wouldn't have more than three or four max" (2026-01-24); three to five new ads at a time unless daily budget is above $200 (2026-02-21); five to eight 322 ads, which is 24 creative permutations, maxes out a campaign unless you spend thousands a day (2026-04-15). The stated range across his own corpus is therefore 3 to 8, and the unit silently shifts from discrete ads to 322 flex units, which multiplies the underlying creative count by about eight. His threshold for an over-launched account is 15 live ads, with five or six as the target state to consolidate to.
Ben Heath, recorded in the same April video: "we now aim for 20 different ad creatives per ad set, we used to be limited to six... post-Andromeda things have really changed", built as six 322 ads. Jeremy Haynes runs 25 to 30 separately filmed ads per ad set, the same set in all three ad sets, which is 75 to 90 live ads. Blue Sense's $350k/month case in [[Creative Science#CR-121|CR-121]] went from about 15 live ads to about 60 and saw CAC fall 20-30%, which is a live account moving in the opposite direction from Charley's threshold.
Charley's two supporting arguments are the reusable part even if his number is not. Training-budget arithmetic: at $100/day across five ads each ad gets about $20 of training in a perfectly even split, at 100 ads each gets about $1, and in reality one ad takes roughly 80% and the rest get nothing. Sequence arithmetic: because conversion depends on the ORDER ads are seen, the search space is permutations rather than a per-ad ranking, and he puts five ads at 125 orderings and a dozen in the hundreds of millions, adding that at one variation per second you would need 78 billion years to walk the variations of 50 once. Verify the combinatorics before quoting the figures, they are loose. The directional claim, that solve time grows superlinearly with ad count, does not depend on them.
His constructive proposal, which is the version worth testing: replace a 30-ad launch with ten 322 flexible ads, each holding three executions of one concept plus two copy and two headline options. That collapses the permutation space to ten readable cells, separates concept from execution so a strong idea killed by a weak headline is not written off, and holding the same copy set across every cell permits a multivariate read of whether the same copy wins everywhere. He concedes even this is more complex than he would like. Nobody in this dispute ran a controlled comparison of live-ad counts at matched spend.

THE FINDING THAT REPLACES THE RECONCILIATION ATTEMPT, 2026-08-19. The practitioners are counting different units, so the numbers were never comparable and no amount of reading will reconcile them. Blue Sense holds the strictest definition on the roster: "I would not consider a different ad being a change in the color a change in the headline a change in the primary text a change in the landing page even landing page tests are not different ads it is the same ad." An ad counts as new only if it varies enough to be a statistically relevant test, and 2 bodies crossed with 5 hooks is 10 ads inside ONE concept, where a concept is defined by offer, angle and audience. Under that definition much of Haynes's 75 to 90 and Heath's 20 are hook and copy variants Blue Sense would call one ad. Set the units side by side: Charley T's number slides between discrete ads and 322 flex units, which multiplies the underlying creative count by about eight; Heath's 20 is per ad set; Haynes's 25 to 30 is per ad set replicated across three ad sets, so 75 to 90 at account level; Piliero's 165 and 62 are per ACCOUNT; and Blue Sense's own only figure is not a live count at all, "they need to launch 11 more ads per month to grow from 130k a month and net sales to 160k in net sales", a monthly LAUNCH rate that is the output of his revenue model and never a statement of how many sit live. Stop comparing the raw counts. Before quoting any number in this claim, state the unit: per ad set or per account, discrete ads or flex units, live concurrently or launched per month, and whose definition of "an ad". Blue Sense's methodological warning is the reason it matters commercially, not just semantically: a volume model built on one definition of an ad does not transfer when you switch to another, "the numbers aren't going to equate and you won't see the results that you're actually after."
Nick Theriot's operating point, stated 2026-07-10, with the fragmentation objection answered. One campaign holds 150 to 180 ad sets with only 10 to 20 live, each ad set carrying 3 creatives, so roughly 30 to 60 live ads against ONE audience. He denies audience fragmentation applies because every ad set runs the identical audience and differs only by creative. The 3 is a production byproduct, not a delivery requirement: one video plus two visual hook variants, and "if I go over four, if I go to 20, it's not going to break anything." He also refuses any per-week test count and reports winners at both extremes, one account testing 50 videos a day on a $20/day budget and another launching one ad a week, both scaling. Real accounts across a large book, no test.
The hardest number now on the table points DOWN, and its causation is confounded. Sam Piliero, 2026-07-05, reading Ads Manager live with the date range switched on screen: a fashion account ran 165 live ads the prior month and 62 in the 30-day period where ROAS moved 0.63 to 1.4 on about $14,000 of spend. The two counts are T2, shown. The causal claim is not, because he attributes the lift to campaign restructure and breakdown pruning and states there was no sale, no new products and no new ads, which leaves the ad-count reduction entangled with the restructure and untested on its own. His own framing keeps volume in the model: "We absolutely do need more ads, but we only need those ads if they are positioned properly."
Meta's own position, and it names diversity rather than a count. Matt Steiner, who owns the retrieval and ranking systems, 2026-06-04: "It's good to have a reasonable candidate set of ads creatives for Andromeda to consider across your various audiences. At the same time. Creating an extremely large number of them probably isn't going to help you more than having a reasonable diversity of ads." Diminishing returns on count, difference as the binding variable, no threshold given. Tiered T3 and NOT T1, because a named platform employee asserting on a podcast quotes no document and shows no data.
Two housekeeping notes on the roster. Ben Heath states no live-ad count in either the 2026-03-03 testing video or the 2026-05-19 scaling video, so his 20 per ad set stands unrevised and unconfirmed as of 2026-05-19; his only adjacent instruction is format diversity inside one ad set with no number attached ([[Creative Science#CR-054|CR-054]]). And do NOT read the "2 to 5 ads" in Meta's creative testing tool as Heath revising toward Charley T. That is a Meta UI cap on a split test, raised to 7 by August 2026, and it says nothing about steady-state live ad count ([[Creative Science#CR-113|CR-113]]).

THE UNITS ARE NOW ON RECORD, 2026-08-20, and they dissolve most of this debate. Four operators stated their unit explicitly, and the units count different quantities.
Ben Heath counts LIVE ads PER AD SET. He names the old platform guidance and the change: "they used to have a recommendation of don't have five or six ads live in an adset at any one time... That has now changed with Andromeda. with some other updates... So the recommendation typically is 20 plus and we will often run adsets with 20 plus." He manages to no ceiling at all: "I don't worry about an upper limit anymore. The constraining factor is usually how many good quality ads can be created. What are the resources available?" His 20-plus is a concurrency count and its binding constraint is production capacity, not delivery.
Nick Theriot never counts live ads. Across five transcripts read in full, every number he gives is a PRODUCTION AND LAUNCH RATE. His planning unit is the CONCEPT at 3 creatives each, counted per week per client and priced against retainer: 5 concepts, "which is 15 new creatives", to a $10k/month client, contractually capped at 20 concepts per month; four times that at $40k/month, so 20 concepts per week, 80 per month, about 240 creatives per month; 1 to 2 concepts per week for a solo operator, which he says is fine. His revenue ladder switches the word to "ads" and stays a launch rate: 5 to 6 new ads a week at $0-30k/month revenue, 10 to 20 at $30-60k, 20 to 40 at $60-200k. The two reconcile because his "ads" and his "creatives" are the same object and a concept is three of them. He also refuses the question this claim asks: "Even if I'm on $10 a day of daily budget, I do not have a number for this. I can care less about that. I don't worry about optimization. I don't worry about the algorithm having too many ads."
Blue Sense uses four units and none of them is a live account ad count: spend held by ONE creative (clients "that currently hold all their spend in one ad that's like VSSL style", about a quarter of a million a month); ads per TEST AD SET ("a maximum of three to five ads within an ad set", ABO, 2025-02-26, pre-Andromeda); TESTS per month (five a month is right at $2,000 of spend and far too few at $100,000, with a physical ceiling of 20 to 30 at the low tier); and the spend a winner can hold, which is his working definition of a winner, "finding a creative that can hold $1,000 a day is spent".
Matt Shiver counts ads inside ONE test ad set: one campaign, one ad set, five ads, one offer or one pain point each, all in the same creative style, at roughly $200/day for B2B, with Meta allocating. "I'd probably test five... if it's B2B, let's just say 200 bucks a day. And then whichever of these ads or offers Facebook likes the most and thinks can have the most scale will get the spend."

THE CORE POSITION, and it replaces the reconciliation attempt above. Heath counts LIVE ads per ad set. Theriot and Blue Sense count LAUNCHED ads per week or per month. Those are different quantities, which is why the numbers never reconciled. Most of the debate recorded in this claim was operators answering different questions. Two things survive. First, the vocabulary problem is solved: state the unit before quoting any number, and never set a launch rate against a concurrency count. Second, the genuine disagreement is narrower than it looked and it is only this, how many ads should be LIVE AND ELIGIBLE at once. On that question the roster is Heath at 20-plus per ad set with no ceiling, Haynes at 25 to 30 per ad set, Theriot at roughly 30 to 60 against one audience, Charley T at 3 to 8 with 15 as his over-launched threshold, and Blue Sense's $350k/month case improving from about 15 to about 60 ([[Creative Science#CR-121|CR-121]]). Nobody has run a controlled comparison at matched spend. The rest of this claim is arithmetic about production capacity and belongs in [[Creative Science#CR-024|CR-024]] and [[Creative Science#CR-119|CR-119]].

COPY TESTS DO NOT CONSUME AD SLOTS, which removes another block of the apparent disagreement. Ads Manager takes up to five primary text variations, five headline variations and five description variations inside a SINGLE ad unit, read off the Meta product surface: "you can see we have one of five. So we can have up to five different primary text variations within this one ad. We can do the same for headlines. Click in there, you see one of five. And do the same for descriptions." Heath's rule follows, and it is why his 20-plus is all distinct creative: put copy variation inside the ad unit and reserve separate ads for different creative, because "let's say for each ad creative you needed three or four different ads to test different headline variations, you very quickly get into just silly numbers of ads." This CONFIRMS Blue Sense's standing definition that a colour change, a headline change or a primary-text change is the same ad, and it means any count inflated by copy permutations was never a count of ads. Full field detail at [[Creative Science#CR-130|CR-130]].

Per-ad-set numbers added 2026-08-20. Charley T's guest Jared runs 15 to 30 creatives per ad set inside one prospecting CBO and states the distribution up front: "We know we're not going to get even distribution. It's only going to be two or three that get spend early. That's fine. The rest of them aren't going to get any spend." Charley's counter in the same session sets his floor at five 3-2-2 ads: "you can hit creative diversity with five different 322 ads." His endpoint case is the hardest version of his position and carries no after-data: an account pruned "down from 198" to about nine live ads over a couple of months, two of the nine being 322s in a split test, with "all of these numbers got substantially better" asserted and nothing shown. His stated mechanism for why more ads hurt is T4 and he flags his own bias, that a large candidate set makes the per-user sequencing decision harder, "but like I also heard probably what I wanted to hear."
Blue Sense's definitional standard restated 2025-02-26, from the other direction and with a spend unit attached: "split testing copy variations is not going to double your account... what will double your account is finding a creative that can hold $1,000 a day is spent." He puts the share of accounts for which copy and interest split tests change nothing at 97 to 98 out of 100.
THERIOT'S 3 IS TIGHTER THAN PREVIOUSLY RECORDED, and it changes how his counts should be read. The three ads in each of his ad sets are variants of ONE concept sharing the SAME TEXT HOOK, differing only by visual hook for videos or by background for photos. "the three ads I do for each ad concept is going to be three videos or three photos all with the same text hook or the same text on the the beginning part of it if it's a photo. And only thing I do different is a different visual hook for the videos and a different background for the photos. And that's it." The consequence, stated plainly: anyone reading his ad counts as distinct MESSAGES is off by a factor of three. His 240 creatives a month is 80 messages. Apply the same correction before comparing his numbers to anybody counting concepts.
One number reported in the source batch's reader notes is NOT banked here: that the equal-spend alternative to Shiver's single-ad-set test is five ad sets of one ad at about $500/day, roughly 2.5x the cost for the same read. No verbatim quote for the $500 figure was carried, so it is not a finding in this file.
**2026-08-23. The vocabulary half of this dispute is now essentially closed, and one operator's number turns out not to be a smaller number at all.**
**Charley T's structure, stated plainly (2026-02-17): five ad objects per ad set, each a 3x2x2.** "Five rings all connected... And each ring can be built as a 322. That means three creatives, two primary texts, and two headlines... So while it looks like one ad in the account, inside are multiple combinations." Three of the five rings are prospecting and two are retargeting. **That is about 60 creative combinations live, from five ad objects.** Set against Ben Heath's 20-plus, he is not running fewer creative variants. He is running fewer AD OBJECTS with the variation pushed inside the ad unit. The disagreement is about WHERE THE SPLIT HAPPENS, not about creative volume, which is the same trap this claim already flags when it notes his unit "silently shifts from discrete ads to 322 flex units". His launch rates, both explicitly launched-not-live: 10 ads in 30 days, and "We've launched five ads in the last 6 weeks and two of them are losers."
**Matt Shiver, unit pinned to live-concurrent and the budget corrected.** "I'm going to put $100 a day minimum, typically, for optimizing for these leads and I'll put five to 10 ads inside of there." That is 5 to 10 LIVE-CONCURRENT inside one ABO ad set at a $100/day floor. Corrections to what this codex previously carried for him: the $200/day figure and the "one offer each" framing do not appear, and the anti-pattern line about ten ad sets at $5 each is not in these transcripts and should not be sourced to them. He separately runs 20 to 50 ads in a single CBO ad set for pure volume, and 3 to 5 ad versions per ad set as a WEEKLY LAUNCH unit, so both units genuinely coexist in his vocabulary.
**Blue Sense supplies a live-concurrent number and then disowns precision.** "We generally aim for three to six, sometimes up to 10 ads in an ad set. Um, I wouldn't recommend running one. Probably wouldn't recommend running a 100." His own summary is the honest one and belongs in any answer we give: **"transparently nobody knows what the actual right amount is."**
**Nick Theriot, after SEVEN transcripts, still states no live concurrent count.** Every figure remains a launch rate (1 ad idea per day per client, 5 per week), a composition ratio (3 creatives per idea), or an account topology (campaigns = countries x product categories). The nearest miss is hedged and illustrative: "at any given time we could have 10 ad sets with all problem aware each one." **New and better than the revenue ladder: he states the launch rate is set by HEADCOUNT, not by budget or account size.** "every week we just keep launching five ad sets. That's just cuz what my creative strategists are paid to do." One strategist at one idea per working day yields five a week, which predicts the 10-20 and 20-40 tiers are simply headcount multiples and explains why he refuses to index ad count to daily budget: budget does not employ the strategist. He also never mentions switching ads off between launches in either transcript, so the climbing-live-count finding survives intact.
**Ben Heath gives NO live count in four transcripts read this pass.** The only ad-count figure is "the five new ad creative that we produced at the end of the week", a batching example, launched per week. Do not read it as a concurrency ceiling.

### CR-158 · Funnel position has a readable metric signature, so brief the next ad against a target frequency and CPM instead of against a target CPA
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The signature, stated while grading two ads from the same account. An upper-funnel ad (storytelling, prospecting VSL) earns MORE spend, runs LOWER frequency and LOWER CPM, and looks WORSE on CPA and ROAS. A lower-funnel ad (us-versus-them comparison, social proof) earns LESS spend, runs HIGHER frequency and HIGHER CPM, and looks BETTER on CPA and ROAS. "the us versus them, I would expect that ad to earn less spend, to have a higher frequency, have a higher CPM, and a better CPA or return on ad spend, whatever. It is closing the deal more. This is likely to earn more spend, have a lower frequency and lower CPM and look less efficient." The consequence is that a straight CPA ranking systematically demotes the ads doing the acquiring, which is the same warning [[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]] reaches from the spend side and the same premise as the element-level assembly in [[Creative Science#CR-142|CR-142]].
The briefing move that follows, and it is the part that is actually enforceable: name the target frequency and CPM in the brief. If the account's working ads all come in around 1.3 to 1.4 frequency, commission an ad expected to land at 1.2 with a lower CPM. "Can you reduce your CPM by just enough to ultimately feed those ads that are already working, but with people that might not have been as sold when that ad went live? Can you move one stage up in awareness? You don't need to go all the way to the top of the funnel." One step up the awareness ladder, not a jump to the top, because the job is feeding the converters rather than replacing them. Asserted from account reviews, no before-and-after shown, and the frequency numbers are illustrative of one account rather than a benchmark.
Sources: Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07; Professor Charley T, If You Run Facebook Ads... ROAS Doesn't Matter in 2026, 2026-01-21
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-159 · Read which part of the creative is broken off which metric is broken: CTR splits hook from body, conversion rate splits ad from page
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Three positions and three different fixes, from a live account walkthrough. High CTR with low ROAS means the hook works and the body does not: "the hook's great. So the initial 3 seconds or so is really good on this one, but the body of the video ad might not be as good. So maybe we could take the the main body from other video ads that convert better, combine it with this hook, and that could be a top performer going forward." High CTR with low conversion VOLUME points off the ad entirely, at page friction or a mismatch between the hook and purchase intent; the shown case was CTR above 3% with about 14,000 people reached and 3 purchases. Decent ROAS with low CTR means the ad converts once clicked and is failing to stop the scroll, shown at 1.43 ROAS on a 0.55% CTR, and the fix is a new version of the same static with more vibrant colour or a clearer product focus, not a new offer or a new message.
Two cautions. The readings originate from Meta's AI business assistant and Heath endorses them on camera rather than deriving them, so this is a practitioner endorsing a machine-generated diagnosis, not a measured result; no fix was run and re-measured. And the hook-transplant fix is the same untested move recorded in [[Creative Science#CR-124|CR-124]], run in the opposite direction. What makes the set worth banking is that it is the only place in this file where a metric PAIR, rather than a single metric, is mapped to a specific part of the asset, which is what turns a dashboard into a production instruction.
Sources: Ben Heath, How Good Is Meta's New AI Business Assistant?, 2026-06-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-160 · A small brand may not need to fund top-of-funnel problem awareness, because the category leaders already paid for it
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The argument against building prospecting VSLs for a small DTC brand: the awareness work has already been bought by somebody with a much larger budget, and your first impression usually lands on someone the category has already educated. His data point, from running New Balance's ads: "60% of the first impression where somebody was the first time they ever saw an ad from New Balance. That person in the last seven days had been on Nike, Adidas, Converse, Reebok or Kuma." One category, one advertiser, one period, recalled with no dashboard, so read the 60% as an order of magnitude and not a constant.
What it changes if it holds. In a category with large incumbents advertising heavily, budget spent moving a buyer from unaware to problem-aware is duplicated effort, and the higher-leverage slot is the comparison, the differentiator and the offer, which is the same conclusion the funnel-position signature in [[Creative Science#CR-158|CR-158]] reaches from the metric side. It does NOT generalise to a category with no large advertiser, where nobody has funded the education, which is exactly where [[Creative Science#CR-127|CR-127]] and [[Creative Science#CR-022|CR-022]] apply. The cheap check before committing to a TOF ladder: is anyone bigger than you already running problem-awareness creative in this category, and would your buyer have seen it in the last week.
Sources: Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-171 · The creative threshold ladder, and two operators who set the same hook-rate floor a factor of two apart
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
THE LADDER, one operator, stated as hard floors. Cost per LINK click under $1.50 in the US and under $1.00 worldwide, which he calls "the check engine light of your entire ad account". Link click-through rate at 3% or higher, and below that he reads a creative failure rather than an audience failure. Hook rate floor of 50% of impressions reaching 3 seconds. Hold rate floor of 25% reaching 50% of the video. CPM of $40 to $50 in the US. He concedes the numbers are arbitrary and deliberately hard in the same breath: "you may think that these are aggressive. And reality is they are, but you have to hold yourself ads or ad account to a high standard." Nothing was shown against any of them.
THE FACTOR-OF-TWO DISAGREEMENT, and it is the reason this claim is contested. A second operator puts the hook-rate target at "like 25% above" and reports a client account hitting 60% at one point. So the floors on the same metric are 50% and 25%. That is not a rounding difference, it is the difference between killing an ad and scaling it, and neither operator shows the distribution his number came from. The creative that produced the 60% is worth recording because it is unusually specific: B-roll of the coach on stage doing the exact thing the prospect wants to do, a voiceover on top, and a direct callout. Numbers recalled from the account, nothing on screen.
THIRD CTR NUMBER, and it is a different metric wearing a similar name. The same operator puts UNIQUE link click-through rate above 1%, with 0.5% to 1% acceptable on a direct-offer ad because the ask is harder than a free webinar registration. The column was SHOWN in Ads Manager; the threshold is asserted. Do not stack this against the 3% figure above, because link CTR and unique link CTR count different denominators, and the 3% carries no statement of which one it means. Whenever any of these is quoted, name the exact column.
THE DIAGNOSTIC LADDER THAT MAKES THE THRESHOLDS USEFUL, from the first operator, five scenarios. Zero conversions is an OFFER problem, not a targeting problem, because "Meta's so good at working out who to put your ads in front of". Some conversions with low hook rate and low CTR is a CREATIVE problem. Some conversions with HIGH CTR and high hook rate is a LANDING PAGE or post-click problem, since the ad did its job. Many conversions with no profit is a BUSINESS MODEL problem, pricing or missing upsells. He names the creative case as the one he meets most. This is the part to actually run, because it converts a metric read into a place to work, and it is asserted from account reviews with no data attached.
READ AGAINST THIS FILE'S OWN POSITION BEFORE USING ANY OF IT. [[Creative Science#CR-018|CR-018]] holds that a 21% hook rate sat next to a 2.2% CTR and a strong CPA, so a low hook rate is not a losing ad. [[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]] ranks hook and hold rate as diagnostics only and puts amount spent at the top. A floor of 50% would have killed the CR-018 ad. The reconciliation available is that a threshold is a trigger to INSPECT rather than a trigger to kill, which is how the diagnostic ladder above uses them, and nobody in this file has run the comparison that would settle it.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, how to turn any product into a money printer w branded dropshipping, 2026-02-17; Dr. Matt Shiver, How Francis Scaled His Fitness Business with Instagram Ads, 2026-04-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, Facebook Ads Tutorial for Beginners Updated for 2026, 2026-03-24; Ben Heath, How To Succeed With A NEW Meta Ad Account in 2026, 2026-05-27
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-045 · B-roll + trending music + captions (20-30s, hook = desired state or problem) is the top-spending format across most of his accounts
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A few b-roll clips with no natural sound, a trending Instagram/TikTok song underneath, captions on each clip, about 20-30 seconds, opening with either the desired end state or the current problem. He calls it "probably the top spending ad for the majority of our client accounts right now." A music-only variant lets captions carry the story.

### CR-046 · Pixar/cartoon-animation AI videos are the one AI creative format consistently winning, because viewers know it is animation
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Cartoon-character ads (AI song or storytelling format) are "the one thing of AI that's just consistently dominating on all of these accounts." The mechanism: people clearly know it is animation, so the AI-ness is not read as deception, unlike photoreal AI UGC.
Second operator, same direction, stated harder: realism is no longer the competitive axis for AI creative, and deliberately unrealistic animation was among his top performers last month. "some of the top ads in my account over the last month has been stupid stuff like this." Two discounts on that. No per-ad figures and no ranking were shown, and the animation example he plays on screen is a third party's ad rather than one of his own, so the corroboration is a stated recollection sitting next to somebody else's creative.

### CR-047 · Winning native ads run 2,000-2,500 characters of primary text with a small image through self-created third-party Facebook pages
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Very long primary text (~2,000-2,500 characters) over a standard image, published through third-party Facebook pages the agency creates itself. The third-party page is part of the format working, keeping the ad reading as editorial content rather than a brand ad.
Blue Sense supplies the definitional split that keeps this distinct from creator ads. A partnership ad runs through a real creator's existing handle and is usually paid. Whitelisting runs through a third-party page you create yourself: "rather than Grunes making another page called Grunes, instead they might make a page called fiber health magazine or something like that and then they're running the same ads." Two motivations, not one: credibility (it reads as third party rather than as the brand selling to you) and page-cap relief, since Meta caps live ads per page. His production note: reskin the creative to be more advertorial to suit the third-party framing rather than porting identical brand assets onto the new page.
The same operator names the page as the single tell that breaks a UGC asset. Reviewing the strongest ad in a seven-ad set, the only improvement he could find was the handle it ran through: the script and delivery pass as organic content until the company page reveals the sell. "The only thing that gives this away and makes it feel less authentic is that I know this person is selling me something because it's on a company page and not a personal." The same brand runs a second version through a personal page as a partnership ad, which reads as more authentic, and Hyro runs a version through a page created specifically for whitelisting. Cheapest available test: duplicate an existing UGC winner onto a personal or third-party page with the asset unchanged. Judgement call, no split shown.
The audience where the format has a stated number attached, and the reason given for it. On a 60-plus demographic Theriot replaced AI UGC with native long-form-text ads and reports cost per link click falling from $3 to $5 down to about $0.90, with cost per purchase and CPM also lower. His explanation is generational format familiarity: "they come from the newspaper ages... it's a long form primary text for them to read and it's more like a newspaper ad in that sense." Numbers recalled from memory with no screen, no dates, no spend and no account named, so T3 and the ratio is the useful part rather than the absolute. Consistent with the long-form-copy static for 50+ audiences in [[Creative Science#CR-048|CR-048]]. Worth testing directly in any account whose buyer skews over 55, which includes most chiropractic and commercial-vehicle work.
The division of labour inside the format, stated by a third operator and it inverts the usual reading of a static: the image is the scroll stopper and the primary text does all the selling. "this creative itself doesn't do any selling. What does the actual selling is right here, which is the actual primary text." He adds a corollary that reads backwards against normal art direction, that a native static can work through CONTRAST, so a visually unpleasant image can be the stopping mechanism. His example ad is described as "scaling really well" with no figures attached. Consistent with his own 80% copy weighting in [[Creative Science#CR-012|CR-012]], and it is the same speaker, so treat it as one position stated twice rather than as two.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure A Meta Ads Account At Every Spend Tier In 2026, 2026-06-01; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10; Mark Builds Brands, i tried running only AI ads for 30 days, 2026-04-29
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-048 · Statics still work across the awareness spectrum but produce fewer winners than the b-roll video format
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Statics remain in the portfolio from most-aware offer statics to product-benefit and unaware native-style statics, and Charley T notes statics work well in lower-funnel closing roles. But Nick explicitly finds "a lot more of this one [b-roll video] working versus static ads"; statics are a diversification play rather than the core format in his 2026 accounts.
Blue Sense argues statics are not inherently bottom-of-funnel at all and that the belief is a skill issue: "I know people doing $200,000 a day in revenue on just AI images," plus single static ads carrying $500,000 of lifetime spend. His decision rule is production economics rather than per-ad performance: run cost of production against average contribution margin per ad, by format. His illustrative numbers are an image at ~$50 cost returning ~$300 contribution against a video at ~$500 returning ~$400, so even when videos win per ad, the image ratio wins and the correct call is to over-index on images. Numbers are illustrative, not pulled from a shown ledger. His three top-of-funnel static formats: the fake-organic tweet card (IM-8, run through a doctor's personal page as whitelisting so no brand name attaches), the advertorial "best products of 2025 and the ones to avoid" card driving to a brand-owned review site into a quiz funnel, and long-form-copy statics for 50+ audiences on Facebook feed.
Fourth static format from the same operator, with its downstream requirement attached, which is the part operators skip. The highest click-through-rate statics by a wide margin are infomercial-style images revealing almost nothing, his example being a headline about the number one cause of gut pain with an arrow pointing at a man holding his stomach. Pure information gap. The cost is that the traffic is unprimed and not ready to buy, so a product page wastes it, and the format only works behind a long educational listicle or advertorial that withholds the product until the end and then pushes to a sales page. Judge this format on cost per acquisition through the full funnel, never on CTR, which it will always win. Described as an old-school direct-response method that still works.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; Professor Charley T, Copy This Simple Meta Ads Strategy, 2026-08-10; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-049 · Gadget/demo formats scale because a visible unique mechanism plus impulse price removes the need for awareness education
Tier: T3 · Status: active
When the mechanism is visible (magnetic hover microwave cover, folding grill, wiper blades), showing it in the first 2-4 seconds with a text-overlay hook is enough; the product is understood in 3-4 seconds and the low price makes it an impulse buy. Products without a visible mechanism (supplements) need the full awareness-stage education ladder instead.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale (12 Real Examples), 2026-08-03

### CR-050 · Founder/expert UGC arguing the science wins in large sophisticated markets, beating hired creative agencies
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A brand he scaled to $1M/month gets its best creatives from its operations director, who studies the product research daily, green-screens himself over the data, posts organically, then moves the same videos into the ad account; those consistently beat all external creative agencies' output. Caveat: does not work well in super-niche unsophisticated markets.
Second operator, harder claim, and it is about the FOUNDER rather than an expert employee. On Charley T's channel, a guest agency operator names the founder-led VSL the highest converting format across every new brand they onboard: "you can run anything with a personal narrative from you why you built the company... This is 100% the highest converting format for every new brand that we work with." Charley T agrees. The 100% is rhetorical and no per-brand data is attached, so this is two practitioners agreeing rather than evidence. The same segment gives their full endorsed set of five, which is a compact starting portfolio for a new account: problem-solution statics that agitate then resolve, founder-led VSLs, social proof and review statics, us-versus-them comparison ads, and prospecting story VSLs. Note that the last of those is the one [[Creative Science#CR-160|CR-160]] argues a small brand may not need.
Sources: Nick Theriot, Top Creatives I'm Running For 30K Days In July 2026, 2026-07-29; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07

### CR-054 · Accounts relying on a single creative style stop scaling; deliberate style diversity keeps them scalable
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The unifying theme of his winning-formats list is variety: b-roll+music, UGC, animation, native, statics, whitelisting. Advertisers who run only native ads or only AI UGC "can't scale anymore." The prescription is an ongoing intention to test new trends and styles so the account keeps getting new looks. Ben Heath supplies the two-axis test that makes this auditable: diversify across FORMAT (image, video, carousel) AND across STYLE within a format (founder-led, UGC, creator-partnership, client testimonial, animated, slideshow, meme, ASMR). Ten statics of a similar type is not diversification, and neither is ten UGC videos. He treats UGC (creator with no following) and creator-partnership ads (creator with a following) as genuinely different styles even when they look identical on screen. He also names the mechanism and the obsolete habit: different people respond to different creative and the system now knows which person likes which, so the old "find the single best ad and scale it to the moon" model is the wrong operating mode post-Andromeda; wider format and style coverage reaches more of the audience and delays fatigue.
Andrew Faris adds the layer Ben's two axes leave out, and puts it first: creative diversity has three layers in strict order, MESSAGE, then format, then talent. Messaging diversity is the foundation and the thing to get right before anything else. Format diversity is second, described as putting different outfits on the same message. Talent or account diversity is third, achieved through different creators, ages and genders on screen. He says most teams invert it by starting with format or creator sourcing. Since a format-and-style audit passes an account that runs ten different-looking executions of one argument, run the message audit first.
His mechanism for holding format diversity at scale is a running library of replicable ad FEATURES, explicitly not templates where assets slot into the same arrangement every time. The unit is a feature that can carry many different messages, and the sourcing habit is hunting Instagram for unfamiliar formats then dissecting what makes each one work and how it fails. Operating practice from his agency, no comparative test of the ordering.
Heath's structural instruction, stated 2026-03-03 and tied explicitly to Andromeda: run the formats simultaneously INSIDE one ad set rather than splitting them across ad sets. "within an ad set, you're going to have statics and carousels and videos, for example, because different ones are going to speak to different people." He gives no count, which matters for [[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]. Same session restates the style taxonomy as a distinct axis from format: UGC, influencer, founder-led, product or service demonstration, single client testimonial, multi-testimonial, animation, and they can be combined. His stated difference between UGC and influencer is not visual, it is that the influencer carries a following and therefore costs more and buys more scroll-stopping power.
The mechanism is disputed by Nick Theriot and the disagreement is worth carrying because he contradicts himself inside it. On 2026-07-10 he denies the delivery-side reason outright: "the technical aspect that people believe you need creative diversity cuz Facebook updated their algorithm and now we're in Andromeda era and yada yada yada. And that's completely false", and puts the whole effect on consumer taste, different people preferring different creative types. In the same transcript he endorses the delivery-side reason from the other direction, saying a cosmetic iteration is "exactly what Andromeda was going after when it said if you keep doing similar creatives, it's going to [ __ ] you over" (see [[Creative Science#CR-034|CR-034]]). Both positions are asserted with no data. The reconciliation that fits both of his statements: he rejects manufacturing diversity FOR the algorithm and accepts that near-duplicates are penalised BY it, which are compatible once the operative test is whether a variant reaches people the original could not. Do not bank his two statements as independent support for anything. Either way the operating instruction is identical across every voice here, diversify, so the disagreement is about why, not what to do.
Heath restated the delivery reason on 2026-02-17, in the same session where he gives his 20-plus-per-ad-set number, and it is the cleanest statement of the personalisation argument: Meta wants images and video, UGC, founder, demonstration and testimonial inside ONE ad set so it can match style to person, "for this person, they seem to really respond to UGC style ads, so we're going to put more of those in front of them. This person seems to respond to product demonstration style ads." So his diversity instruction and his ad-count instruction are the same instruction: 20-plus exists to give the system enough different things to choose between ([[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]). Still asserted, no delivery data.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; Ben Heath, Learn 97% of Meta Ads in Under 29 Minutes, 2026-08-18; Andrew Faris, The 3-Part Creative Diversity Framework I Use To Scale Past $10M On Meta Ads, 2026-05-01; Ben Heath, Copy This Meta Ads Testing Strategy for Better Results, 2026-03-03; Nick Theriot, Sh*t I care about when scaling Facebook Ads, 2026-07-10; Ben Heath, The BEST Facebook Ad Campaign Structure for 2026, 2026-02-17
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-055 · Launch with 2-3 creative variations, never one; the variety converts the launch into information and slows fatigue
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A single first ad is a guess; a small set of simple variations turns the launch into data about what the audience responds to, and the spread of exposure helps overcome fatigue. His hook standard: a 6-10 word claim or problem callout, with the offer understandable within the first ~2 seconds.
Sources: Ben Heath, What You NEED To Know To Get Started With Facebook Ads, 2026-08-06
