### CR-056 · Meta now takes one master creative upload (vertical with a 4:5 safe zone) instead of per-placement sizes; builds must design to the safe zone
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Shown live in the Ads Manager upload flow: you can no longer upload individual per-placement sizes; one asset renders across formats, so the vertical master must keep critical content inside the 4:5 safe zone. A related new surface auto-showcases the ad next to catalog products in carousel format, so catalog headline/primary text fields must be filled. Ben Heath's earlier standing advice (upload 9:16 for Stories/Reels plus 1:1 for feed rather than one horizontal asset, captions for sound-off viewing) still governs the design intent.
Dating the change: on 2026-04-28 Ben Heath demonstrated the old behaviour live, uploading a vertical master, rejecting Meta's square auto-crop as not working, clicking "replace" and uploading a separately-built square version, which he recommended as an optional but performance-positive extra. Sam Piliero's observation of the new behaviour is dated 2026-07-26. The per-placement upload therefore closed somewhere in that three-month window. A month earlier still, on 2026-03-24, Heath was giving the delivery mix as the reason to build per ratio at all: "A lot of ad impressions are delivered square, mostly feeds, and a lot of ad impressions are delivered in a vertical format, think reels and stories, far fewer in a horizontal format." Read that as the design brief that survives the upload change, not as a live instruction to upload three files.
The safe-zone rule as a production instruction, from the same 2026-03-24 session, and it is the reason to leave visual touch-ups on: keep captions in the MIDDLE of the frame and keep critical information away from the top and bottom edges, so nothing load-bearing is lost when Meta crops or expands. "if you see video ads of mine, the captions are always going to basically be in the middle, so that Meta can do those sorts of things. We're going to make sure that the really important information is not right at the top, right at the bottom."
Never ship a landscape master. Matt Shiver's argument is screen real estate at constant cost: "if you're going to pay Meta to put your ads in front of people, the more real estate that you take up, the better because you're paying the same. If I have just this video here, the whatever this is, 16 by 9, then that doesn't even take up half of the screen." His fix for footage already shot landscape is to pad it into a vertical frame with a headline banner above rather than serving it as 16:9. Reasoning only, no test, so T4 inside this claim.
Sources: Sam Piliero, The Only Facebook Ads Video You Need in 2026, 2026-07-26; Ben Heath, What You NEED To Know To Get Started With Facebook Ads, 2026-08-06; Ben Heath, The BEST Instagram Ads Tutorial for Beginners in 2026, 2026-04-28; Ben Heath, How To Use Creative Enhancements on Your Facebook Ads, 2026-03-24; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-057 · Differentiation is the core scaling variable and it decays: every edge gets copied, so creative strategy is a permanent search for the next separation
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Across every brand he has seen scale or fail, the deciding difference is being different; when all ads in a niche look the same, the consumer picks randomly. Being different works, gets copied, stops working. Five working modes: new mechanism, exaggerated claim/visuals, new avatar, new desire, new creative style. Style novelty is an arbitrage that closes: the first brands pushing native ads in their niche converted really well, then "everyone and their mom" ran them and style alone stopped differentiating.
Mark Builds Brands adds a construction step and a timing test. Construction: before writing anything, catalog the visual style, length, format and tone of every top competitor and creator in the space, then invert each variable. His worked example is Sam Sulek in fitness YouTube, running raw video frames as thumbnails and 30-plus-minute videos into a niche of highly produced thumbnails and 9-15 minute videos; he attributes that growth to contrast rather than to authenticity, and says authenticity was only one of many inverted variables. The obvious failure mode he does not address: some conventions exist because they convert.
Timing test: public discussion of a tactic is the saturation signal. He observed native-static performance decline across multiple accounts at roughly the same time the format started being talked about publicly on social media. By the time a format is being posted about as "the new meta," the practitioners who found it have run it for months or years. He separates the format (decays) from the principle (does not), noting native statics are decades old on native ad networks. Operator implication: adopt the principle, build a novel execution of it, and do not copy the executions currently circulating in marketing content. Multi-account observation, no numbers shown.
Blue Sense separates the two effects cleanly and gives the workflow that harvests the decaying half for free. Format is not what makes an ad work, but a new format still lifts performance in its early window purely because it is novel, until everyone catches on. So test formats on ORGANIC, where reach costs nothing, and translate whatever pops into paid. His live example at the time was the third-party company-breakdown green screen, everywhere on organic and almost nowhere in ads, which IM8 Health ported into an ad. That makes the novelty premium a repeatable harvest rather than a lucky spot, and it is the same free-pre-test logic as [[Creative Science#CR-128|CR-128]].
Sources: Nick Theriot, It Took Me 10 Years to Learn This Secret Facebook Ads Scaling Strategy, 2026-07-24; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-131 · The Twitter-style static is his most profitable and longest-lived format, and doubles as the near-free control for hook testing
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
"My most profitable ads have always been the Twitter style ads. Like they have been just tried and true. I've tried so many other ones and we have some that are profitable but like those last the longest." Production is a phone workflow: type the tweet, share it to story, screenshot the story. He tested white and black background versions and the format keeps winning. Because it costs almost nothing to make, he runs it as the permanent control for hook testing, then ports the winning hook verbatim into video and carousel, which isolates message from production quality. He is explicit that talking-head-wins-everything is offer-dependent rather than universal. Single-operator claim from a coaching and agency account, no numbers shown. Extends [[Creative Science#CR-037|CR-037]] and [[Creative Science#CR-114|CR-114]], which list Twitter-style static as one format in the rotation, by naming it the highest-ROI one and the designated control.
Alex Cooper runs the same play as the cheap-validation route for an angle that looks too narrow to fund: a static carries the copy at minimal production cost, so it answers the angle question before any video budget is committed, on the logic that copy strong enough to work will work without production. His worked case is an anti-dandruff shampoo client and a Reddit thread about people using a competitor's product on beard dandruff. They tested it as statics, the statics performed, then they built videos, which also performed, and beard dandruff is now a standing angle in that account. Practical order: new angle goes to static first, video only after the static clears.
CONTESTED BY THE SAME OPERATOR, 2026-06-16, nine days before the claim above, and the contradiction is on the durability half specifically. On text-tile and Twitter-style statics: "These can work like they'll get you very cheap leads, but they often have a short lifespan or a short shelf life because once people have seen it enough, it's not like something that people are going to share. Sometimes if video ads or carousel ads are cold enough, but have enough value and education in them, people will share them." So on 2026-06-16 the format is cheap and short-lived and on 2026-06-25 it is the longest-lived thing in his account. Neither statement carries numbers or a date range. His proposed mechanism for the short version, shareability, is at least a testable one, and it predicts the format should outlive its shelf life in a category where nobody shares anything.
What survives the contradiction, because both tellings agree on it: the tile is the cheap CONTROL, not the scaling asset. Use it to test hooks and offer names at near-zero production cost, then promote whatever wins into video or carousel where the education can carry and the asset can be shared. That is the same two-stage shape as the organic pre-test in [[Creative Science#CR-128|CR-128]]. Do not quote the longest-lived line without this note attached.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-132 · Format follows what the ad is selling: a direct offer needs video or carousel, a free resource can run on a static that does not fatigue fast
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Rule as stated. If the ad sends people to apply or watch a VSL, use video, unless there is a very strong risk reversal, and even then a risk reversal may pull the wrong buyer. If the ad sends people to a lead magnet, free community or blueprint, a static carries it and does not fatigue quickly, "because the resource itself is so valuable. It doesn't need a 60 to minute video to sell it." Two secondary gates: if the client is bad on camera, start with carousel or static regardless of the offer; if it is a mass-market fitness offer, video is mandatory because personality is the only differentiator that cuts through. Asserted from his own client base, no comparative data.
The same rule stated as a format-to-funnel constraint, with the mechanism named: the Instagram-organic-style post, meaning a phone photo of the deliverable plus bubble text, works for lead magnets and webinar funnels and does not work for a direct application VSL funnel. His reason is that the format itself reads as PRODUCT-AWARE, "the style of the ad itself is like, get this product for free, right? And if you make it irresistible, people will claim it, but it doesn't work as well for like your coaching program." So the format signals what kind of ask is coming, and a format that promises a free thing cannot carry a high-commitment application. Asserted from his own client base, no comparison shown.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-133 · Fashion creative must be planned against inventory: brief multi-product assets so a stockout does not kill the ad, and keep old-season ads running while they are still efficient
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A winning single-product fashion ad that could have held $100k of spend has to be switched off the moment the product or its core size sells out, which is why fashion accounts struggle to accumulate evergreen scaling assets and why a scaling CBO is often not viable there. Fix at the brief stage: never commission a UGC asset featuring one SKU, always ask for multiple products or a haul format, which extends asset life and widens the audience the ad can resonate with.
The mirror-image error is killing old-season ads the day the new drop lands. Sell-through on last season's stock only declines from here, so cutting its only demand driver guarantees markdown later. Creative planning already runs 1.5-2.5 months ahead of product availability, so plan the wind-down of the old alongside the launch of the new, using inventory visibility to decide which old products still need paid support. An agency without inventory data cannot make this call and defaults to the destructive version.
Cheap standing production rule from the same section, worth applying but untested (T4 within this claim): put size data on screen or in the primary text, because returns silently halve fashion efficiency and a creative-level intervention that reduces them is worth more than an equivalent CTR gain. No numbers given for the size-data rule.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-134 · Fashion production mix should be 25% campaign shoots, 40% UGC and creator, 20% organic repurposing, 15% statics, and the format the vertical runs least is the one that wins
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The 40% UGC bucket explicitly includes founder-led and employee-generated content. Two underused formats inside it: EGC, meaning staff shooting in-store and behind-the-scenes content that many brands already post to TikTok and never repurpose into ads; and raw fitting-room try-on content, which he says has carried a couple of Australian brands into international scale. The split is a starting allocation to move with performance data, not a law. Most fashion brands sit far above 25% on campaign shoots, which he calls the structural error of the vertical because that content is not native to the platforms.
The library evidence is SHOWN: across five live Meta ad-library teardowns (Zara, White Fox, Sabo, Country Road, I Love Ugly) the recurring gaps are no partnership ads, no low-fi content, and no audio hooks, with brands assuming audio is off-brand. Country Road's single top-performing hook, live 22 days, was a piece of low-fi UGC, the format they run least. Ad counts and format mixes are shown on screen; the performance inference is drawn from ad longevity and library sort order rather than account metrics, so treat the ranking as inferred. Same teardown surfaces the volume problem: Country Road ships about 10 ads every 3-4 days at $1B of revenue, and Zara runs zero standalone videos.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-137 · Creative that does not read as marketing clears the "is this a promotion" filter, which is the shared principle behind native ads, advertorials and quiz funnels
Tier: T4 · Status: active
"Being able to create marketing content that doesn't look like marketing content is a superpower. That's the exact philosophy behind all the top-performing advertising assets that we have today. Advertorials, same exact thing. Quiz funnels, same exact thing." Pairs with contrast as the second half of a set: contrast gets attention, disguise stops the reader classifying the asset as an ad and discarding it before the argument starts. The two rules are in open tension (stand out AND blend in) and he acknowledges that as a balance rather than resolving it.
Historical support is the Gary Halbert direct-mail letter, built after observing that people open mail standing over the recycling bin and sort promotion-versus-relevant in one glance, with a claimed $100,000/day in 1960s-70s dollars on a $2 offer and 20 full-time employees cashing checks. Those revenue figures are anecdotal and unverifiable. Distinct from [[Creative Science#CR-047|CR-047]], which is the format spec for native statics rather than the governing principle, and from [[Creative Science#CR-057|CR-057]], which is the contrast half.
The current executable version of the principle in short-form video is the FAKE PODCAST CLIP: film looking off-camera as if answering an interviewer, open on a status-plus-reset hook such as "I'm a millionaire now, but if I had to start from zero, this is exactly what I would do." Matt Shiver runs it and names the tell without treating it as a problem: "This is a fake podcast clip, but they work really well as ads. You might notice this on organic as well where it almost sounds like the answer's too crisp and no one would ever say that in a podcast." Two things worth taking from that. The disguise does not have to be airtight to work, which weakens the pure-deception reading of this principle and supports the format-familiarity reading. And the format is borrowed from a surface the viewer already consumes voluntarily, which is the same harvest logic as [[Creative Science#CR-057|CR-057]] and [[Creative Science#CR-156|CR-156]]. No per-ad data.
TWO TECHNIQUES THAT CROSS FROM DISGUISE INTO FABRICATION, recorded here with our own position attached because the line matters more than the tactic. Fraser Cottrell overlays a FABRICATED TikTok comment on the hook section so the ad reads as organic, generated with a tool built to produce fake TikTok comments. He also specs an Instagram-story mimicry static, serif text in boxes with the brand handle prefixed with @ and underlined "so it looks like we tagged someone". Both asserted, neither lift-tested. The distinction against everything else in this claim: an advertorial, a native static and a fake podcast clip borrow a FORMAT the viewer already consumes, while these two manufacture the appearance of THIRD-PARTY ENDORSEMENT that does not exist, and one of them stacks that on an AI-generated actor. Our own standing rules forbid manufacturing proof, so the technique is banked as observed practice and we do not run it. The transferable half without the fabrication is real: harvest a genuine comment from your own posts and overlay that instead.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16; Fraser Cottrell, The New Way to Make Ai UGC in 2026, 2026-03-15; Fraser Cottrell, Watch Me Create 3 Static Ads With AI From Scratch, 2026-04-05
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-144 · Organic content promoted as-is has a one-to-two week shelf life, because it was not built to ad structure; rebuild the organic winner as an ad
Tier: T3 · Status: active
"I'll just put it in as an ad and it'll do well for the first week or two and then it just drops off. So again, cuz it wasn't intended to be an ad." He attributes the drop to structural difference rather than audience fatigue: an ad runs 30-60 seconds (90 max) and is problem-agitate-solution, while organic runs longer and does belief-shifting and relationship work. The consequence for the standard "test organically then promote the winners" playbook is that the organic winner should be REBUILT to ad structure, not boosted. His own caveat: this approach would probably work better for someone whose organic gets real volume, and his own organic is too small to generate a verdict. Sits against the seeding evidence in [[Creative Science#CR-120|CR-120]], where a creator's organically viral video ported directly into paid and became the account's breakout ad, so the two should be read as a live question rather than a settled rule.
The sharpest version of the argument is the economics of the wrong audience, from Blue Sense. A commercial snow and ice management ad opens with a submarine escape explainer then cuts to the business, and pulled a few million views organically; he was served it in Australia, where it does not snow, because he watches that content type. His arithmetic: 2 million to 10 million organic views cost nothing, so 10 leads is free upside, while buying the same 2 million to 10 million views costs roughly half a million dollars in ad spend for the same 10 leads. Meme and unrelated-content openers therefore survive on free reach and die on paid, with a 1% to 2% exception in niches such as gaming. Numbers illustrative, not measured, but the direction is the point: organic is scored on views and paid is scored on who the views were.
Two smaller transfer rules from the same theme. Cross-platform, a working Meta ad pasted into YouTube fails most of the time; it needs adapting because YouTube is a creative and hook game more than a targeting game. Cross-surface, when choosing an existing post to promote, take the Instagram post over the Facebook post, because a native Instagram post out-engages a Facebook post repurposed onto Instagram, and name the ad with the Instagram caption so it is findable later. Both practitioner claims with no side-by-side shown.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29; Blue Sense Digital, Google Ads in 2026 (w/ Austin from Grow My Ads), 2025-12-15; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026 (Full Course Post-Andromeda), 2026-04-09
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-152 · Break CPM and frequency down by placement, then shift reach by supplying only a 9:16 asset so Meta defaults toward Reels and Stories
Tier: T3 · Status: active
CPM often varies widely by placement, and Instagram Reels is frequently the cheap pocket, so a placement-level breakdown can expose reach available at a fraction of the cost. He explicitly rejects the obvious move, a custom-placement ad set forcing spend into Reels, in favour of an asset-level nudge: "we'll only upload videos in 9 by 16 tall so that we don't give Facebook a or Meta, sorry, a 4 by 5 feed asset so that Meta hopefully defaults to Reels or Stories." Note the hedge in his own words ("hopefully"), and check it against [[Creative Science#CR-056|CR-056]]: now that Meta takes one master vertical upload and renders it across formats using a 4:5 safe zone, the "give it no feed asset" lever may already be weakening. Asserted, no placement data shown.
The lever is weaker than the hedge suggests, and there is now a named setting that defeats it. Meta's "flex media" enhancement does exactly the opposite job: "Show media you chose for a specific aspect ratio across all placements when it's likely to improve performance." Ben Heath describes the effect in the direction Faris wants, Meta putting the vertical file into Instagram feed where it takes more scroll space, and leaves the toggle on. So supplying only 9:16 no longer withholds a feed asset from Meta, it just tells Meta which file to stretch. If you are running this play, check whether flex media is enabled on the ad before reading any placement shift as evidence the lever worked. Neither operator has shown placement data either way.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Your Meta Ads Account Has Too Many Campaigns, 2026-05-12; Ben Heath, How To Use Creative Enhancements on Your Facebook Ads, 2026-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-163 · The low-production lead-gen video spine: avatar and offer inside the first 4 to 10 seconds, props doing the positioning, three benefits with a time duration, then the CTA
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Two builds of the same structure, both from a coaching and agency book, both shot on a phone. The 18-second version: state the avatar, disqualify the wrong approach, name the system, list three benefits WITH A TIME DURATION attached, then the call to action. The voiceover B-roll version runs the same opening and then alternates proof-of-self B-roll with client case studies, closing on a DM keyword. The timing spec is the tight part: "you can already see in 4 seconds he caught his avatar... If you've ate clean, worked hard, and want to compete in bodybuilding in 2026, but just have no idea how to get there, here's my offer, right? Within less than 10 seconds." So avatar plus offer both land inside 10 seconds, which is the opposite instruction to the delayed product introduction in [[Creative Science#CR-127|CR-127]]. The two are reconcilable by funnel type rather than by preference: a lead-gen ad selling a free or low-commitment next step can name the ask early, an ecommerce top-of-funnel video selling a purchase cannot. Neither operator states that boundary.
The overlooked half is the SET. The frame does the positioning before a word of copy lands: strength and conditioning equipment behind the presenter, a volleyball in his hands, "just visually alone without the copy and text, you can see what he does." That makes props and background a scripting decision rather than a production one, and it is the cheapest credibility available on a phone shoot ([[Creative Science#CR-123|CR-123]]). Both builds are described from ads he runs, with no per-ad performance data shown.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-167 · A shown format CPA gap in one account: video at 856 GBP against images at 189 and carousel at 222
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Read off a screen share of a creative analytics tool: "Videos are getting more spend than the other ad formats, but generating a significantly higher CPA 856 pounds in comparison to images at 189 and carousel at 222." A 4.5x gap between the most expensive and the cheapest format in one account, with more of the budget sitting on the expensive one. The counts are T2 because they were on screen.
FOUR CAVEATS, and they have to travel with the numbers. The segment is a PAID SPONSOR slot for the analytics tool, so the example was selected to demonstrate the product finding a problem. The account is not named. No date range is stated. The spend split is given only as "videos are getting more spend", with no figure, so the CPA gap cannot be weighted. Nothing here establishes a general law about formats, and a single account with an unstated date range is exactly the shape of evidence that produces "video doesn't work for us" folklore, which [[Creative Science#CR-126|CR-126]] warns about by name.
What it is genuinely good for: it is a worked instance of spend allocation and efficiency pointing in opposite directions, which is the diagnostic the tool exists to surface and which a format-blind ad set will hide. The transferable move is the breakdown itself, split CPA by format on your own account before deciding where the next production budget goes. Note it also sits against [[Creative Science#CR-125|CR-125]] and Charley T's flat rule that format has nothing to do with campaign structure; a format-level CPA read does not require format-level ad sets, only a breakdown.
Sources: Ben Heath, I Reviewed Real Facebook Ads Heres What Converts, 2026-02-24
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Creator, UGC and AI creative

### CR-058 · Creator/partnership (whitelisted) ads consistently outperform brand-produced ads
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Meta CMO Alex Schultz says advertisers get better ROI doing authentic content with creators and boosting it as ads, and Ben Heath independently reports the best-performing ad in audited accounts is often a creator ad. Nick Theriot: whitelisting pays off for 80-90% of advertisers who actually fund it, run inside the main CBO with one ad set per creator. Ben's economics: allocating ~10% of a large budget to partnership ads typically lifts ROAS by more than the 10% cost.
Blue Sense adds the delivery mechanism and a much higher observed ceiling. Partnership ads target using the audiences of BOTH handles, which is why they scale on cold traffic and not only in warm retargeting. On some of his accounts partnership or collaborator ads carry up to 40% of total account spend, four times Ben's allocation: "If you are not running them, you were just at a disadvantage to competitors." He also notes Meta now lets you choose which handle displays (brand only, creator only, or both) and optimises the combination. His own hedge on the ceiling: "I think people are catching on to this actually, and the arbitrage is probably coming towards its end." Asserted from agency accounts, no shown data. Sourcing mechanics are in [[Creative Science#CR-115|CR-115]]; the whitelisting-versus-partnership distinction is in [[Creative Science#CR-047|CR-047]].
The advantage is double, because there is a delivery-cost half as well as a creative-quality half. Kiel: "one of the big things that we've seen for a long time is partnership ads can get a cheaper CPM." He reports it consistently over a long period across his accounts, from a large volume of partners posting regularly, so you borrow the partner's audience AND get a platform pricing advantage. Carry his hedge: his explanation for why, that Meta shifted platform favour from the shopping feed toward partnership ads and partnership content, is flagged by him as a theory. No data shown. The CPM half is banked in full at MD-053.
Ben Heath states the trade-off arithmetic that makes the allocation decision explicit: "if you take 10% of your advertising budget and pay that to creators, but that creator makes your ad campaign 40% more effective, that's a great trade, you should do that every time." Illustrative numbers, no case data, and the mechanism he names is two-part, the recognised creator raising hook rate and the endorsement raising conversion. Charley T adds two operating angles from the same session: partners reinterpret your product in ways you would not, and if video production is a weakness you can let partners carry the video while you run statics.
Faris independently observes the audience-borrowing effect and flags that it contradicts his own model, which is worth recording because he does not resolve it. He argues serving is based on content and engagement rather than account and social profile, then reports that partnership ads do seem to pull audiences from the creator's account. His two candidate explanations, neither committed to: some account-level signal reaching Meta, or simply that different creators appeal to different users. Either way he treats it as a workaround that adds diversity regardless of mechanism.
Heath's hardest version of the claim, 2026-03-03, and it is the strongest ranking any operator in this file gives a single intervention: "there is nothing that I see repeatedly improve Meta ad results more than that. Than hiring influencers to produce video ads." He names it as also the most ignored of his recommendations, because it sits outside advertisers' comfort zones, and reports the lift showing up in three places at once, hook rate, view to click, and click to conversion. Corroborated only by peer conversation at industry events, no data shown, from a book he states as $300M of lifetime Meta spend.
Two figures that size the opportunity, both weakly sourced. Andrew Faris relays a Meta summit stat as roughly 30% of top advertiser accounts running partnership ads, and immediately turns it around: "You hear that and it's like, 'Well, 70% is not then.'" He does not quote a slide and flags it as recollection, so carry it as an order of magnitude. And Blue Sense's 2025-02-26 case is the hardest single result attached to the format anywhere in this file: on one account, two partnership ads had produced 80% of all results in its history and had not been run for four months; relaunched, they returned CPCs 10x lower than anything else in the account and ROAS 3x higher inside a week, with account performance almost doubling. Multiples only, one week, nothing shown ([[Creative Science#CR-121|CR-121]]).
Sources: Ben Heath, Meta's CMO on AI Ads and Rising Meta Ad Costs, 2026-07-20; Ben Heath, $100 vs $100,000 Facebook Ads Strategy, 2026-08-12; Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; You Just Found a Winning Ad, 2026-07-31; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Andrew Faris, Your Meta Ads Account Has Too Many Campaigns, 2026-05-12; Professor Charley T, Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies, 2026-04-15; Andrew Faris, The 3-Part Creative Diversity Framework I Use To Scale Past $10M On Meta Ads, 2026-05-01; Ben Heath, Copy This Meta Ads Testing Strategy for Better Results, 2026-03-03; Andrew Faris, 2026 Meta Performance Marketing Summit Recap, 2026-05-20; Blue Sense Digital, 5 Step Checklist to Increase Meta Ad Performance Overnight, 2025-02-26
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-059 · When boosting creators, authenticity and audience-to-target match drive results, not creator reach
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Meta's internal finding per Schultz: advertisers who chase the biggest creator and manipulate the message to fit the brand do far, far worse than those who pick a smaller creator whose audience matches the target and let them speak authentically, then buy the sponsored ad. Selection criterion is authentic match first, reach last.
Charley T adds a second selection axis that does not conflict with audience match: aim for minimal overlap with the audience your ads ALREADY reach, because the incrementality comes from the non-overlapping side. His stated best result is a sneaker company partnering with a woman making DIY furniture and arts and crafts, whose audience had never been served the brand's ads, which he says brought in more new customers in three weeks than any other ad had in the previous three years. Result asserted, no numbers shown. The rule is a small Venn overlap rather than a creator embedded in your category where everyone is already being advertised to.
Faris reports the corollary from outcomes rather than selection: creator winners repeatedly land outside the demographic the brand targeted. Brands expecting 30-year-old creators win with a 60-year-old, brands expecting all-female creators win with a male, a mom talking about the product for her daughter beats the creator talking about herself. He treats that range as a structural reason creator programs work at all, because it produces creative diversity a scripted pipeline would never have briefed. Both asserted from observation, no data.
Sources: Ben Heath, Meta's CMO on AI Ads and Rising Meta Ad Costs, 2026-07-20; Professor Charley T, Facebook Ads in 2026: NEW Secrets, Tips & Strategies, 2026-04-15; Andrew Faris, The 4 Cylinder Marketing Engine Powering My $10M+ Ecom Clients' Success, 2026-05-04
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-060 · Real-creator UGC still beats AI UGC; use AI video only for storytelling and problem visualization, never product demonstration or fake social proof
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Nick Theriot: after producing hundreds of AI UGC videos, his accounts are still winning with standard ship-the-product UGC. Blue Sense: AI product demos are a double loss (viewers who spot the AI distrust the brand; viewers fooled into buying get a product that can't match the demo, producing refunds and zero LTV); good AI uses are visualizing the problem, founder-story illustration, and expensive-to-shoot effects, cutting to real footage the moment the product appears. UGC works because of the creator's genuine authority, which an AI actor cannot replicate.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale (12 Real Examples), 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-061 · AI-generated creative splits into two lanes: compliance-heavy industries get unblocked by AI legal review, while truth-demonstration brands should not use AI creative at all
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Schultz says Meta is building AI judges/lawyers that review creative better than the average human lawyer, expecting to unblock compliance-heavy advertisers over time. Brands whose ad IS a physical proof (Persil stain removal, Dove real beauty) should not use AI imagery because a generated image makes the demonstration false. Forward-looking claims from Meta's CMO, no data shown.
Fashion is the category where the truth-demonstration rule carries a measurable financial consequence rather than a reputational one. Blue Sense uses AI heavily in every other category and excludes fashion creative specifically: "if someone can't get clear visibility on exactly what the garment actually looks like in real life, then return rates end up shooting up." In a visually driven purchase the buyer commits based on exactly what the garment looks like, so any generated divergence converts into a refund rather than a merely disappointed customer who keeps the product. Supplements and benefit-driven categories do not carry that exposure. Asserted as an agency policy, no return-rate data shown.
Service-based lead gen is a third excluded category and the reason is the same one, the ad is a claim about something that happened. Ben Heath refuses Advantage+ creative and generative imagery on a lead-gen account: "what's being offered here, lead-based, is a service. It gets a little bit sticky in terms of where you're just creating stuff that didn't actually happen. Like, these are images of the service being implemented. No, we want them to remain true." He also refuses it on already-edited video, on the separate ground that the edit was deliberate. So the working rule across three operators is that AI creative is safe where it illustrates a problem or a concept and unsafe where it depicts a delivery, a garment, or a result.
Sources: Ben Heath, Meta's CMO on AI Ads and Rising Meta Ad Costs, 2026-07-20; Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25; Ben Heath, How Good Is Meta's New AI Business Assistant?, 2026-06-03
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-062 · Data-driven targeting does not obsolete mass-reach celebrity creative
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Schultz, self-described as "all the data, the targets," says the single best thing he did was the Super Bowl 2025 spot with Chris Pratt, Chris Hemsworth and Kris Jenner, a buy his own analysis would have called overpriced and unlikely to land. Meta also runs roughly 1,000 creators for its glasses marketing, buying the creator playbook at scale itself.

### CR-063 · Spend AI video budget on the hook clip only: top-tier model for the first 3-5 seconds, cheaper models after
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The first 3-5 seconds are the most important by far, so the most expensive video model (Seedance 2) is reserved for hook clips and cheaper models handle post-hook footage. He moved from Veo 3.1 to Kling Omni post-hook because fewer retries made it net cheaper despite higher per-generation cost. Native statics were all Nano Banana Pro; video = good NBP images then image-to-video.
The SAME operator gave a different rationing rule three months earlier, and the two are worth holding together because the axis changes. Rather than reserving the expensive model for the first 3-5 seconds, he reserves it for REMAKING PROVEN WINNERS and bans it from testing outright: "All I'm doing is taking my top performing creatives and remaking them with Seedance. I'm not using it for testing because, again, it's very slow and it is quite expensive." So the earlier rule rations by position in the timeline and the later rule rations by whether the concept has already earned spend. They are compatible if applied together, expensive model on the hook of an ad that already won, and nobody stated that reconciliation. Model ranking as of 2026-04-29, asserted from daily production with no side-by-side and no prices given: Kling 3.0 best quality-to-cost, Veo 3.1 cheaper and faster but limited on clip length, Seedance 2.0 most realistic and by far the most expensive. Image-to-video is his default pipeline and text-to-video is reserved for from-scratch concepts, which matches the NBP-then-image-to-video build above.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping, 2026-07-30; Mark Builds Brands, i tried running only AI ads for 30 days, 2026-04-29

### CR-064 · AI image ad pipeline: strategist defines the desire and required elements, pulls scaling-static inspiration from Trend Track, then a designer builds in Higgsfield
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Strategy first (which desire, what the image must show and say), inspiration from brands scaling on Trend Track, production in Higgsfield, sometimes seeding with a ChatGPT reference image, especially when compositing specific people. Claude can be connected to Trend Track and Higgsfield to find top-scaling image ads and recreate them for your product automatically.
Independent live-account support, and note it is AI applied inside an existing design process rather than one-shot generation: Faris reports the top-spending ads in one of the accounts his agency runs are built from AI-designed BACKGROUNDS with very minimal real assets. He offers it deliberately as a counterweight to his own scepticism about AI hook tools. No spend figures, CPA, or comparison against non-AI creative in the same account. The transferable read is that the win came from designers using AI on the part of the frame nobody photographs, which is also the cheapest place to test it.
Sources: Nick Theriot, NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05; Andrew Faris, Why Are Early AI Adopters' Businesses Performing So Badly? (With Patrick Coddou), 2026-05-08
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-065 · The creative strategist's job is deciding what ads to make and why, not making them
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The strategist directs: research (largest share of the day), scripts, shot lists, casting input, edit review, and post-launch data review checking whether the imagined outcome happened. They are not the editor, camera talent or caster. Five years ago the role was mostly script writing; now it is mostly research fed into AI for first drafts the strategist crafts, because targeting moved into the creative and the strategist therefore owns delivery outcomes.
Andrew Faris names the hiring error that follows from getting this wrong: "a creative strategist is a copywriter first, a messaging person first." The role fails for most teams because they hire someone good with a camera when they need someone good with words, which follows directly from message sitting above format and talent in [[Creative Science#CR-054|CR-054]]. Screen the role on writing, not on a reel.
Meta states the same division of labour as its own strategic position, which is the strongest available reason to staff this role. Matt Steiner, who runs monetization infrastructure and ranking: "humans are never going to outperform the machines at that type of work again. Humans are never going to out optimize the machine. They're never going to move the budget as quickly, as responsively to price changes." What Meta leaves to the operator is novel message ideation plus the post-hoc reasoning about why a message did or did not resonate. Tiered T3 and not T1: a named Meta employee on a podcast, no document quoted and no data shown. Read alongside MD-001, since it is the platform conceding that settings and budget are no longer the lever and creative is.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Become a $10k/month Creative Strategist in 2026, 2026-07-26; Andrew Faris, The 3-Part Creative Diversity Framework I Use To Scale Past $10M On Meta Ads, 2026-05-01; Sam Piliero, Meta Ads Insider Exposes How The Algorithm ACTUALLY Works, 2026-06-04
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-066 · AI ad tools regress to averages, so angle selection and prioritization must stay a human judgment call
Tier: T4 · Status: active
LLMs write decent copy and analyze video but are built on averages: they favor what has worked before and cannot integrate customer research, product research, competitive landscape, past performance and growth plans to decide which angles to test first and in what format. The defensible human layer is that synthesis-and-prioritization step plus cross-account pattern recognition.
Blue Sense turns this into an allocation rule: apply AI to the MIDDLE of the creative process and keep humans on both ends. "With AI application, I would try to skip the outsides and really maximize throughput on the middle." Where it pays: briefing and scripting, via a Claude skill that walks a roughly 20-question intake (persona, concept, offer, long-form or short, awareness stage) and outputs a brief or script 99% of the way there; and production, where VSLs and static image ads can be ~90% AI. Where it costs: on the analysis end it mis-weights the metric hierarchy and misses account structure and commercial context, so using it for core decisions actively misleads. On ideation he is specific that no amount of context or Reddit scraping produces differentiated concepts, and that if you do use an LLM for personas you must hand it 30-40 good manual ones first, then cut its 20 outputs down to 4-5 and re-prompt. Asserted from agency workflow, no output-quality test shown.
Three sourcing and adoption rules converge on the same point, that the model's output quality is set by what you hand it and by whether you can grade the answer. Alex Cooper: do the research manually BEFORE running it through AI, because the model answers confidently whether it is right or wrong and without your own reading you cannot grade it. His concrete tactic is sorting reviews by length and reading the longest ones for five minutes, which carries context no summary preserves; he also notes AI cannot do the consumption analysis in [[Creative Science#CR-156|CR-156]]. Charley T on copy specifically: give the model the business goal of the ad plus real source material, meaning existing marketing assets, customer language, and pages that already convert, otherwise the copy reads fake. He names writing to your own taste as the single biggest mistake advertisers make, and his voice test is that the ad should sound like the BRAND if it is claiming authority or like the CUSTOMER if the customer needs to see themself in it, with one of each running together.
Adoption filter from Patrick Coddou and Faris, which is the cheapest guard against tool churn: name the problem first, then evaluate whether the tool solves it, never the reverse. The failure mode they name in their own Slack is broadcasting a tool announcement and saying "test it out". Faris adds the hidden cost, that whoever evaluates the tool stops doing the work that already produces results, so evaluation needs dedicated space rather than being tacked onto delivery. He also discounts public AI demos hard: he has seen a thousand individual AI outputs posted as things that work and found them dramatically harder and worse when reproducing them, especially with visual generation. His case is Sora 2 hooks, planned six months earlier and never shipped because usable output required endless re-prompting and someone dedicated to mastering the tool. He compares it to Shopify revenue screenshots on Twitter, true, engagement-optimised and missing the causes, and treats the absence of "we used Sora 2 and grew 200%" stories as itself evidence the business impact is small.
Nick Theriot draws the same line and names the specific prompting failure that follows from crossing it: "AI is doing the copywriting and we're just more doing the positioning of like what desire we want to go after, doing the manual research together, and then we get AI to do everything else." The failure mode is asking the model for "a different desire" instead of naming which desire, which hands the model the one decision the human is supposed to own. Enforceable version: every AI copy prompt must state the desire, the persona and the awareness stage explicitly, and if you cannot state them the brief is not finished. Workflow, no data.
A negative signal worth recording because it is about what META is steering advertisers toward, not about tool quality. Faris reports that at the 2026 Meta Performance Marketing Summit, for the second year running, Meta said little about mass-generating AI creative as a way to win: "they really didn't say much about mass generating AI creative as a way of winning on Meta. They're sort of quiet about that, even though it's all anybody wants to talk about on Twitter." What Meta pushed hard instead was creators and partnership ads ([[Creative Science#CR-058|CR-058]]). An absence observed by one attendee, so it proves nothing on its own, but it is the platform declining to endorse the thing its own tooling most obviously enables.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Become a $10k/month Creative Strategist in 2026, 2026-07-26; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026 (Full Course Post-Andromeda), 2026-04-09; Andrew Faris, Why Are Early AI Adopters' Businesses Performing So Badly? (With Patrick Coddou), 2026-05-08; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Andrew Faris, 2026 Meta Performance Marketing Summit Recap, 2026-05-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-067 · Editing polish does not drive conversion outcomes; raw video now performs
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A micro-niche business channel uploading zero-edit whiteboard-and-camera videos daily ("you could literally see me walk up to the camera and turn it on") generated 3-4 qualified bookings per day. Time spent editing is usually personal brand preference, not a data-driven driver of retention or conversion; the 2018-19 hyper-edited retention style has inverted.
Mark Builds Brands reports the same from paid, on roughly $4M spent in one account: his highest-spending, most profitable ads look bad. He is careful about the direction of the relationship: ugliness does not create profit, clarity does, and ugly ads correlate with profit only because they were built for clarity rather than for looks. He names content creators making cinematic ads as the archetypal failure. Two rules follow: never reject a creative on production-value grounds alone, and never spend budget upgrading polish on an ad whose message is already clear. Asserted, no A/B on polish.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Actually Grow a YouTube Channel (he has 3.5M subs), 2026-08-06; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-120 · Creative diversity comes from three to five independent SOURCES of creative running in parallel, funded as variable spend you can defund in a week
Tier: T3 · Status: active
She's Birdie's five sources, stood up over 3-6 months: the agency's traditional performance creative, agency edits of existing brand assets, briefed paid UGC actors (5-10 per month, sourced through a group), TikTok affiliate seeding (200-500 creators per month), and a retained micro-creator roster. Total creative budget stated as $25-30k/month on a low-mid eight-figure business. Andrew Faris's coarser version of the same idea is two buckets that must both be present, creator content and traditional performance-marketing content.
The transferable number is the retained micro-creator economics: roughly 50-60 creators paid under $100/month each, delivering 10-15 videos per creator per month, which implies 500-900 pieces of content a month for well under $6k, an order of magnitude below agency per-asset pricing. The price is attributed to volume of outreach and hard negotiation ("it just takes some tough skin and enough digging"), staffed by a dedicated hire who does nothing but source and negotiate. Second-order benefit: the videos post organically first, so you get a free performance read before porting winners into the ad account, the same logic as [[Creative Science#CR-128|CR-128]].
Evidence that the seeding source pays: an affiliate video that went viral organically on TikTok became the account's breakout paid ad, scaling from roughly $5k to $50k in spend within a couple of days and taking about 50% of February spend, driving six-figure revenue days outside Q4 for a brand whose Q4 is 40-60% of annual revenue. Faris credits manual bids for making the scale-up fast once it proved out. Both are explicit that nobody predicts which asset does this: "we never predict that any ad, whether we make it or a creator makes it, is going to have the kind of impact that did."
The budgeting frame around it is reasoning rather than a tested finding (T4 within this claim): deploy creative budget as "mobile artillery" rather than "stationary artillery", meaning variable spend across swappable sources instead of a fixed retained creative agency, because a fixed retainer buys a cost-per-asset and an inevitable drift toward re-shooting variations of the same concept. His stated decay point, about the fifth iteration of the same concept from one source, is illustrative and not measured. Numbers throughout were read aloud from the account, not screen-shared.
The definitional line between the two engines is the brief: a creator is BRIEFED, whether loosely or from a tight talking-points list, and a performance ad is SCRIPTED. That difference produces content socially native in ways scripted work cannot replicate, and it demands a different operational skill set, mass coordination of people and deals rather than production systems. Brands routinely pick one lane; both are needed at account level, and the audit is whether an ad-library scan shows genuinely different kinds of creative.
Which is why the same account's record month is the corrective to reading CR-120 as a creator-first claim. A TikTok creator ad ate a large share of She's Birdie spend in February and into March, but the top-spending ads driving the record April were long-form SCRIPTED explainer ads showing the product clearly and explaining why it works. Faris reports the same in an audit of an unrelated account and calls the explainer the muscle brands must develop, with another client winning on classically designed stills, some low-fi social-native, some traditional banner style, with strong clear headline copy. No numbers shown for the April read.
One economic note on the seeding source: take affiliate and commission-only creator deals wherever available, since they push production risk entirely onto the creator, and pay generously on the upside because you carry no downside. Faris expects the model to expire. The same commission-only structure existed on Instagram and Facebook, margin flowed to brands, creators eventually demanded upfront payment, and the deals became hard to strike, so treat the current TikTok mass-affiliate wave as an arbitrage window rather than a permanent structure. Prediction plus historical pattern, no data.
Sources: Andrew Faris, From A 65% Decline To All-Time Revenue And Profit Highs (Richie Mashiko), 2026-04-27; Andrew Faris, The 4 Cylinder Marketing Engine Powering My $10M+ Ecom Clients' Success, 2026-05-04; Andrew Faris, The One Part Of Your Business Where You Should Be Wasting More Money, 2026-06-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-135 · Commission a silent take of every fashion UGC asset: non-talking fashion UGC performs, and it ports into foreign markets without the accent penalty
Tier: T3 · Status: active
"Fashion UGC does not need talking, which is honestly one of the huge benefits of it." Some of his best-performing fashion UGC has no voiceover, which removes the main barrier to reusing an asset in another country. The ask is cheap: have the creator shoot a silent take alongside the talking take, which gives a second testable asset and a globally portable one. This is a workaround for [[Creative Science#CR-099|CR-099]] (UK and Australian accents do not transfer into the US) that avoids the re-voicing cost. Format note from the same section: close-up to full-body pullback is currently working, and the durable lesson is that formats rotate, so track what works organically and port it into paid rather than treating any format as permanent. Asserted, no comparative data.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-169 · Nobody in the AI-creative literature has compared AI creative against non-AI creative, so every stated advantage is a production-cost claim
Tier: T3 · Status: active
THE FINDING, and it is about the evidence base rather than about any tactic. Across the AI-creative sources read into this file, including three transcripts whose entire subject is AI creative, not one runs a performance comparison against non-AI creative in the same account over the same window. Every advantage claimed for AI creative rests on production speed, production cost or volume. That is a real advantage and it is not the advantage people quote it as.
THE ONLY TWO NUMBERS ATTACHED TO AI CREATIVE ANYWHERE. [[Creative Science#CR-168|CR-168]], a month of AI-only ads at $2.2M of sales on just over $1M of spend, which has no control period and no non-AI comparison. And self-reported production times, which are not performance at all.
THE PRODUCTION ECONOMICS, stated, and this is the part that is genuinely established: about 30 minutes for a finished AI UGC video, "You can create AI UGC that looks just like this, and it only takes about half an hour"; about 5 minutes for a finished AI static including generation; $5 to $30 in AI credits per high-quality ad. Set that against the creative-budget arithmetic in [[Creative Science#CR-119|CR-119]] and the per-concept cost of roughly $300 at agency rates in [[Creative Science#CR-024|CR-024]], and the cost case stands on its own without needing a performance case.
THE RULE THAT FOLLOWS, and it binds our own writing. Any AI-creative claim we repeat is labelled a PRODUCTION-COST claim until somebody runs the comparison. We do not write "AI creative performs better", because no source in this codex has shown it. We can write that it costs one to two orders of magnitude less to produce, because several sources have shown that.
THE TEST TO RUN ON OUR OWN ACCOUNTS, which is cheap and is the reason this claim is worth its space. Launch matched pairs into the same ad set, same message, same offer, same hook, one produced conventionally and one produced with AI, and read cost per result and share of spend. We have five accounts and a standing creative cadence, so the marginal cost is one extra asset per concept. Until that runs, the honest position is that AI creative is cheaper and that its performance relative to conventional creative is UNMEASURED, not equal and not worse.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, i tried running only AI ads for 30 days, 2026-04-29; Fraser Cottrell, The New Way to Make Ai UGC in 2026, 2026-03-15; Fraser Cottrell, Watch Me Create 3 Static Ads With AI From Scratch, 2026-04-05
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-168 · Thirty days of AI-only ads produced $2.2M in sales on just over $1M of spend, with no control period
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Both surfaces screen-shared, a sales dashboard and the ad account: "this is the results from last month specifically, $2.2 million in sales. And if we jump over into the ad account, you can see just over a million dollars to spend for the month of March." The month was run on AI-generated ads only. The two figures are T2 because they were on screen.
WHAT THE NUMBERS DO NOT SHOW, and the list is longer than the claim. The account is not named. There is no control period against non-AI creative, so nothing separates the AI production method from everything else about the account. No attribution basis is stated for the $2.2M, so it may be platform-reported, may be shop revenue, and the two rarely agree. No margin is given, so a 2.2x blended return says nothing about whether the month was profitable. And a 2.2x blend on a $1M/month spender is an unremarkable number in its own right, which means the result is equally consistent with AI creative being neutral.
The defensible reading: a $1M/month account can be run entirely on AI-produced creative without collapsing. That is a capability finding and it is worth having, because it removes the objection that AI creative cannot carry real spend. It is not an efficiency finding. Filed against the standing evidence gap in [[Creative Science#CR-169|CR-169]].
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, i tried running only AI ads for 30 days, 2026-04-29
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-172 · Are fully AI-generated statics variation-hostile? One operator keeps every word out of the generation, another decomposes the finished image back into editable layers
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
POSITION ONE, generate the image only. Fraser Cottrell generates the visual with AI and builds all text in Figma or Canva on top, because text baked into the generation cannot be iterated: "the problem there is that your text and everything is baked in, so you can't change it for variations. You have to do the whole generation again." Under this rule a headline test costs a text-layer edit, and the cost of a fully generated static is that every variation is a fresh generation with fresh reroll risk.
POSITION TWO, generate everything and decompose afterwards. Mark Builds Brands shows Canva Magic Layer taking a fully generated static apart into individually editable elements: "it'll scan your entire image and basically make every element on there dynamic. So, if you don't like one specific part of the text, the color, whatever it is, instead of going back and forth with Nano Banana Pro, you can literally just edit it right here." Shown on screen, one example, no statement of how faithfully it decomposes a hard case.
WHY THIS IS BANKED RATHER THAN RESOLVED. It is a testable disagreement about a tool capability, not a difference of wording, and it decides a production workflow we run daily. If decomposition is reliable, the fully generated static is strictly better because it keeps composition and typography inside one coherent generation. If it is not, every generated static is a dead end the moment a variation is needed. Nobody ran the comparison, and the two speakers are describing different tools on different dates, so the disagreement may already be a tooling-generation gap rather than a real one.
THE CHEAP RESOLUTION, and it takes an hour. Take one existing fully generated static, run it through the decomposition step, and change the headline. If the rebuilt image is publishable, position two wins and the constraint disappears. Until then the safe default is position one, because it fails gracefully: text kept in a layer costs nothing if decomposition later turns out to work. Relates to the variation-volume requirement in [[Creative Science#CR-024|CR-024]], which is what makes the workflow question expensive at all.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, Watch Me Create 3 Static Ads With AI From Scratch, 2026-04-05; Mark Builds Brands, i tried running only AI ads for 30 days, 2026-04-29
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-173 · AI output quality is claimed at "10 out of 10 pretty much all the time" and shown at three rerolls per image by the same operator six weeks later
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
THE MARCH CLAIM: "The great thing about where AI is right now is that pretty much all the time the output is 10 out of 10." THE APRIL POSITION, from the same speaker, with the failed generations on screen: "unfortunately, this is what happens with AI images. It is you're normally going back and forth to get exactly what you want." He runs three rounds on one image and at least two on another, all shown.
BANK THE APRIL POSITION. It carries the shown evidence and the March claim does not. The March claim is also contradicted inside its own video, where the same speaker says "you're always going to get some like weird AI hallucination stuff", so it is refuted by its author twice over. Anyone quoting the 10-out-of-10 line is quoting the weakest sentence in the corpus.
WHY THIS MATTERS BEYOND ONE SPEAKER. Reroll count is the hidden variable in every AI production-cost figure in this file. The 5-minute static and the $5 to $30 in credits per ad in [[Creative Science#CR-169|CR-169]] are stated as if the first generation lands, and the same operator's shown workflow says it takes two or three. Multiply accordingly before quoting any AI production cost, and note the same correction already exists in [[Creative Science#CR-063|CR-063]], where a more expensive video model was net cheaper because it needed fewer retries. Retries, not sticker price, are the unit that matters.
THE ADJACENT UNSUPPORTED CONFIDENCE CLAIM, recorded because it is the assumption underneath the entire AI-creative case: "I can almost guarantee you that no one will tell that they are AI." No blind test, no comment-section evidence, no survey, no A/B. It sits directly against [[Creative Science#CR-060|CR-060]], where viewers who spot the AI are said to distrust the brand, and against [[Creative Science#CR-046|CR-046]], whose whole mechanism is that animation works BECAUSE viewers know it is not real. Treat undetectability as an open question, and note that the cheapest evidence available on it is free: read the comments on your own AI ads.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, The New Way to Make Ai UGC in 2026, 2026-03-15; Fraser Cottrell, Watch Me Create 3 Static Ads With AI From Scratch, 2026-04-05
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Meta creative enhancements

### CR-115 · Meta's Creator Marketplace is a free in-Ads-Manager sourcing tool with a dedicated creator inbox, and it now covers Facebook as well as Instagram
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Shown live in Ads Manager: All tools, then under "Engage audience", Creator Marketplace. It used to be Instagram-specific and now works for Facebook too. Meta recommends accounts likely to perform for your audience, including a "creators who are expected to drive high ad performance for you" list, and contacting a creator through the marketplace routes the message to a dedicated inbox rather than the creator's general DMs, so it is far less likely to be ignored. Practitioner layer (T3): brand deals are much cheaper than advertisers assume, with roughly $800 plus free product cited as a workable deal for a small creator whose audience matches the target; brief for volume by asking for a couple of different ads plus several hook variations and different locations in one shoot, since the marginal effort to the creator is small and the extra assets extend the ad's runway; keep the brief loose because the creator's authenticity is the asset being bought, so specify the required CTA and the one benefit known to sell, then leave execution to them. Fully scripting the creator is called the wrong move. Reinforces [[Creative Science#CR-058|CR-058]] and [[Creative Science#CR-059|CR-059]] with the sourcing mechanics.
Scale figure from the same operator on 2026-03-03: "There's like 1.5 million plus creators growing all the time. You can search by category." Quoted from memory with no screenshot of the count, so T3 inside an otherwise T2 claim.
The brief format, stated as a hard rule by two more operators and with the failure mode named: bullet points and a high-level overview, never a script. "give them a list of bullet points, give them like a highlevel overview, but brief like a big brief, a big script is like not the way... the creator audience knows what they say and if you script it they will know that it's not genuine and you lose the whole value of it completely." The mechanism is specific to SEEDED and partnership ads, where the creator's own audience is part of the buy: that audience has a baseline for how the person talks, so a scripted read is detectable by exactly the people the format exists to reach. For a hired UGC actor with no following the argument is weaker, which is a boundary none of them draws.
Sources: Ben Heath, Learn 97% of Meta Ads in Under 29 Minutes, 2026-08-18; Ben Heath, Copy This Meta Ads Testing Strategy for Better Results, 2026-03-03; Professor Charley T, The BEST NEW Way to Run Facebook Ads in 2026 (Andromeda 2.0), 2026-06-13
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-068 · Which Advantage+ creative enhancements to enable
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Four operator positions. Professor Charley T runs a traffic-light system: red (text generation, enhanced CTA, 3D animation) default off because they rewrite the marketing; yellow (music, image expansion, text improvements) only under specific asset conditions, with restricted-words configured first; green (visual touch-ups, brightness/contrast, relevant comments, sitelinks) stay on because they only optimize delivery per placement. Sam Piliero's rule: disable anything that alters the media itself, keep additive overlays/sitelinks/text improvements on. Mark Builds Brands and Dr. Matt Shiver: turn every enhancement off (Shiver also clears the related-media slots because auto-attached variants destroy per-creative attribution; Mark's $1k/day case study ran with everything off). Nick Theriot: the toggles make no measurable performance difference, he runs them off. No side showed controlled data.
Empirical anchor from Meta's own defaults, SHOWN by Ben Heath in the ad UI on a video ad: Meta pre-enables only the low-risk enhancements (visual touch-ups, adapt to multi-image format) and leaves the media-altering ones such as video effects off. "Meta's only going to turn on the ones that it thinks are relatively safe... They're not going with some of the more experimental stuff, like adding video effects." That default split matches the green-light side of Charley T's system (delivery and format adaptation on, creative rewriting off) rather than the all-off position held by Shiver and Mark Builds Brands. Heath's own recommendation for beginners is to accept the defaults. Note the enhancement menu differs between image and video ads, so a rule written against one does not transfer cleanly to the other.
Fifth operator position, and the only one that converges from the all-off camp onto a specific keeper. Jeremy Haynes disables the Advantage+ creative enhancements wholesale at the ad level, including site links, with exactly one exception: the enhancement that surfaces a positive comment. Charley T does not dispute it in the reaction. That single keeper sits inside Charley's green light (relevant comments), which makes the practical consensus across five operators narrower than the debate reads: media-altering enhancements off, comment surfacing on, everything else contested.
Piliero restated his rule on 2026-07-05 with both lists filled in, which makes it the most enforceable version in this claim. Rejected because they alter the asset: visual touch-ups, ad music, generated backgrounds, ad animations, image touch-ups. Accepted because they only alter what surrounds the asset: overlays, spotlights, text improvements, reveal details over time. "If it changes or modifies the image or the ad, we do not select it. If it changes things around the image or the ad, we're comfortable with that." Note he now rejects visual touch-ups, which Meta pre-enables by default and which sits in Charley T's green light, so the three positions disagree on that one toggle specifically. Separate line from the same session: Meta's in-platform image generation is not run on live ads at all as of July 2026, "although it is getting pretty good", a judgement across roughly 100 brands with no comparison shown.
Ben Heath's per-toggle walkthrough, 2026-03-24, is the most complete list anyone gives and it sits between Charley T's traffic lights and Piliero's alter-versus-surround rule. It also splits by CONDITION rather than by toggle, which is why it is worth carrying in full. Enhanced media text (beta), which replaces text inside your media with AI-generated text, goes OFF whenever the in-image text is meaning-critical, testimonials in particular, "it could totally distort the meaning... these other options with the testimonials absolutely can't be"; consider it on for a simple one-benefit overlay or a stated offer. Add overlays goes OFF when the image already carries text and ON when it carries none, because overlaying Meta's text on yours "can easily get very messy and confusing". Add animation goes OFF for text-heavy images and ON for a product or demonstration image. Add video effects goes OFF when the edit is already graded bright and punchy on purpose and ON when the footage is dull. Enhanced CTA goes ON, because the generated phrase list is reviewable and editable before the ad runs and the non-default phrasing stands out against standard buttons. Visual touch-ups stay ON for image and video, and his reason is placement proliferation rather than image quality: with Threads and 4:5 in the mix, square, vertical and horizontal no longer describe the delivery surface, so Meta needs room to adjust ([[Creative Science#CR-056|CR-056]] carries the production rule that makes this safe). Flex media stays ON, which lets Meta serve the file you built for one ratio into a placement that usually takes another. Music is a small lever, defaults on, and must be auditioned because it lands off-brand, "Super corporate, right?", and he rates it low overall since most impressions are consumed without sound. Structural note from the same session: the option set differs by format, video gets far fewer options than image, and simpler videos get more options than complex ones, because Meta can only alter what it can alter without breaking the frame. So a rule written against an image ad does not transfer to a video ad.
Two operating facts that decide whether any of the above holds, both from Matt Shiver on 2026-06-30, both shown on screen. First, enhancements RE-ENABLE THEMSELVES after publish: "I've seen so many times where I launch an ad and then the creative enhancements just turn back on again... They publish it and then a couple days later for some reason things turn on." Confirmed across his own and client accounts. Second, there is an account-level master switch: Advertising settings, creative feature, "test new features", ON by default, and turning it off is intended to stop future creative-feature testing on the account. Meta warns on the way out that it may affect running campaigns, so his own caution is not to flip it on a high-spend healthy account. Anyone holding an all-off position needs the master switch, because per-ad toggles do not stay where you put them.
The full surface list at ad level, read off the screen in the same session, so the audit has something to check against: creative setup (site links, website highlights, products and services), media, related media, Advantage+ creative image generation, enhanced CTA, related comments, related details. Shiver keeps enhanced CTA on and often related comments, turns the creative-setup group off for lead gen and local, and keeps Advantage+ image generation off for lead generation while allowing it might work for ecommerce if every generated variant is reviewed. Related media is a separate problem and is banked at [[Creative Science#CR-161|CR-161]]. Note the convergence: enhanced CTA plus comment surfacing is now the keeper set for Heath, Shiver and Haynes independently, which is a narrower consensus than the four-way disagreement at the top of this claim implies.
Shiver's all-off position dated earlier, 2026-03-24, and it is BROADER than the enhancement menu, which is the part worth carrying. Toggled off live on screen in one pass: multi-advertiser ads, dynamic creative, and every Advantage+ creative enhancement including text improvements, overlays, image rotation and translation. Two of those three are not creative enhancements at all, so his position is really "let nothing outside my build touch the ad", and multi-advertiser ads in particular appears nowhere else in this claim. The judgement is ASSERTED throughout; he shows the switches, not a result. Read with [[Creative Science#CR-161|CR-161]], since related media is on by default and is the same failure shape, and with the re-enable-after-publish finding above, which is what makes a blanket position require the account-level master switch rather than a per-ad sweep.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The NEW BEST Creative Enhancements Guide for Meta Ads, 2026-07-25; Sam Piliero, The Only Facebook Ads Video You Need in 2026, 2026-07-26; Mark Builds Brands, ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days, 2026-07-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Run Facebook Ads for Coaches & Agency Owners, 2026-08-04; Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; Ben Heath, The BEST Instagram Ads Tutorial for Beginners in 2026, 2026-04-28; Professor Charley T, Meta Ads expert reacts to $1M/month Strategy, 2026-02-14; Sam Piliero, The BEST Facebook Ads Campaign Structure for 2026, 2026-07-05; Ben Heath, How To Use Creative Enhancements on Your Facebook Ads, 2026-03-24; Dr. Matt Shiver, New Facebook Ads Features & Changes (July 2026), 2026-06-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, Facebook Ads Tutorial for Beginners Updated for 2026, 2026-03-24
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-069 · Enhancement usage should be inversely proportional to your confidence: AI enhancements make every ad roughly a C+ ad
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Exception to the red-light rule: activate text generation plus AI image tools when a brand-new account has nothing working, or when positioning a new offer, effectively asking Meta's cross-niche buyer data to find the direction. Works especially well for print-on-demand and mom-and-pop shops running bare product images. The more confident and focused the creative test, the fewer machine variations should be allowed.
The same operator generalises this from creative enhancements to Meta's whole product roadmap: "almost every new ad product coming out of Facebook is not for people that are already successful. It's for helping people be successful at the start." He claims it partly from having seen unreleased AI ad products under NDA, which he will not detail, so that basis is unverifiable and unfalsifiable as stated. It is still usable as a default posture when a new Meta feature ships: assume it raises the floor rather than the ceiling, and test it only if the account currently sits below that floor.
Contested on the default posture toward any new Meta feature, and both sides are folklore. Blue Sense argues the opposite of the floor-raising read: "anytime Meta puts a new feature into the platforms, they'll always prioritize it because they want to get data on whether it works. And so, when there is a new feature, there generally is a little bit of delta in there in going and trying it", and says that window has now closed for flexible ads. FLAG THIS AS FOLKLORE. No evidence is offered, no delivery data, no Meta statement, and the claim is unfalsifiable as stated because the advantage is always said to have already expired. Charley T's version is equally unsupported, resting partly on unreleased products he saw under NDA and will not describe. So the codex holds two opposing default postures on new Meta features, neither with data: adopt early for a temporary delivery premium, or ignore unless the account sits below the floor. It is cheaply testable by launching a matched pair into a new surface and an established one and reading share of spend. Until someone does, treat a new Meta feature as neutral and test it on its own merits. The same folklore recurs in MD as a general delivery-priority claim; recorded here only in its creative-feature-adoption form.
Sources: Professor Charley T, The NEW BEST Creative Enhancements Guide for Meta Ads, 2026-07-25; Professor Charley T, Meta advertisers: We've got a big problem, 2026-01-17; Blue Sense Digital, Why We Turned OFF Flexible Ads & Cost Caps in 2026, 2026-02-25
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-161 · "Related media" silently injects other creatives from your own ad account into a running ad, which invalidates creative tests; the spend split is visible under Breakdown, related media
Tier: T2 · Status: active
