Shown in Ads Manager, 2026-06-30. Related media is an ad-level enhancement that is on by default and pulls OTHER creatives already sitting in your ad account into the ad you just built. "it's looking at the account and it's going, all right, these ads also could work well, like the videos, the images, the carousels, could already work well with your existing ad. We're just going to lump them in there." Shiver reports four or five creatives being auto-added to a single ad on upload. The reporting dimension exists: Breakdown, then type related media, and it splits spend into original media, meaning what you uploaded, versus related media, meaning what Meta added.
Why it matters more than the other enhancements. Every other toggle alters how your asset is presented. This one changes WHICH ASSET IS RUNNING, so a creative test can be won by a creative that was never in the test: "you could say, 'Wow, this ad is crushing it.' And in reality, you actually have related media turned on and the related media, which are not the ads that you originally put in there, are getting most of the spend." His recommendation is to turn it off on every ad. Two consequences beyond his own verdict. Any per-ad performance read taken since this shipped is suspect unless the breakdown was checked, which includes ad-count and hit-rate figures ([[Creative Science#CR-118|CR-118]]) and anything resting on spend as a creative-quality proxy ([[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]]). And it is a second mechanism, alongside near-duplicate grouping, by which the ad you think you are measuring is not the ad delivering, which is the open question at MD-003.
Related surface from the same session, also on by default and also worth an audit rather than a verdict: "related details" renders an email capture form inside the ad unit. Shiver reports submissions never reaching him and producing no notification, and engagement with the form pulling spend. A client noticed it on his ads before he did. That account is his own experience and unverified, so T3 inside an otherwise T2 claim; the rendered form itself is shown on screen. Tier note: the source batch filed this T1. Held at T2 to match this file's convention, where T1 is Meta documentation and a surface shown live in Ads Manager is T2 (see [[Creative Science#CR-113|CR-113]], [[Creative Science#CR-056|CR-056]], [[Creative Science#CR-130|CR-130]]).
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, New Facebook Ads Features & Changes (July 2026), 2026-06-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-130 · Meta takes up to five primary texts and five headlines per ad plus its own AI variants, and "optimize text per person" can swap a headline into the primary text slot
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Both SHOWN in the ad build UI. The field limit reads "one of five" on the primary text selector, and Meta auto-generates persona-labelled AI variants from your first input which can be edited before acceptance. Meta then tests the filled slots against each other, so shipping one of each forfeits free optimisation. Practical rule: fill multiple slots with on-brand variants and delete the off-brand AI ones rather than accepting all of them.
"Optimize text per person" is enabled by default and Heath recommends leaving it on. It means Meta can surface your headline in the primary text position and vice versa. The consequence for copywriters is structural: a headline written as a fragment that only resolves after the primary text can be served as the primary text, so each field must stand alone in either position. Neither the field limit nor the swap behaviour was recorded anywhere else in this file; both sit adjacent to the flexible-ads debate in [[Creative Science#CR-042|CR-042]] and [[Creative Science#CR-043|CR-043]] but are a separate surface.
Three field constraints from Charley T that change how the copy is written, all asserted by a practitioner with no documentation cited, so verify in the UI before designing around them. Headline caps at 40 characters AND does not render on Instagram placements at all, so an ad whose argument sits in the headline loses it across roughly half the places it serves; the headline's job narrows to connecting the creative to the offer and driving the click. Primary text truncates at 125 characters before "see more", which he treats as a structural device rather than a limit: the first 125 characters are the hello and everything below the fold is the close, so any detail that might repel a marginal reader belongs below the fold where only an already-interested reader reaches it. He also reports using below-the-fold copy to link a free Facebook group, credited with building communities of over half a million people.
Scan-path model attached to the same section, T4 and unsupported by eye-tracking: creative first, then down to the headline, then back up to the primary text, which makes primary text the connective tissue and the preview of the landing page. His continuity claim is that when the primary text matches what the visitor then reads on the page, the click converts to add-to-cart more often, which is the copy-field version of the congruence rule in [[Creative Science#CR-086|CR-086]].
Field usage at volume, from Jeremy Haynes: five body copies and five headlines written broad enough to apply across the board, reused on EVERY creative rather than written per ad, giving 25 copy permutations per ad. Charley T grants this is the only genuinely scientific element of that setup, because a shared copy set across all creatives permits a multivariate read of whether the same copy wins everywhere, and notes no such read was ever presented.
The floor and the quality gate on those slots, from Sam Piliero across roughly 100 brands: a minimum of 2 and preferably 3 genuinely distinct primary texts plus 3 headlines and 1 optional description, and the variants must be different in substance or they produce no signal at all. "buy my shoes and buy my shoes today are the same thing. Instead, we want A to be very unique from B and so on. This will allow us to generate signal to understand what's better." Filling five slots with paraphrases raises the variant count and teaches the system nothing, which is the copy-field version of the near-duplicate problem in [[Creative Science#CR-034|CR-034]]. Practitioner standard, no test data. Field default from the same session: Shop Now is the CTA button on about 80% of their brands, with Get Offer reserved for sales and promos and Learn More for clicks that have to read something before converting. A share recalled from memory, nothing shown.
The third slot is DESCRIPTIONS and it takes five as well, confirmed by the same operator on a second date and read off the same surface: "if we click into primary text, you can see we have one of five. So we can have up to five different primary text variations within this one ad. We can do the same for headlines. Click in there, you see one of five. And do the same for descriptions." That makes 5 x 5 x 5 of copy inside ONE ad unit, and it is the fact that settles a large part of the ad-count argument: copy variation never consumes an ad slot, so any live-ad count inflated by headline permutations was never counting ads ([[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]). Heath's rule follows directly, and it is a division of labour rather than a preference: "much better to do it within these text variations and have that copy testing done within an ad unit as opposed to separate ads", because "for each ad creative you needed three or four different ads to test different headline variations, you very quickly get into just silly numbers of ads and too difficult for you as the advertiser to be able to monitor that." Separate ads carry different CREATIVE. The ad unit carries different COPY.
The placement fact that makes "optimize text per person" more than a nicety: primary text renders ABOVE the image on Facebook mobile feed and BELOW it on Instagram. So the reading order of your copy relative to the creative is already different across placements before Meta moves anything, which is Heath's stated reason for leaving text improvements on and a second reason every field must stand alone.
CTA copy, from a Google-side team but the constraint is identical: state the RESULT the buyer receives, never the mechanical action. "Don't put learn more anywhere. It is time for us to say a final farewell to our learns, our submits, and our click here. If you want someone to take action, tell them what they're getting." Their replacements are all outcome phrases, get your quote, see pricing ranges, compare options, get a quote in 24 hours, shop wholesale sets, get the sample kit. Note this collides with Piliero's field default of Shop Now on about 80% of brands and Learn More for clicks that must read something first. The collision is smaller than it looks: Meta's CTA button is a fixed picklist, so the outcome phrasing has to live in the enhanced-CTA phrases, the headline or the description rather than on the button. T4, asserted, no test.
Sources: Ben Heath, The BEST Instagram Ads Tutorial for Beginners in 2026, 2026-04-28; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026 (Full Course Post-Andromeda), 2026-04-09; Professor Charley T, Meta Ads expert reacts to $1M/month Strategy, 2026-02-14; Sam Piliero, The BEST Facebook Ads Campaign Structure for 2026, 2026-07-05; Ben Heath, The BEST Facebook Ad Campaign Structure for 2026, 2026-02-17; Ben Heath, How To Use Creative Enhancements on Your Facebook Ads, 2026-03-24; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

## Ad craft, offers and retargeting creative

### CR-070 · A converting e-commerce ad does four jobs in order: call out the avatar, educate on the problem, position the mechanism as the unique solution, deliver an urgent risk-limited offer
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The avatar callout creates "this is talking to me"; problem education makes the pain feel real; the mechanism must be framed as the unique solution, not a generic one; the offer is a fair value exchange with urgency and limited risk so the purchase happens in-session. Presented as the operating template for Andromeda-era e-commerce ads, anchored to a supplement brand that sold for $1B running distinct avatar angles in one account.
Sources: Sam Piliero, The Only Facebook Ads Video You Need in 2026, 2026-07-26
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-071 · Every line must pass the so-what test: lead with the benefit/outcome, justify with the feature, never the reverse
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Pattern: "firmer skin in 4 weeks using a triple peptide complex", not "triple peptide complex". A pain-led or benefit-led headline beats a feature-led one and is one of the few things worth split-testing because it is above the fold. Same rule in video: say "you'll sleep better because it has magnesium", not "contains magnesium"; a feature dump is dead weight unless tied to a differentiating so-what.
He names this the number one biggest mistake in creative and gives the reason features cannot carry a cold audience: any competitor can state the same ones. Quick-drying tech, unmatched quality, reliable performance, grass-fed, sugar-free, clean ingredients. Placement rule rather than deletion rule: the winning fashion ad in his review set still uses its features and puts them at the END, after desire has been built, while both failed ads open on features into cold traffic. Second-order trap: a buyer cannot evaluate an ingredient they do not know, so an unexplained ingredient such as taurine reads as a risk rather than a benefit.
The conversion method that turns a feature into an angle is to find the physiological consequence the buyer already feels. Given a jerky brand listing grass-fed and no processed ingredients: grass-fed means higher omega-3, omega-3 maps to brain health, no carbs maps to no crash, so the ad opens on feeling sluggish and crashing after morning porridge, agitates it, and sells cognitive steadiness. He immediately flags the open question, how many people eat carbs in the morning, feel a crash, and would switch to jerky, and answers it correctly: that uncertainty is why you build multiple angles off one feature rather than one.
Nick Theriot supplies the ladder that makes the so-what test mechanical, with four named rungs instead of two: feature, then performance, then benefit, then desired state. His worked example is the iPod, 1GB, holds 1,000 songs, 1,000 songs in your pocket, freedom to move around with your music. The ad is written at the DESIRED STATE rung and everything below it is justification, which is the same instruction as the benefit-first rule stated as a climb rather than a swap. He adds the half most copy briefs drop: the desired state is carried by what the ad SHOWS as much as by what it says, and the iPod campaigns communicated it visually rather than stating it. Historic product, no live data, so the ladder is a drafting tool rather than a finding. Cheapest use: take any feature currently sitting in an ad and force it up all four rungs before writing the line.
Two enforceable drafting rules from a B2B search team, both T4 and both narrower than the principle above, which is what makes them usable. Sentence shape: "you get [outcome] when [mechanism]", one sentence carrying one promise, and the only named way to break it is stuffing a compound sentence in. Word choice: replace category adjectives with measurable units, and they name the classes, "you want to use time, you want to use volume, you want to use range, you want to use SLA, error rate, setup time, payback window", against "better up times", which every competitor also writes and which costs character count for nothing. This is the specificity criterion in [[Creative Science#CR-123|CR-123]] turned into a substitution list. Asserted for short-form conversion copy, no test.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026, 2026-08-10; Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale, 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-072 · Objection-facing ads function as middle-of-funnel creative and can out-ROAS the rest of the account
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The Black Stuff soap ad was built to surface and knock down known natural-soap objections (no lather without chemicals, tight dry skin after) and call out the researched buyer subset. It got a 28% hook rate and over 2.1 ROAS, higher than other videos in the account, attributed to sitting MOF where viewers already know the brand. Deciding an ad's funnel position before making it is presented as a required step.
Blue Sense adds a method for generating the objection set instead of guessing it, stated across two videos. Size the portfolio to the non-buyers: at a 5% site conversion rate, 95% did not buy, and he splits that into roughly 20% price, 20% brand distrust, 20% quality doubt, 20% not ready yet. The percentages are illustrative, not measured, and getting the real mix requires actually surveying why people did not buy. Treatments map one to one. Price gets discounts, value anchoring and cost-per-use framing, with the supplement-versus-daily-coffee comparison called out as a bottom-funnel winner. Distrust gets review counts, customer counts and UGC testimonials. Quality doubt gets product-in-use UGC, before/afters and demonstrations. Not-ready gets educational and lifestyle content for presence. The mix of bottom-funnel assets should mirror the objection mix.
Top-of-funnel variant of the same move, which is stronger because it runs BEFORE the product exists in the ad: kill the competing solutions on camera first. A tonsil-stone VSL lists what the wellness category pushes, tongue scrapers, oil pulling, chlorophyll water, activated charcoal, plus manual squeezing, salt water and apple cider vinegar gargles, and explains why each one fails on mechanism. The reason it lands as recognition rather than as a pitch is that a sufferer has already researched and already knows those options. Every alternative is dead before the product is introduced. He prescribes the same for an electrolyte brand against Gatorade and Powerade, and for a caffeine gum against both coffee and matcha. This is the executable version of the failed-solutions archetype in [[Creative Science#CR-015|CR-015]], and it pairs with the delayed product introduction in [[Creative Science#CR-127|CR-127]].
The PRICE objection is the one with a measured downstream effect attached, and moving it into the ad fixed a page the team could not fix on the page. On a high-priced product with a weak site conversion rate, Charley T's guest surfaced buy-now-pay-later availability in mid-funnel creative, before the click: "we essentially started addressing price objections in the funnel... what this did was we raised the conversion rate without doing any extra work on the website because people were aware that they could use buy now pay later options." Direction only, no figures, but the mechanism is worth generalising: a landing-page conversion problem can sometimes be a pre-click information problem, and the cheapest CRO test is putting the missing fact in the ad. Run this before rebuilding a page.
Two construction rules for the objection line itself. Name the friction in the buyer's own words and resolve it in the same sentence rather than arguing it away, the worked example being "already using HubSpot and Salesforce, keep your stack": "Name the friction. Name the pain point. Name the problem. And then show how you've already thought about that." And the Q&A format is the ready-made vehicle: an off-camera interviewer reads a real client FAQ, answered on camera, closing on the CTA, with the question narrow to the ICP rather than mass market. Matt Shiver's demonstrated question is "Hey, why is Meta reporting a 5x ROAS when my P&L says we're flat?", which qualifies the viewer and demonstrates expertise in the same beat. Both asserted, no data.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16; Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Professor Charley T, Copy This Facebook Ads Strategy (Post-Andromeda), 2026-02-07; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-073 · A back-in-stock scarcity hook retargets prior site visitors through creative signals alone and produces top-of-account hook rates
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The Nuve ad opens "Everyone's been asking when this tiny device is coming back... back in stock", manufacturing scarcity even for viewers who never checked availability and signaling Meta to serve it to previous visitors. It hit a 35% hook rate (high for the account), 2.2% CTR, and strong ROAS. The script then re-pitches the product because MOF viewers have forgotten the details.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-074 · Seed repeat-purchase and gifting angles year-round in ads for one-time-purchase products
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The Nuve ad drops "apparently all of you have been buying extras for your entire family" to plant multi-buy. A one-time-purchase product still has gifting and multi-household demand (birthdays, anniversaries), so planting the idea early extends LTV instead of waiting for Q4 gifting campaigns.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, How to Make Meta Ads So Good People Can't Stop Watching, 2026-08-16
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-075 · One always-on evergreen offer as the headline of most creative makes high-volume testing consistent
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Brands like The Black Stuff keep one evergreen offer permanently on and headline it across most creative. Holding the offer constant isolates the creative variable, making it easy to test lots of creative with consistent results, paired with a small set of seasonal offers rather than constant offer churn.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, Creative Volume, 2026-08-02
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-076 · Full price transparency in ads, VSL and application form filters unqualified leads and cuts no-shows for a trusted brand
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The Blueprint (Unspeakable/Nathan) YouTube coaching offer states the price in ads at times and always in the VSL and typeform. Prospects arrive pre-sold with no surprise objection, so the calendar fills with people who can invest and no-show load drops; called their single best-performing ad strategy. Caveat: it works because the brand already carries large public trust; a new B2C offer may lose too much volume showing price up front.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Actually Grow a YouTube Channel (he has 3.5M subs), 2026-08-06
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-077 · A retargeting ad set needs 10+ concurrent ads across different pain points AND formats; refresh by injecting new ads, not rebuilding
Tier: T2 · Status: active
With a small warm pool and a deep event, Meta must work through all ads in sequence; some people respond to statics, some to b-roll, some to video, so format diversity matters as much as angle diversity. When ad-set cost per call rises, kill only the ad soaking most spend and inject fresh ads on a rolling basis. Shown live: a new ad took most of the last 7 days' spend at a $118 cost per call inside an ad set whose $14K spend produced $60-70K revenue.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, The 'Right Way' to Run Facebook Retargeting Ads in 2026, 2026-07-28
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-078 · Retargeting creative skips education: warm audiences need a direct avatar callout plus the offer, sent to a short application page
Tier: T3 · Status: active
People who already follow you need an offer, not trust-building; webinar and free-training funnels are for problem-aware cold traffic. His winning retargeting static is a Canva image: callout ("I'm looking for 5 coaches who want to scale to 100k/month in 2026 with paid ads") plus the offer ("we will script, edit and launch your ads in 14 days") over a 5-question application page with no video. Primary text runs PAS in three lengths matched to the same angle; headline is the bare offer with risk reversal.
The same three-part static is now his best cost per call on COLD traffic as well, which widens the format past retargeting: "it's really three parts. Who's it for? Offer, CTA. And it just works, right? I'm getting the best call cost from this right now." Production spec added: build it over a photo with a dark gradient overlay at the bottom so the text pops. He also retracts his own prior objection to the callout, which is the useful part for anyone in a sophisticated category: "I used to think that these looking for five ads just didn't work and I was like, 'Everyone knows that we're taking more than five people on.' And, you know, they just work." Sits against the persona-callout objection in [[Creative Science#CR-008|CR-008]], and the reconcilable read is that a callout paired with a concrete offer is a qualification device while a callout standing alone is a weak hook. No numbers shown either way.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, The 'Right Way' to Run Facebook Retargeting Ads in 2026, 2026-07-28; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-079 · Deceptive black-hat claims outperform compliant ads (1.5-2x vs a best of 1.0-1.3x ROAS on the same brand), but carry clawback exposure
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A client hired the agency to convert fabricated-claim ads into compliant ones. Lying earned 1.5-2x ROAS against a ~1.5x breakeven; after 3 months the best compliant result was 1.0-1.3x. His conclusion: ethical marketing usually cannot match black-hat performance, but courts can claw back 100% of revenue (a $10M-revenue, $2M-profit brand can owe the full $10M), while compliant ads can run indefinitely.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads, 2026-08-14
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-080 · Products without a unique mechanism or marketplace superiority are near-unscalable on Meta regardless of creative quality
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Hard disqualifier across his failed clients: if the same product is buyable cheaper on Amazon and nothing justifies the premium, there is nothing for the ad to dramatize and the account does not scale. "All the creative and copy and tweaks and manipulation of people simply doesn't help. It's still a pig at the end of the day."
The scripting error that manufactures this problem in a product that does not have it: never equate the product with its generic category. The Hyro ad says "if you drink electrolytes like Hyro", which collapses the brand into the ingredient, and once the viewer accepts the product is just electrolytes the cheapest supplier wins or they put table salt in a glass of water. The fix is to position against alternatives on a spec the buyer can check, in that case the sodium, magnesium and potassium ratio that maximises absorption, and the sugar load in Gatorade and Powerade. Production note from the same review: demonstrate the education beat with plain salt on the table rather than with the branded product, so the mechanism reads as information rather than as a pitch.
Ezra Firestone frames the upstream version, and it sits in tension with this claim rather than agreeing with it: "the best product doesn't win in the marketplace, the best promise wins. The job of the product is to live up to the promise that you made." He splits a business into product, promise, and support plus operations, with promise winning new customers and product retaining them. Read together with the disqualifier above, the workable version is that the promise wins the acquisition and the product decides whether the acquisition was worth making, so a commodity with a great promise can acquire cheaply and still fail on repeat. Asserted from 20 years of operating, no data shown.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I spend $100k a day on Facebook Ads, 2026-08-14; Simple Creative Testing Framework, 2026-08-12; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Andrew Faris, Ezra Firestone: Nothing Has Changed In Ecom In 20 Years, 2026-05-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-081 · In saturated skeptical markets, deliver the new mechanism in raw unedited UGC because polish reads as manufactured
Tier: T3 · Status: active
For a heavily saturated category (women's hair growth) the ad must present a revolutionary new system differentiated on speed, absence of hated side effects, price, or guarantee plus social proof. Raw unedited UGC of women with great hair tends to work best precisely because the market is so skeptical that produced-looking ads underperform.
Sources: Nick Theriot, NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-127 · Introduce the product as late as possible in a top-of-funnel video; pulling the introduction earlier to beat the retention dropoff lowers total purchases
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The named error is reading the retention curve, seeing 80% dropoff by the 90-second mark, and moving the product introduction earlier so more viewers hear it. That trades qualified intent for raw exposure. A good top-of-funnel ad walks the viewer through the awareness stages inside the asset (unaware to problem aware to solution aware to product aware) before naming the product, which is why VSLs work. The smaller group that reaches a late product introduction converts far better, so absolute purchase volume out of the asset is higher despite fewer people seeing the product.
The related failure is the bridge. A perfect hook, body and CTA are all wasted if the hook-to-body transition jumps straight to "product X is your solution", because that produces a step change in perceived sales pressure instead of a gradient. Stated identically in two videos, asserted, no retention-versus-conversion comparison shown. Same direction as [[Creative Science#CR-021|CR-021]] and [[Creative Science#CR-022|CR-022]], which delay the product intro on awareness-stage grounds.
The model that makes this auditable, stated 2026-01-29: plot PRODUCT AWARENESS, meaning the viewer's sense that they are being sold to, against seconds. The target shape starts at zero through the hook and creeps up until the CTA. The failure shape is a staircase, awareness jumping at the product mention, jumping again at the bridge, jumping again into the CTA, and each jump is where retention breaks. "You want to slowly bleed it into the ad so that the consumer doesn't realize that they've been moved through this process." An ad that names the product early then returns to education produces a bump rather than a step and is an acceptable middle case.
Concrete timings from graded ads: hold the product out about 20 seconds on a top-of-funnel video, and up to two minutes in a VSL. In a winning fashion ad the product is displayed and explained but never SOLD for the first 20 seconds, so it reads as ordinary content. In a tonsil-stone VSL the viewer is 90 seconds in and still does not know what is being sold, which he names as a reason the asset scales; it had been running about a month with 407 likes and multiple hooks over one body. The counter-case is an ad naming the product in the first sentence, with no likes and no comments: "anyone that watches this knows immediately within 1 to 2 seconds that they're being sold to", which converts it into a warm-audience asset by default, because the only people who will watch are existing customers or people already deciding to buy. Prescribed fix for that ad was to pull the three benefits stated at the one-minute mark to the front and build three separate ads from them.
Second bridge failure, and he says it is the most common among writers who can already handle the parts: the join from body into CTA. Going straight from an education segment to "book a call below" gives an educated viewer no reason to act now, and the damage shows on the retention curve at the END of the video rather than at the start. Cheap retention device for a bridge sentence carrying no information payload: keep it visually alive. In one whitelisted ad the presenter picks up a sponge and moves it around the table during the single bridging sentence, doing nothing with it, and the same principle applies to cutting to a supermarket pan or a whiteboard mid-sentence, which makes an identical script materially more likely to hold.
Cheap fix for the CTA bridge on an ad that is deliberately organic-looking, and note it was proposed for someone else's ad and never run, so T4: instead of a spoken CTA that breaks the register, append a 3 to 5 second black end card carrying the offer in text. "instead of a verbal CTA at the end, just like probably like after the scene ends, a 3 to 5 second like a black screen and then just like with a offer on the screen in text." It keeps the body inside the disguise principle in [[Creative Science#CR-137|CR-137]] while still making the ask, and it is testable in an afternoon on any existing asset.
THE VOLUME SPLIT THAT FOLLOWS, offered by the same shop as a starting point to flex per account: 80% of creative volume at unaware plus problem aware, 10% solution aware, 10% product aware plus most aware. It is a number, so it is bankable, but nothing in the episode measures it and no account is named. Two related lines from the same source. A VSL-style ad that walks a viewer through all five awareness stages inside ONE asset is "some of the most scalable ads that I've seen in accounts", which is the format-level version of the argument at the top of this claim. And a static can spend a quarter of a million dollars at the unaware and problem-aware stages provided the primary text carries the load, which removes the reflex that upper-funnel work requires video. All ASSERTED, no account data anywhere in that episode. The static half is consistent with the single-static-$500k lifetime figure in [[Creative Science#CR-048|CR-048]] and with the long-form-copy static there.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, How to Write Ad Hooks That Scale, 2026-01-29; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16; Blue Sense Digital, How To Make Your Meta Ads Scale, 2026-04-09
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-129 · Legacy retail and fashion accounts run about 90/10 desire-led versus pain-led creative; the target mix is 60/40 to 70/30 toward pain
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The cause is the asset base. Campaign shoots do exactly one job, build desire through models and aspirational framing, and carry no pain-point content, so any account fed by them is structurally overweight desire. His position is that the best-performing performance-marketing assets sit on pain, which makes the desire-heavy mix a handicap for any brand whose growth now depends on paid acquisition. The ratio itself is the auditable diagnostic: look at the current live creative mix through the desire/pain lens and rebalance production briefs, not budgets. He frames the desire share as the branding-driven portion of the portfolio, so this is a mix rule rather than an argument to eliminate brand assets. The fashion version of the same number is stated separately: fashion brands run about 90/10 and the best accounts sit at 30-40% pain-point.
Named fashion pain-point hooks: "I never know what to wear to [occasion]", and for gifting, "my partner is impossible to buy for", both opening on the problem then moving down the awareness stages into the sell. The brand objection (pain-point content is off-brand) is handled by running it as partnership ads through the creator's handle so it never appears on the brand feed. Angle construction should accentuate a product feature or a specific customer situation (work to date night without changing) rather than only aspiration, which is also what makes the concept matrix work: angles x products x personas generates thousands of concepts. Stated across three videos by the same operator, asserted, no split shown. Consistent with [[Creative Science#CR-100|CR-100]], which says campaign-shoot assets only look good on pre-existing brand equity.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Scale an eCommerce Brand Profitably in 2026: The Full System, 2026-06-15; Blue Sense Digital, Meta Ads Creative Strategy in 2026: The Full System, 2026-05-11; Blue Sense Digital, Why Most Fashion Brands Are Running Paid Media Wrong, 2026-05-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-138 · Cap ad copy at an eighth-grade reading level, target fifth grade
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Run as an explicit step in copy chiefing rather than a preference: after drafting, reduce the reading level before shipping. "Bring your copy down to a fifth grade reading level. Or if you want to be even a little bit extra, an eighth-grade reading level. I probably would not ever go beyond an eighth-grade reading level." The stated failure mode is copywriters trying to impress with technique, producing copy that is technically understandable but does not land emotionally, and he claims plain direct copy routinely beats clever "direct response on steroids" copy. Untested assertion, but specific and enforceable, which most clarity advice is not: run a readability check and reject anything above grade 8.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-140 · The emotional-delta open: meet the reader at their current state, lift them to the solved state, then drop them back
Tier: T4 · Status: active
A three-beat structural rule for a lead or VSL open, distinct from the awareness-stage rules. "Meet them where they are, bring them up that high level, and then bring them back down to their original level. The reason that you bring them back down is simply because your goal ultimately is to create the highest what I call emotional delta." The mechanism given is contrast by experience: being broke the second time hurts more than the first, because you now know what the alternative feels like, and the copy manufactures that asymmetry deliberately. The named failure mode is leaving the reader in their current state for the whole ad, which produces no aspiration and no action. Reasoning only, no test. The consciousness-calibration framing he wraps it in (David Hawkins) is unfalsifiable and is dropped here; the three-beat structure survives without it and is testable against a straight problem-agitate-solve open.
The compressed version of the same contrast device, for a line rather than a lead: "instead of X, do Y. Right now, you're at point A. Have you heard of point B." It makes the value legible in fewer words than a before-and-after sequence, and the named failure mode is the one that matters, "you don't want to dunk on your buyer. You don't want to be too gimmicky." Contrast without contempt. T4, asserted, no test, and it is the same constraint our own house rule applies from the writing side.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-141 · Copy optimises for sales and content optimises for engagement; VSLs written as content bombed in repeated tests
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The two sit at opposite ends of a direct-response-to-brand spectrum and the skills do not transfer. Content optimises for engagement, likes, clicks and views. Copy optimises for sales. Supporting evidence is secondhand and unquantified: a named copywriter (Peter Kell) tested content-style VSLs multiple times and "every single one of them bombed", with no numbers shown. Second observation offered: operators with under 10,000 combined followers out-earn much larger audiences on copy alone. Two operator rules follow. Do not hire the person who is best at getting attention to write the asset whose job is conversion. Do not judge a conversion ad by content metrics, which is the same conclusion [[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]] and [[Creative Science#CR-018|CR-018]] reach from the metrics side. Tiered T3 because it reports someone else's repeated tests without data; treat as directional.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21
Last touched: 2026-08-18

## Seasonality and offer timing

### CR-082 · Riding a desire's seasonal intensity peak took an account from ~$700/day to $21k/day profit in 11 days; gift demand spikes 2-3 weeks pre-holiday and permits ~10x spend at LOWER cost per purchase
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Shown in-account: June 5 the account spent ~$4k/day for ~$11k/day revenue and ~$700/day profit (~3x ROAS). Repositioning one product as a Father's Day gift into the pre-holiday spike: June 14 hit $7k spend / $33k sales / $10k profit; June 15 $8.2k spend / $41k revenue (~5x); June 16 $10k spend / $59-60k revenue / $21k profit at 5.47x ROAS, with the product's spend up roughly 10x at a lower cost per purchase. Same three CBO campaigns throughout; the change was timing the desire's peak intensity window.
Sources: Nick Theriot, $30k days with Facebook Ads using Emotional Intensity, 2026-07-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-083 · Even evergreen desires have intensity seasonality; check current intensity before targeting a desire
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Weight-loss desire peaks in January from post-holiday contrast and troughs mid-summer because time-to-result exceeds the remaining occasion window (the beach-trip pain is real but buyers know they cannot lose the weight in time, so emotion does not convert to action). Operating rule: a desire that fails now may work in three months; verify intensity before committing creative to it.
The free check that turns this from intuition into a diagnostic: pull the Google Trends curve for the angle's search term before blaming delivery. On one of Faris's accounts an angle's search volume rose from spring into summer and fell from August into September, and the account's spend on a manual bid tailed off at the SAME percentage curve as the Trends line. The angle read as a health angle, but the seasonal driver turned out to be wanting to look good in a bathing suit, not New Year health resolutions, which is a different calendar entirely. Two consequences he draws: build the creative library for an angle starting in January so it is ready when demand rises in March and April, and stop hammering an angle in its off-season. Run this before diagnosing fatigue ([[Creative Science#CR-027|CR-027]]), because a demand-shaped decline and a delivery-shaped decline look identical in Ads Manager.
Theriot names the same free check independently, "this is where I'll literally just use Google Trends", and adds a second worked curve that is useful because it inverts rather than repeats the weight-loss one: dry skin runs at peak intensity through winter and falls to a must-be-nice offer between April and July. Two operators now prescribe the identical pre-commit check on two different products, which is the cheapest step in this whole file and is still routinely skipped.
The offer consequence, which is the part that turns the diagnosis into a decision: in the peak-summer body-image window the offer must promise INSTANT results, because the buyer's deadline is days away. "You cannot sell someone a weight-loss program during this period of time. You have to sell them instantaneous results." So the desire is at full intensity and the standard offer is still unsellable, because time-to-result exceeds the remaining occasion window. Generalise it as a gate on any dated-occasion campaign: check the gap between your time-to-result and the buyer's deadline before committing creative, and if the gap is negative, change the offer rather than the angle.
Sources: Nick Theriot, $30k days with Facebook Ads using Emotional Intensity, 2026-07-22; Andrew Faris, I've Managed Over 100 Meta Ads Accounts. Here's Why Yours Is Broken., 2026-05-27; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Nick Theriot, Your Ads Results Are Doomed If You Don't Do This Now!!!, 2026-06-19
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-084 · Re-skin the current offer for each holiday, and choose the Black Friday sale mechanic by micro-testing sale events all year
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Two linked practices: take whatever offer is running and re-skin it for the incoming holiday; and treat every sales event during the year as a micro-test of which sale mechanic the audience responds to, so Black Friday runs the proven one. Q4 prep: inventory orders need 3-4 months lead (start early Q3), content filmed September-October, everything ready by November.
Two additions from the She's Birdie account, where a real promo calendar is named a top-three growth lever. First, the brand-native cultural moment treated as a full campaign rather than an email and an Instagram post: International Women's Day for a women's safety product, which Richie says visibly moved the numbers. Second, Faris's mechanism for why a calendar works at all: every offer becomes limited-time and tied to a moment, which manufactures urgency and lifts conversion rate repeatedly through the year. Richie's prior blocker was self-imposed "brand integrity", and the unlock was hearing that Portland Leather Goods runs sales six days every month. Most brands are described as far too precious about discounting. Asserted, no numbers shown for the promo-calendar lift.
Professor Charley T inverts the wind-down half and the mechanism is worth stealing: a seasonal ad that still converts outside its season should keep running. He ran a Black Friday ad, with Black Friday named in the video text overlay, through to May, including on Roku connected TV. The claimed mechanism is that the viewer reads a still-live Black Friday offer as a mistake the brand forgot to switch off and moves to exploit it. The precondition is offer construction, and it is the load-bearing part: the Black Friday offer was a PREMIUM offer positioned as a Black Friday opportunity, not a discount on primary products, so there is no margin reason to end it. "If it's making money, don't turn it off." Practitioner account of a real run, no performance data shown. Build the holiday offer so that it can survive its holiday.
Two production numbers that make a promo calendar executable rather than aspirational, both from Theriot. Lead time is 21 DAYS and he breaks it into named weeks: week one research and writing, week two editing, week three a buffer for delays such as shipping product to a creator. "We always like to start production 21 days before" any dated event. And the sale-period build is a duplication rather than a new batch: "we'll also take our top-performing ads, we'll duplicate them, but we'll put a banner on those creatives as well", alongside purpose-built sale statics and videos. A holiday therefore costs a re-skin, not a shoot.
The counter-move for a known-bad acquisition window, which is the only positive tactic anyone offers for a trough: hype a new product drop for two to three weeks and release it INTO the trough. "we drop the new product during this time to reactivate all of our existing customers, which counters the decline of new customer revenue coming in because of ad costs being a little bit more expensive." It swaps expensive new-customer revenue for cheap existing-customer revenue rather than trying to win the auction. Requires a product pipeline and a list, so it does not transfer to lead gen or to a single-SKU brand. Asserted, no numbers.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05; Andrew Faris, From A 65% Decline To All-Time Revenue And Profit Highs (Richie Mashiko), 2026-04-27; Professor Charley T, Mastering Andromeda: Creative Testing and Scaling, 2025-12-13; Professor Charley T, If you run Facebook Ads... everything just changed., 2025-12-06; Nick Theriot, Your Ads Results Are Doomed If You Don't Do This Now!!!, 2026-06-19
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-085 · Build/pick-your-own-bundle offers are a newly emerging winner across accounts in 2026
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Letting buyers pick a preset bundle or build their own from the catalog with a discount is an emerging cross-client pattern. It is operationally hard for some stores to implement, which limits adoption and keeps it differentiating.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03
