## Landing pages and funnel congruence

### CR-086 · The landing page must mirror the ad's persona, angle and offer; congruence, not page format, is what wins
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Blue Sense: change any of persona/offer/angle in the ad and the page must change with it (menopause-creatine ad to a menopause PDP); a generic 9-benefit page makes the product look like snake oil to a viewer sold on one claim. Faris: anyone who actually split-tests landers knows they very often do nothing and sometimes hurt; wins never come from format (listicle, advertorial, design polish) but from carrying the ad's message through the funnel with a commensurate offer. A PDP/home page must by definition speak to everyone, capping its specificity.
Brad Plock extends the scent match past the headline into the images. The full chain: ad hook, to page headline, to the reasons-why copy, to the images supporting each reason, to the persona the page is built for, to the PDP images the user finally lands on. "If you're talking about aesthetics in your ad and you go to a landing page that's talking about comfort, you've lost." The PDP-image step is the new one and he credits it to Zach at Mars: make PDP images persona-specific to match the lander. Reasoning only, no data. Auditable move: read only the images on an existing lander and ask whether they alone would tell you which ad sent the traffic.
Library evidence that the top spenders already do this, from Alex Cooper: AG1, Hollow and Marek all pair a persona-specific ad with a persona-specific page. AG1 runs an ad built for the woman struggling with bloating and the click lands on a page written for that same persona. Observed in public ad libraries, no performance data attached, so read it as what the biggest budgets have converged on rather than as proof it works.
Congruence of PROOF is the extension, from a Google Ads local-services context and portable to any of them: whatever specific claim the ad makes, the page must carry clickable evidence for THAT claim, meaning reviews the visitor can open and read, awards, board memberships. The paired copy rule is that for homeowner services a referral-trust claim beats a superlative, "the home inspector that your friends and your families would recommend" over "the number one home inspector in town", because the first one is a claim proof can be attached to and the second is not. Asserted, no split test and no numbers, T4. It converts congruence from a messaging audit into a checkable one: read the ad's strongest claim, then find the thing on the page a sceptic could click to verify it.
The hardest version of the continuity build, running on five to ten of Blue Sense's clients since at least 2025-10-30: the page OPENS with the same video creative the visitor just clicked, then scrolls into a top-of-funnel educational page. "you'd click on this ad and then you'd land on the ad again and you'd start to scroll through into a very top of funnel educational landing page." His argument against the default destination is that a cold click onto a template Shopify PDP has exactly one job, a price check, because those pages carry almost no storytelling and no ability to educate, so the click is spent and nothing is transferred. And a multi-benefit PDP actively breaks a single-angle ad by dilution: the visitor arrived having self-selected one problem, is shown twenty tick-boxes, and "it's one of 20 value props, it's probably not going to fit me where I am, which is that's the only thing that I want solved."
Priority order for continuity downstream, from the guest on the same recording, plus the threshold that triggers it. Ad to landing page ranks above ad to email, "it's definitely not as important as the continuity between the landing page and the ad, like that's the most important." The email threshold: when roughly 60% of new customer acquisition comes from one product and one landing page, rewrite the PRE-purchase flows (abandoned cart, abandoned checkout) to that pain point and USP, and keep post-purchase flows broad. With 100 SKUs and balanced traffic, do not personalise at all. That is the first stated condition anywhere in this file for when message-matching stops paying downstream.
Independent ranking that puts this claim in its place, relayed and endorsed by Faris from the Marketing Operators: on what extends reach, creative is first, signal engineering second, landing pages LAST. "creative and creative diversity is the key." Consistent with his own position above that landers very often do nothing. Read CR-086 as the rule for how to build a lander when you build one, not as an argument for building one before the creative program exists.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026, 2026-08-10; Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler (And Harder To Execute) Than You Think, 2026-07-20; Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Solutions 8, Google Ads Essentials: Tips & Strategies, 2025-12-11; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Sending Cold Traffic on Meta to PDPs, 2025-10-30; Andrew Faris, I've Never Tracked CPMr For My $10M+ Clients. Here's Why., 2026-04-24
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-087 · A persona-congruent lander took ~90% of account spend within one day and was expected to roughly double account spend (She's Birdie)
Tier: T2 · Status: active
A College Safety Bundle listicle lander launched under already-running back-to-college ads absorbed roughly 90% of account spend within one day; all top spenders pointed to it, ROAS held, and Faris estimated it would double account spend. The page itself was flawed (irrelevant background video, unclear color selector); message-offer congruence did the work, and shipping fast beat polishing.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler, 2026-07-20
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-088 · Naming a bundle after the angle/persona drives the message through the funnel and beats the identical product under a generic name
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A ghost product whose name contains the angle (College Safety Bundle) creates funnel-long congruence. At a prior brand the same product and offer named two ways: the bloating-named bundle crushed the generically named version. Positioning the product for one specific person lets the ad message survive the click.
The same lever works on the product name itself, not just the bundle. Reviewing a caffeine gum ad that opens on 60mg of caffeine, guarana, B vitamins and taurine, Blue Sense points instead at the competitor's product line being called Energy and Focus: a buyer who wants steadier focus knows on sight the product is built for that outcome, which removes the need for a long-form VSL to establish the desire connection at all. He still wants the mechanism explained before he will trust it, so the name buys relevance, not credibility. Naming is the cheapest place in the funnel to carry the angle, and the only one that costs nothing per impression.
The cheapest instance of this rule anywhere in the file, and it is a lead magnet rather than a product: the SAME asset renamed from "blueprint" to "cheat sheet". Matt Shiver ran it as three statics, "we tested out nine-page cheat sheet. We tested nine-page blueprint. We tested just blueprint. And we almost had a double twice the amount of clicks when we said a nine-page cheat sheet. So again, it's the same exact lead magnet, but we just changed the offering a bit and we could test it with a static in literally 10 seconds of making on Canva." Read the RESULT with care: he retells the same test three weeks later as "we actually decreased his cost per lead by about 50% just by changing the name of it", and near-double clicks and a halved CPL are not the same measurement. Two retellings, two metrics, no dashboard either time, so bank the direction and not the multiple. What is genuinely transferable is the method, since a name test costs a Canva static and ten seconds, which makes it the highest-leverage-per-dollar test in this whole section.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler, 2026-07-20; Blue Sense Digital, Reviewing 7 Ads: The One Change I'd Make To 6 Of Them, 2026-01-22; Dr. Matt Shiver, I've Tested 1,000 Facebook Ads, These 15 Print For Online Coaches, 2026-06-16; Dr. Matt Shiver, The Exact Facebook Ad Testing System I Use to Scale Clients Past $100k/mo, 2026-07-07
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-089 · Test a landing page without post-click split tools by launching it under duplicate copies of existing winning creative
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Instead of a post-click A/B tool, launch proven winning ads pointed at the new lander alongside the originals and let performance decide. Faris credits this with removing the split-testing bottleneck and letting his agency build and validate landers at much higher speed. Nick Theriot runs the same pattern (duplicate ads or ad sets with only the URL changed, inside the same CBO).
Brad Plock adds the boundary condition, which keeps on-site CRO tooling useful for its actual job. If the change does not alter WHO you are trying to reach (buy box layout, five versus seven reasons why), split test it in a tool. If it changes the person the page is aimed at (five reasons for women 45+ versus an advertorial), duplicate the ad and let Meta allocate. The underlying logic: split tests answer either/or questions and page-type changes are and questions, so the mechanism has to match the question. Reasoning only, no data. Rule of thumb before choosing a test method: ask whether the variant is aimed at a different buyer.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler, 2026-07-20; Nick Theriot, You Just Found a Winning Ad, 2026-07-31; Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-090 · In test phase, a lander split can be decided within $300-400 of spend; leniency is earned at ~$500 (creative) or ~$1,000 (lander) of profitable spend
Tier: T2 · Status: active
Day-one traffic split 50/50 across two PDP variants; within the first $300-400 of spend lander 1 significantly outperformed, lander 2 was killed and spend consolidated. General rule: assets must prove themselves to earn spend; once a creative has ~$500 or a lander ~$1,000 of profitable spend, tolerance for dips increases. Executed in the shown $1k/day case study.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, ZERO to $1k/day in 3 days with ai dropshipping, 2026-07-30
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-091 · Build landers only for top-spending ads that carry a specific angle, not for every ad
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The repeatable process: scan the account's top-10 spenders, identify which carry a really specific angle/persona, and build the next lander (ideally with a matching offer) for that ad. Seasonal-moment personas (back-to-college in July/August) are especially lander-friendly because a large group needs the same thing at once; expect spend to decline sharply when the moment passes.
Brad Plock adds a second selection route and one hard constraint. Route: the top-spending ad is sometimes not the biggest messaging angle in the account, so tag-level aggregation of persona and angle across all ads (he uses Motion's AI tagging) surfaces the real opportunity and its trend over time. He also argues a seasonally relevant angle is still worth a lander even if the page only works for three months, because three months of better efficiency pays for the build. Constraint: build the page around exactly ONE ad's message, never around three top spenders that say slightly different things, because a page that tries to serve three messages serves none. His ads run one to two minutes, which is enough story to build a whole page from.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler, 2026-07-20; Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-092 · Creative variety that scales is variety of MESSAGE: many specific messages stacked beat one broad message
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The ability to reach the most people is the ability to speak to individuals really effectively and stack those individual messages, not to broadcast one message to everyone. A college-bound woman gets the college message; a woman who moved to a new city gets her own lander/offer; some prospects will pass on a mismatched persona offer and that is acceptable. Each persona message can carry its own product mix and offer.
Faris and Alex Cooper both state the inversion of the old rule explicitly. The traditional advice was to write for as many people as possible so the ad could scale; under engagement-based serving, highly specific content earns deeper engagement, which is the signal Meta uses to expand distribution, so specificity BUYS reach. Faris's illustration is organic: coaching four-to-six-year-old t-ball, his feed serves content on how to teach a six-year-old to catch rather than general youth baseball coaching. Reasoning plus a personal feed observation, with no paid test of the reach claim by either.
The paid observation that keeps this honest: reach and performance are separate axes. Faris ran one product against three Mother's Day messages aimed at divorced moms, moms of rainbow babies, and boy moms, and the rainbow-baby ads performed very well while explicitly NOT having the widest reach in the account. So a narrow message can be a top performer and still look small on a reach column, which is the same warning [[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]] gives about reading the wrong metric. No CPA, ROAS or spend shown. Note this sits against the spend-ceiling argument in [[Creative Science#CR-007|CR-007]]; the reconcilable version is that specificity buys efficiency now and caps volume later.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Secret To Great Landing Pages Is Simpler, 2026-07-20; Andrew Faris, The 3-Part Creative Diversity Framework I Use To Scale Past $10M On Meta Ads, 2026-05-01; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-093 · Cold/unaware VSL traffic must land on advertorial-style pages using the same spine as the ad script
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Structure for both lander and VSL: hook, problem agitation, unique mechanism, objection handling (3 biggest FAQ objections from customer research), proof matching the claim, risk reversal, CTA. The lander continues the ad's education for people who clicked before finishing the ad. Landing pages, VSLs and ads are the same copywriting converging into different formats; 60-95% of traffic is mobile, so judge on the phone.
Page ORDER is part of the spec and it inverts the standard ecommerce layout: the product block, the price and the add-to-cart sit at the BOTTOM, below every education block, with a sticky add-to-cart as the only shortcut. "you have to scroll and scroll and scroll and scroll before you get there and so you're just forced to either just scroll as hard as you can to find it or to actually just start consuming content from the brand... Now when the price hits you, you've been anchored against all this value above." Two mechanisms doing one job, price anchoring and forced consumption. This is the structural reason a cold-traffic advertorial is not a PDP with more copy on it. Asserted across a five to ten client deployment, no conversion-rate comparison shown.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, eCommerce CRO Masterclass 2026, 2026-08-10; Why Your Meta Ads Don't Scale, 2026-08-03; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Sending Cold Traffic on Meta to PDPs, 2025-10-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-094 · 90% of his traffic goes to listicle landing pages, currently beating advertorials and nearly all direct product pages
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The funnel is ad to listicle to product page. "Right now we're 90% listicle landing pages" and the listicle is beating a lot of advertorials and almost all product pages he sends traffic to. A pre-sell-page format claim from live portfolio allocation, no CVR comparison shown.
Brad Plock adds the difficulty ranking and one repeatable advertorial path. Listicles are the easiest starting format (images plus copy, with a comparison chart against alternative products, brands or bad alternatives). Advertorials are hard to hit and need a defined audience, with one exception that works extremely well: take a founder video transcript, have AI draft the advertorial in the founder's own language, then human-edit. His warnings attached: AI-designed landing pages are now visibly AI-designed, AI-written copy is poor without heavily thought-through instructions, and copy is the part that matters most on a listicle. Faris confirms his own agent-built lander system has not produced big wins yet.
Sources: Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03; Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-147 · A landing-page test is an "and" not an "or": the win condition is added account spend, and a matching lander visibly reallocates delivery and can revive ads that took no spend
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Read as a split test, a new lander taking only 20% of campaign spend looks like a loser. Read as spend expansion it is the opposite: total campaign spend rose from $5,000/day to $6,000/day, so the incremental 20% was net-new spend the PDP could not absorb, because some buyers need a different funnel than a product page. That reframes launching a lander as identical in purpose to launching new creative, hunting more spend at the same target rather than a winning asset. The corollary Brad names: sometimes the win shows up as account-level efficiency instead, which you can either bank or scale into.
Two pieces of corroborating evidence from the same account. Delivery reallocation: the ad was a founder video about why she built the product for women her age and the lander retold that story for that demographic; women 45-54 got more efficient and then scaled, while 55-64 and 65+ did not change. Two secondary metrics moved with it, PDP bounce rate ~90% versus the lander at ~70%, and CPMR held or improved while spend doubled over two months, though Brad discounts both (CPMR as lagging, bounce rate as hand-wavy) and treats spending efficiency as the real read. Dead-ad revival: "we actually duplicated an ad that wasn't getting any spend at the PDP, and it became the second top-spending ad." The implication is that a large share of dead ads in mature accounts may be lander-mismatched rather than bad, which reopens hundreds of written-off creatives. His practical resolution given limited resources: once a lander exists, duplicate every ad sharing roughly the same message onto it, because the downside of duplication is only that they do not spend. Stated exception: accounts near their page or ad limits.
Shipping rule from the same conversation: default the lander to click through to the PDP rather than building checkout on it, because most of the value comes from a headline matching the ad and a page that communicates to the customer, and "one extra click to your product page is not going to destroy anything." A shoppable section is a later optimisation and a good split-test candidate once the page is dialled in. This removes the most common excuse for not shipping a lander at all.
All numbers ASSERTED to the audience (Brad showed Faris privately), not screen-shared. Faris raises a live mechanism objection worth recording: Meta forecasts delivery off upper-funnel engagement signals that are identical across two ads with the same creative, so a URL swap should not change delivery at all. Brad's counter is a relayed Meta-rep claim that Meta crawls the page and that crawl alone differentiates delivery. Neither is settled; the test is a URL-swap duplicate with a demographic delivery breakdown checked inside the first week. One piece of evidence now leans toward Brad's side: Blue Sense independently names the landing page as one of four assets Meta reads to decide who to serve, alongside the copy, the creative and the spoken transcript (see [[Creative Science#CR-148|CR-148]]). That is a second practitioner asserting the page is a delivery input, still with no documentation behind it.
Blue Sense reaches the same "do it, and do not call it a creative test" conclusion from the volume-model side, and the taxonomy point is the useful half: "landing page testing is a needle mover um you just have to do it correctly at volume with statistical relevancy but it is not a new ad." Under his definition a landing page swap does not create a new ad at all, which means it must be budgeted and counted separately from creative volume rather than inside it (see [[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]]). He also says e-commerce brands under-do it relative to lead gen, extrapolated from lead gen with no data. T4.
A second measurement trap on the same asset, and it points the opposite way from the first: an educational cold-traffic page may show NO first-click gain at all. "it might not have an immediate impact on first click because maybe you have a weak time to purchase, but it's going to help the entire funnel in educating rather than just educating in front end ads." So a first-session conversion-rate read can score a working page as a loss, on top of the spend-share read scoring it as a loser. Between the two, the only honest scoring for an education page is account-level spend and account-level efficiency over a window longer than one session.
The confound that invalidates most angle tests, from the same operator: the reason an angle wins may sit on the PAGE rather than in the ad. "the reason why that angle is working might not actually have anything to do with the ads, it might be completely due to the fact that the landing page at the very top you were saying that value proposition again... it has everything to do with the continuity on the actual landing page." Practical consequence, and it is a hard one for anyone running angle batches cheaply: an angle test where each angle points at a different page is measuring the page, and an angle test where every angle points at the same generic PDP is measuring which angle happens to survive a mismatch. Neither is a clean read of the angle. Match the pages or accept the confound explicitly.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22; Blue Sense Digital, How Machine Learning Works in Meta Ads (2025), 2025-03-25; Blue Sense Digital, Here's Exactly How Many Creatives You Need To Hit Revenue Targets, 2025-02-24; Blue Sense Digital, Stop Sending Cold Traffic on Meta to PDPs, 2025-10-30; Blue Sense Digital, 5 Step Checklist to Increase Meta Ad Performance Overnight, 2025-02-26
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-149 · Landing-page count scales with persona count, not SKU count: one SKU reportedly supports around 30 live landers
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Zach at Mars is the cited operator, the product is a single SKU, and the page count comes from persona and messaging-pillar count. The rule stated is strict: an ad about a specific pillar (cortisol) must go to a page about that pillar. Page-type expansion (listicle or quiz first, then duplicating out to advertorials and other page types) happens after the first matching page exists, not before. Secondhand and asserted, with Brad's own reaction being that 30 pages for one SKU "is an insane amount". This is the practical upper bound on [[Creative Science#CR-092|CR-092]] (variety of message beats variety of format), and it sets a ceiling operators can plan against, since lander count follows persona count and persona count is partly a product-category selection made at launch.
A second account running the same ratio in the other direction, observed by Blue Sense: five to six angle tests per week, each with its own dedicated landing page. His stated reason is failure rather than upside, "if you then just drive to your generic PDP that says yeah so it has this benefit this benefit and there's like 20 different benefits there then people are going to go oh this is just like a generic supplement... okay bounce." So the page count is set by the ANGLE test rate as well as by the persona count, and an account testing angles weekly needs a page-building capability rather than a page-building project. That is a throughput constraint most operators discover after committing to the angle cadence.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22; Blue Sense Digital, 5 Step Checklist to Increase Meta Ad Performance Overnight, 2025-02-26
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-150 · Watch session recordings after any page goes live: a winning quiz page had zero of 15 visitors start the quiz, and adding a shop button raised revenue per session 20%
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The page was already winning and taking spend in the ad account, so no performance metric flagged the problem; only watching recordings did. "I watched like 15 recordings in a row. Literally not a single person started the quiz." Adding a "shop the product" button directly below "start the quiz" raised revenue per session 20%, measured with Intelligems on the back end. He still does not know why nobody started the quiz, and that is the point: the fix did not require the diagnosis, only the observation that the intended path was unused. Microsoft Clarity is free and he treats installing it plus actually watching recordings as a default step after any page ships. Numbers asserted, not screen-shared.
Sources: Andrew Faris, The Right Way To Test Landing Pages On Meta Ads In 2026 (with Brad Plock), 2026-06-22
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-164 · A progress indicator took quiz completion from roughly 60% to 92%, the contact ask sits AFTER the diagnosis, and whether to gate the quiz at all is contested inside one conversation
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
The build, described by the operator running it: 10 questions, dynamic branching so the next question depends on the previous answer, 12 outcome paths, one tailored resource per path. His words on the lift: "92% completion rate after we put a progress tab at the top of the quiz funnel. It just highlights yellow every time you click. And we were around 60." He flags his own baseline as unreliable in the same breath, "I can't remember the exact number", so treat 60 as approximate and 92 as the only firm figure.
GATE PLACEMENT. Name, email and phone are asked on the last step, after the quiz has named the person's result on screen and told them which resource is coming. His reasoning is that the person has already received the diagnosis, so the ask lands after value rather than in front of it. The contrast he draws is with quizzes that ask for an email to reveal the result, where nothing has been given yet.
THE CONTESTED HALF, and it arrives from the other speaker in the same episode. Vshred's funnel, walked through from the outside, takes no opt-in at all on the quiz and routes each result to its own VSL and a roughly $57 low-ticket offer. The stated read is friction removal: "I think they try to reduce all the friction as possible." So one operator gates late and the other observes a large advertiser not gating at all and selling instead. Nobody ran the comparison. Status contested for that reason alone, and the Vshred description is explicitly hedged by the speaker as an outside observation of an ad account he has not opened recently.
THE FOLLOW-UP DESIGN IS THE PART WORTH STEALING. The resource is a 28-day protocol with checkpoints, and the follow-up messages land on days 7, 14 and 28 to match those checkpoints. The message has a reason to exist that the recipient already agreed to, and the two outcomes both open the sales conversation: the protocol worked, which argues for the paid version, or it was never started, which re-establishes the problem.
VOLUME, so the completion rate has a denominator. About 30 quiz opt-ins a day with name, email and phone on the consumer offer, producing about 5 booked calls a day. The same build on his business-to-business offer produces about 30 opt-ins a MONTH. Same funnel, same operator, roughly 30x the volume, which locates the gap in the offer and the audience rather than in the quiz.
ASSERTED throughout. Nothing screen-shared, no date range on any figure, no control for anything else that changed.
Pairs with [[Creative Science#CR-150|CR-150]], where a quiz page that was already winning in the ad account had zero of 15 recorded visitors start the quiz. Read together: completion mechanics are worth real money and no performance metric in the ad account will show you they are broken.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Lead-gen funnel creative

### CR-095 · Leads who book a calendar slot themselves show up, get contacted, and convert at significantly higher rates than contact-info-only form leads
Tier: T3 · Status: active
When a lead books a call directly into the calendar, quality is nearly always significantly better than a plain instant-form lead you then chase; booked leads are far more likely to show and be reachable. The historic tradeoff was website-booking funnels producing fewer, more expensive but higher-quality leads versus instant forms producing cheap leads that waste sales time.
Sources: Ben Heath, Facebook Ads Just Changed Forever!, 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-096 · Brick-and-mortar funnels start with lead form ads using conditional logic to disqualify, then graduate proven offers to a landing page funnel
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Lead forms beat DM ads for local businesses because conditional logic disqualifies bad fits and withholds their data from the pixel, so Meta learns only on qualified results. The promoted offer should be a front-end self-liquidating offer (e.g. discounted evaluation for a PT clinic) that gets people in the door. Once proven in lead forms, the offer moves to a landing-page funnel. Ben Heath shows the exact build in Ads Manager and confirms the delivery consequence: in the instant form's Questions step, add a multiple-choice question, open Conditional logic, and route the disqualifying answer to "close the form", which shows a separate end page for non-leads and means those people never register as a lead at all. Meta then learns that only the qualifying answers count as results. The same split runs on a website contact form by sending qualified and unqualified submitters to different thank-you pages and firing the conversion event on only one.
Microcopy on the form itself, from a Google-side team but the constraint transfers to any form or instant form: one calm sentence above or below the field or button, addressing time, privacy and effort. "Fears around time, privacy, and effort are your number one killers of conversions and purchases... The way you reduce fear and friction is through one calm sentence, not your privacy policy version of war and peace." Their examples: "takes 60 seconds, no spam", "we will not sell your email address", "you'll hear back from us in one business day". Note the last one collides with our own standing rule against promising a callback timeframe when a voice agent calls on opt-in regardless of hours; use the first two shapes and not the third. T4, asserted, no test.
Restated by the same operator on 2026-08-20 with a named failure mode attached, which is the useful addition. The case is a creator whose audience holds two different populations, fitness coaches who would buy a business offer and consumers who would buy a health offer. His build is a webinar registration form asking name, phone, email and "are you an online coach, yes or no", with the pixel firing on yes only. The mechanism he gives for why the gate is mandatory rather than optional: a new campaign served against a large existing audience will draw opt-ins from all of it, so an ungated form teaches the model the union of both populations. His words: "if all your audience opts in, the pixel is going to be like anybody that likes hormones... just have a disqualifier in there. If you do run ads to it, it's only going to learn off of people who are coaches." Note this is the website-form half of the claim, a landing-page registration, so it is the thank-you-page and conditional-fire mechanism rather than the instant-form "close the form" route. Asserted, no CPL or lead-quality figures on either side of the gate.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Run Facebook Ads for Coaches & Agency Owners, 2026-08-04; Ben Heath, Learn 97% of Meta Ads in Under 29 Minutes, 2026-08-18; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30; Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-097 · Funnel type maps to offer economics: B2B direct-offer/VSL above $200/day and DM ads below; mass-market B2C uses webinars when education-heavy, low-ticket funnels when not
Tier: T3 · Status: active
B2B buyers want it done for them, so direct offers ("result in timeframe or you don't pay") beat freebies; VSL/application funnels need $200+/day (1x the B2B $200-500 cost per call). Mass-market B2C needs a webinar when the mechanism requires education (investing, trading, gut health, but usually not fitness) and a low-ticket funnel as a quality filter when it does not, with a 3-4 month test window. Across his client base DM and webinar ads convert best for B2C, VSL ads best for B2B; B2C also layers follower ads at $50-100/day buying follows at $1-3.
Two additions from the same operator. Post-selection rule for the follower layer: only boost organic posts that call out the ICA specifically, because "something could go viral cuz it's very broad. We can't run ads to that cuz it's going to bring in a bunch of people who aren't their ideal avatar." The selection filter is avatar callout, not engagement, and if no organic post qualifies they build a carousel or video script for the purpose. Specificity branch: an unusual, specific avatar (volleyball strength coach, pickleball coach) can run a direct-offer DM ad immediately and get cheap booked calls because almost nobody advertises to them, while a mass-market avatar cannot and has to route through a lead magnet, webinar or low-ticket offer first.
Charley T supplies the full measurement rig for the follower layer, which is the part usually run blind. Test control: an automated rule turns each promoted post off at a fixed impression threshold, minimum 2,000 and preferably 5,000 or 10,000, so every post receives roughly the same impressions and no single post runs away with the budget. Higher thresholds take longer and cost more and give better data. KPI: cost per follow, computed as spend divided by follows, read from the Instagram post insights via the ad preview share link, tracked weekly in a spreadsheet with post name, follows, spend and cost per follow. Optimisation: compute the account average as total spend across all posts divided by total follows across all posts, turn off every post above that average, which drags the average down, and duplicate the posts that produce follows into a second campaign WITHOUT the impression-cap rule so they can keep running. His framing for why views are the wrong metric: "you will see super popular content get you zero followers, views aren't value, followers are potential customers", and 5 million views from non-buyers is worth nothing against 100 views where 50 are ready to buy. Practitioner method; the account is shown later in the same video.
His B2C ladder stated as a ranked preference with the selection condition attached, 2026-07-07: direct offer FIRST because it is the shortest funnel and picks up solution-aware buyers, webinar second, low-to-high ticket third. "if you're brand new and you have a competitive advantage, like let's say that your market's not very saturated, I always recommend starting off with a direct offer ads because it's just the shortest funnel." The condition is the load-bearing part, the direct offer only works in an unsophisticated or uncompetitive market. His response when it fails is also worth noting, because it is not the obvious one: narrow the niche or sharpen the mechanism before switching funnel type. So a failed direct offer is a positioning verdict first and a funnel verdict second.
A ceiling on the low-ticket branch, added 2026-08-20 by the same operator, and it is a spend ceiling rather than a performance one. The low-ticket front end is a consumer instrument because the addressable market sets how far it scales: "B2B it can work kind of like for your avatar, but the TAM is a lot smaller, so you'll hit a cap with it... versus like a mass market B2C or health offer, you could crank up a low ticket." Price band named as $9 to $37. His acceptance threshold is the part to hold onto, because it prices the qualification directly: breakeven, or even 0.5 back on front-end spend, is a pass, since the return being bought is lead quality rather than revenue. That is the same trade as [[Learning & Signal#LS-009|LS-009]], paid for in offer design instead of in event choice.
Same conversation, spend architecture on a creator account: roughly $200/day against warm audiences and $800 to $1,000/day against cold, with the warm ad set holding the account's highest reported ROAS. Two cautions before that shape is copied. The warm ad set's ROAS is read from platform attribution, and [[Attribution & Incrementality#AT-011|AT-011]] holds that a 1-day-view conversion is a prior site visitor by construction, so a warm ad set is the exact place platform ROAS overstates. And [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-001|MD-001]] holds that custom audiences bound nothing, so a "retargeting ad set" is a spend label rather than a delivery boundary. Asserted, no figures shown for either the ROAS or the split.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Run Facebook Ads for Coaches & Agency Owners, 2026-08-04; How I Built a $2.25M/Year Coaching Business, 2026-08-11; Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026 (Full Course Post-Andromeda), 2026-04-09; Dr. Matt Shiver, The Exact Facebook Ad Testing System I Use to Scale Clients Past $100k/mo, 2026-07-07; Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-146 · A free-course community funnel produces one of the lowest costs per lead he runs, and pays for it with the longest lead-to-call window
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Where most of his own traffic has gone for the past year. The CPL advantage comes from the offer being maximally easy to say yes to: "Hey, I made a free course that's got everything you want in there." The cost is timing, since people consume, get partway, hit the next problem and only then ascend, so cash lands weeks later. He staffs around the lag rather than trying to remove it: a Skool-attached CRM Chrome extension (about $100/month) gives a pipeline plus automated outbound to new joiners, and one setter works DMs, SMS and in-community messages to book calls. Content inside is deliberately unpolished Loom recordings. The planning constraint is the bankable part: this funnel's CPL cannot be compared like-for-like against a call funnel's CPL without accounting for the delay. Asserted, no CPL numbers given.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, Organic vs Paid Ads: What's Actually Working In 2026, 2026-06-25
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-165 · Contact before the call is what holds show rate: 76% company-wide against sub-50% for leads nobody reached, and the operator attributes the difference to the contact being HUMAN
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The figures, from a coaching company running a quiz funnel at about 30 opt-ins a day. Show rate across the company is 76%. For leads that come through a lead magnet and are never reached before the appointment, "it's like sub 50%". Their build puts a phone setter on the lead immediately, while the person is still on the page, and the setter books qualified people onto a closer's calendar.
The operator's own explanation names the human: "having humans handling the outreach has just been significantly better than like any sort of automation through Close CRM. There are automations in there, but if you're qualified, you're talking to humans." He adds a scaling condition, that the further up-funnel the entry point sits, quiz, lead magnet or VSL, the harder the nurture layer has to work, because it is trying to reproduce the conversation a DM thread would have had before the call.
**SPLIT THE CLAIM, because two different things are bundled and only one of them is measured.** The measured part is that leads contacted before a booked appointment show at 76% and leads not contacted show below 50%. The unmeasured part is the attribution of that gap to human contact specifically. He ran no arm where a machine made the contact, so the comparison is contact against no contact, and the human-versus-automation verdict rides on the automation he happens to use, a CRM email and SMS sequence, which is not a caller.
**This is the claim in the codex that argues hardest against our own delivery stack, which is why it is banked in full.** Our stack replaces the human setter with an AI voice agent that calls on opt-in, qualifies, and books against the live calendar. Read strictly, this claim supports the half our stack already does, fast contact before the call, and asserts without evidence the half our stack disputes. We are the ones positioned to settle it, because we hold show-rate data across five accounts where the agent makes the contact. That makes it one of the cheapest T2 upgrades available to us, and the honest version of the test compares agent-contacted leads against uncontacted leads on the same account before it compares agents against humans.
ATTRIBUTION NOTE, following our own speaker-verification discipline: the 76% is stated by the owner of the funnel about his own company. The sub-50% figure sits in an exchange where the auto-transcript carries no speaker labels and the boundary between the two speakers is genuinely ambiguous, so credit it to the conversation rather than to a named operator. No date range, no denominator and no screenshot accompanies either number.
Extends [[Creative Science#CR-095|CR-095]], where self-booked calendar leads show and convert better than chased form leads.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-174 · A 90-minute live workshop fixed a show-rate collapse in a market too sceptical to book a call cold, at a stated 18x blended return across six or seven events
Tier: T3 · Status: active
THE NUMBER, and it is the strongest one in its batch: "I did do the data and across all of the workshops, my ROAS is an 18X." Computed by the operator across a defined set of six or seven workshops in a real account. Stated verbally with nothing on screen, no date range, no spend figure and no definition of which revenue window it counts, so bank the figure and the caveat together.
THE DIAGNOSIS, which is the transferable half. The market was too sceptical to book a call from a cold ad, so calls were being booked and then not attended. The fix was not a better ad. It was inserting a 90-minute live workshop between the ad and the sales conversation. So a show-rate collapse was solved by changing the OFFER OF THE AD rather than the follow-up, which is the opposite of where most show-rate work goes and worth checking against [[Creative Science#CR-165|CR-165]], where the same problem was solved by contacting the lead before the appointment. Two different accounts, two different fixes, same symptom, and nobody compared them.
THE CAUSAL READ HE GIVES, and it is a lead-quality finding wearing creative clothing. The purchase blocker was self-belief and self-worth rather than discretionary income. His reason for the format follows directly: "What I can't necessarily do all the time in a sales call is change someone's self-perception of themselves in 60 minutes." A 90-minute live event does work a call cannot do, which is the same argument the education branch of [[Creative Science#CR-097|CR-097]] makes for webinars in mechanism-heavy categories, extended to a belief-heavy one.
WHERE IT APPLIES FOR US. Any account where leads book and do not show, and where the objection at the call is confidence rather than price. The cheap version of the test is one live event against the existing direct-to-call flow on matched spend, reading show rate and close rate, not ROAS, since an 18x with no stated window is not a number anyone should try to reproduce.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How One Coach Dominates a Niche With Almost No Competition, 2026-04-23
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## Market and geo transfer

### CR-098 · Proven winning ads transfer to new similar markets and behave like fresh launches, unlocking step-change scale
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Multiple independent sources. Blue Sense: a winning ad with $200k+ lifetime spend in one top-5 English market will almost certainly win in the others; one AU brand launched banked winners into the US and ~8 months later the US was 75% of a business he estimates at $30-40M. Nick Theriot: exporting winners to a new country makes them feel like first-time launches, and businesses have 2-3x'd within a month of expanding to one or two new countries.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17; Nick Theriot, I Spend $100k/Day On Facebook Ads, 2026-08-03
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-099 · UK and Australian accents transfer between each other's markets but not into the US; re-voicing winners with US accents often restores performance
Tier: T3 · Status: active
UK ads/accents work in Australia and vice versa, but pushing either into the US performs much worse. The recommended move: take every winner from the last 5 years and reshoot or re-voice with US accents/creators instead of chasing new concepts, because most teams over-index on new concept iteration and under-index on translating proven winners.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-100 · Brand-led campaign-shoot assets only show good ROAS because of pre-existing brand equity; they fail when exported to a market where nobody knows you
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A legacy retailer's plain product-shoot ads return well at home because 25 years of brand awareness does the converting, not the asset. Launched into the US where recognition and reviews do not transfer, the same assets do nothing, revealing they were never performance assets. New-market creative must build trust as if the brand does not exist.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The US Launch Playbook for Non-US Brands, 2026-08-17
