## Product and avatar research

### CR-101 · Find products worth cloning by filtering for stores at 100k-2M monthly visits growing 10-25% month-over-month for six straight months, with a Meta pixel installed
Tier: T3 · Status: active
His TrendTrack filter: minimum 100,000 monthly visits (rough proxy for ~$100k+/mo revenue), 10-25% MoM traffic growth (25% for products starting to rocket), rising direction sustained 6 months, Meta pixel present to confirm active ads. Cross-check the store's year-prior seasonality curve and Google Trends to avoid dying or seasonal trends.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-102 · Pick the launch avatar by tallying avatar self-identifications in the incumbent's reviews and choosing the highest-occurrence group no brand serves
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Read the scaling competitor's website/TikTok/YouTube/Reddit reviews and tick-mark every avatar self-identification. Launch on the highest-count avatar only if no other brand is positioned on that avatar with the same product. The clone recreates the incumbent's top-performing, longest-active, and most-duplicated ads shot-for-shot, recast for the avatar.
Second extraction from the same file, and it produces angles rather than avatars: OFF-LABEL uses. Customers use products in ways the brand never planned for and those uses sit unread in reviews and comment threads. Alex Cooper's anti-dandruff shampoo angle came from a Reddit thread about people using a competitor's product on beard dandruff, an angle the brand's ads had never touched, and it is now a standing angle in that account. Faris demonstrates the same read live on Solstice Watersports, where product images sell dock recreation while reviews describe people using the inflatable dock to clean boat hulls and to inspect a 30-foot powerboat on a lift; the same file also exposes the blocking fear, whether an inflatable is firm enough to stand on. Two passes over one review file, one for who they are and one for what they actually did with it.
Third pass over the same review file, and it reads the FIVE-star reviews rather than the one-stars: identify which desire the competitor leads with, on the logic that the five-star language tells you what the buyer thought they were buying. The decision rule attached is competitive rather than descriptive: if you have no clear way to beat the incumbent on that desire, do not take the same desire, find a different one. That is the review-file version of the differentiation rule in [[Creative Science#CR-057|CR-057]], and it is explicitly not a licence to copy their ads. Method with an AG1 example, no numbers.
The version for a brand that already HAS customers, which none of the passes above cover because they all read someone else's review file. Ben Heath selects the segment from his own data on three axes, and the third is the one nobody puts in a brief: which customers buy again most, which stick around longest on a recurring model, and which cause the fewest headaches. "you can do analysis on those customers and work out which customers are worth the most to you, which either buy again the most or stick around... Those will typically often be the ones that give you the less headaches as well." Then rebuild the messaging around that segment's specific problems. Two things worth taking. Support load is a legitimate selection criterion because it is a real cost the CAC calculation ignores. And this is a strictly cheaper research route than the review-mining passes above, since the data is already in the CRM.
Sources: Nick Theriot, The Most Braindead Way To Find Winning Products in 2026, 2026-08-17; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-103 · Should you copy competitors' ads directly?
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Nick Theriot: never. Copying a competitor caps you below the originator (his illustrative heuristic: roughly one order of magnitude below) and makes your hooks match the niche's saturated pattern. The sanctioned move is monitoring winning ads across different niches (via a trend tracker) and importing the mechanic where it is new, supplemented with customer surveys and YouTube/Reddit problem searches for verbatim customer phrasings.
Mark Builds Brands sits on the same side with a mechanism: swipe the philosophy, not the copy. "When I'm swiping ads, I'm not taking the copy. What I'm doing is I'm actually swiping the philosophy." His argument is about learning rate: 1:1 swiping produces a short-lived result and teaches the operator nothing, so the operator stays permanently dependent on finding the next thing to swipe, while framework extraction compounds into a reusable bank. He also names the AI application, which is to feed extracted philosophies and frameworks into a model to generate large numbers of on-principle variations, rather than prompting from a competitor's literal copy.
Professor Charley T and Ben Heath argue the opposite, in a joint session, with two filters. Heath's longevity filter: an ad running a week tells you nothing, an ad running 6+ months (he stretches to 9-18) has survived and is almost certainly profitable, and this holds "for just about every advertiser." Charley's selection filter: "What you should be looking at to copy is somebody at the level you're trying to get to. Don't go after somebody 10 times bigger than you." Hexclad, Vuori and Hormozi-tier brands have solved the business model, most of their ads fail anyway, and their assets (celebrity, influencer partnerships) are not replicable. He advocates literal shot-for-shot remakes of statics and videos from same-level operators.
Neither side showed performance data from a copied ad. The narrow point of disagreement is copying a same-level operator's execution, and the two positions do partly reconcile: everyone agrees copying a 10x-larger brand is wasted, and everyone agrees the saturated-pattern risk is real. Nobody tested it.
Theriot's own sanctioned move, stated concretely on 2026-06-15: pull the SCRIPT STRUCTURE from a completely unrelated niche, his example being a testosterone brand's top ads used as the template for a breath spray. The stated benefit is that the borrowed structure is unfamiliar in the destination category, which makes it a differentiation play rather than a copying one, and it is the same lever as the novelty premium in [[Creative Science#CR-057|CR-057]]. No performance data on the resulting ads. Practical rule that follows and settles most of the argument: cross-niche structure is safe to take, same-niche execution is what carries the saturation risk.
Heath restated the longevity filter solo on 2026-02-20 with the negative half spelled out, which is the part that makes it usable as a screen: "if they only launched an ad last week, you don't know that thing's working. They might just be experimenting. If they've been consistently running an ad for more than say 6 months, particularly if it's longer than that, that ad is almost certainly working for them, and you could look to model from it." His shown example is his own ad set running since May 2025, about nine months at recording. The filter is cheap, checkable in the public library, and the only quality gate in this whole claim that does not require judgement. Note what it cannot tell you: an ad that survives six months in a brand-equity-heavy account may be surviving on the brand rather than on the creative ([[Creative Science#CR-100|CR-100]]).
Sources: Nick Theriot, NEW Customers or ALL Customers with Meta Ads In 2026?, 2026-08-05; How To Create Facebook Ad Hooks That Scale In 2026, 2026-07-27; Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Professor Charley T, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Low Budget (joint session with Ben Heath), 2026-02-28; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Ben Heath, How to CRUSH Facebook Ads with a Small Budget in 2026, 2026-02-20
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-139 · Recorded customer calls outrank social mining as a hook-language source, and the phrase should ship verbatim rather than rewritten
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Research-source hierarchy: recorded calls with your own customers beat Reddit, Facebook groups, TikTok and Instagram mining, and most ecom operators never do it at all. He runs a team that calls customers and reviews the transcripts himself specifically for hook language. "There was this one very specific phrase that one of my customers used to describe the issue that they were having. And I took that very very specific phrase, word for word, I used it as a hook on one of my ads and it ended up becoming one of the most profitable ads in my entire ad account."
Two rules follow: transcribe every customer call and mine it for phrasing, and do not rewrite the phrase into marketing language, ship it verbatim. That second rule is a correction to the standard advice in [[Creative Science#CR-103|CR-103]], which describes customer phrasings as "rewritable as hooks". Where no customer base exists yet, his substitute is building an AI persona on the research documents and having a spoken conversation with it, with per-angle avatars for different buying reasons. Single anecdote, no CPA numbers given.
Alex Cooper states the same no-paraphrase rule independently and adds the selection filter that separates a signal from a lucky quote. The rule: "you generally don't want to paraphrase what people are saying, because that is how your customers speak and your customers are your best copywriters." His shipped example is "hubby looks freaking hot", which ran as an ad and performed, against the paraphrase "my husband is great", which carries none of the charge. Faris's reading of why it works is a targeting argument: the word choices encode age, gender and cultural position precisely enough to act as a delivery signal on a personalised feed (see [[Creative Science#CR-148|CR-148]]). Product was jeans, buyer was male for roughly three quarters of the year and gift buyers in Q4.
The filter: only elevate a verbatim phrase when the underlying SENTIMENT recurs across many reviews, then pick the most emotive wording of that recurring pattern. Many customers were saying a version of "my husband looks great in these jeans", which established the pattern, and the job was then finding the most striking phrasing of it. He is blunt that most of what sits in a review file would make bad ad copy, so recurrence is the screen.
Source ranking from the same operator, which sits against Mark's calls-first hierarchy rather than agreeing with it: comments rank richest, including comments on your own ads and on YouTube, TikTok and Instagram, and on competitors' content when your own volume is thin. Support tickets rank next. He has cut back on Reddit because the content is increasingly bot generated and is shifting toward sources tied to identified customers. Carry the contradiction plainly: the Reddit judgement is asserted with no data, and he still credits Reddit with one of his best angles ([[Creative Science#CR-102|CR-102]]).
Two sourcing numbers from Nick Theriot that make the hierarchy operable. The post-purchase quiz to an existing customer list returns roughly 10% response, and his stated instance is 652 responses from 6,000 customers, asked around why they bought and what they love; he treats it as his primary read on which desire buyers actually bought on, which is a different question from which desire the ad sold. Note what it is: a survey response rate, not an advertising test, and nothing was shown to verify it. Second, a volume gate on the free sources, which is the part usually left to feel: a Reddit thread is only worth mining at roughly 50 to 100-plus comments, and a search returning threads with a handful of comments each is a signal the desire is not live in the market. Heuristic, no calibration. Both sit under the same rule as the rest of this claim, that the phrasing you take out ships verbatim.
A fourth source that costs nothing and is usually sitting unused in a B2B business: the front-facing SALES TEAM, plus recorded discovery calls, taking the words and pains buyers repeat most. Same instrument as the recorded-customer-call rule above, pointed at prospects rather than customers, which makes it available before there is a customer base to mine. The team stating it attaches the correct caveat, which is that following every copy rule still does not settle the wording, "you could follow all of these tips to the letter and still something doesn't quite land and it could be something as a verb choice. So run AB tests." That is the honest boundary on this whole claim: sourcing gets you the candidate phrases, it does not tell you which one wins.
Sources: Mark Builds Brands, 8 years of marketing advice in 70 minutes, 2026-03-21; Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02; Nick Theriot, How to scale Facebook ads using Human Desire, 2026-06-15; Solutions 8, How to Get Conversions with Effective Ad Copy (Winning Words), 2026-01-30
Last touched: 2026-08-20

### CR-155 · The 5 Fs persona document: fears, failures, fantasies, false solutions and failed solutions, each its own titled section with ranked categories and verbatim quotes underneath
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Alex Cooper's angle-research artifact, run on every client at Ad Crate. Each persona gets one document carrying the five Fs as separately titled sections, with ranked categories inside each and the supporting verbatim customer quotes nested under each category. Two more layers wrap it: the trigger moment, and the awareness and sophistication level. He says FAILED SOLUTIONS convert into ad scripts most directly, which matches the failed-solutions archetype in [[Creative Science#CR-015|CR-015]] and the pre-emptive objection kill in [[Creative Science#CR-072|CR-072]], and that a well-populated document yields a large number of launch points in a few minutes of reading.
The trigger moment is a separate extraction pass and it is the part most research skips. It is the specific scene where the customer decided the problem needed solving. For the jeans example the persona is not "wives of 35 to 55 year old men", it is the wife who fell in love with her husband at 30, has watched him let himself go, and wants him to look like the man she married. That scene can open the ad visually or be narrated in the first line. Customers volunteer these moments unprompted in long reviews, post-purchase surveys and ad comments, and a survey can ask for it directly if the question is phrased respectfully.
Preconditions elsewhere in this file: the persona must be psychographic, not demographic ([[Creative Science#CR-008|CR-008]]), the phrasing ships verbatim ([[Creative Science#CR-139|CR-139]]), and the document is built manually before any AI pass ([[Creative Science#CR-066|CR-066]]). Asserted from agency practice, no output-quality comparison shown.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-156 · Consumption analysis: search the persona's own pain point on TikTok and YouTube, train a burner feed on the results, then copy the FORMAT that wins there
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Runs after the 5 Fs and answers a different question: not what the persona believes, but what they watch. Type each persona's fears and pain points into TikTok and YouTube exactly as they would ("how to get rid of dandruff"), build burner accounts, then watch, save and like the results so the algorithm fills the feed with that content quickly. What Cooper extracts is format, not message: creator type, production level, setting, tone, how the problem is addressed, and how the transition into a solution is made. He also swipes high-performing organic directly from that feed.
Two reasons this is worth staffing. It answers the format question with evidence from the buyer's own consumption rather than from the ad library, which is what CR-054 and CR-125 both leave to guesswork. And Cooper states plainly that AI cannot do this step, because the input is a personalised feed a model cannot see, which makes it one of the few research tasks that stays manual. Asserted from agency practice, no test of whether format-matched creative outperforms.
Sources: Andrew Faris, Alex Cooper's "5 F's" Framework For Researching Ad Angles, 2026-07-02
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-170 · Filter a competitor ad library to 2,500-character ad copy to isolate native ads, then a top 1% ad-rank filter cuts 31,000 ads to 197
Tier: T2 · Status: active
THE METHOD, run on screen in a third-party ad library. Set the ad-copy-length filter to 2,500 characters and the result is almost purely native ads, because nothing else in the category runs primary text that long. That is the same 2,000 to 2,500 character spec already banked as the format definition in [[Creative Science#CR-047|CR-047]], used backwards as a search key. Then add the tool's top 1% ad-rank filter and the pool collapses: "it's 31,000 ads that we can see. What happens if I go to top 1%? What happens right there? We immediately see we go from 31,000 to 197 ads." Both counts SHOWN, which is why this is T2.
WHAT THE COUNTS ARE AND ARE NOT. They are counts inside a third-party library, not performance from any ad account. The 197 is what one vendor's ranking model considers the top 1%, and no one has published how that rank is computed. So the filter is a sampling method that gets you from an unreadable pile to a readable shortlist, and the shortlist is a hypothesis list rather than a winners list.
THE LONGEVITY READ, and it needs the same discipline. One competitor ad in the shortlist had been running 384 days. A long runtime proves the advertiser kept paying for it, which is genuine information and is the same logic as amount-spent-as-quality in [[Creative Science#CR-122|CR-122]]. It does not prove profitability, and a library cannot see margin, refunds or whether the account is buying revenue at a loss. Read runtime as a signal that the CONCEPT survived, never as a claim about the economics.
WHY IT IS WORTH THE SPACE. Most competitor research in this file is qualitative and unbounded. This is two filters that turn a five-figure ad pile into a shortlist you can actually read in an afternoon, and the copy-length key generalises to any format with a distinctive structural fingerprint. Pairs with [[Creative Science#CR-103|CR-103]] on what to do with competitor ads once you have them.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How to create run Native Ads with AI I show everything, 2026-07-01
Last touched: 2026-08-21

## YouTube and organic distribution

### CR-104 · On YouTube, retention and CTR on existing videos, not topic TAM, determine whether the algorithm expands distribution
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Thousands of creators use the same keywords, so the ranking lever is per-video data: strong retention signals enjoyment and YouTube serves the video more broadly. Upload gaps are a negative trust signal, while consistent cadence compounds distribution (Andrew has uploaded 3+ videos weekly since 2009 across 7,000+ videos to a 3.5M-sub channel). Facebook exchanges money for data; YouTube exchanges upload time for data.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Actually Grow a YouTube Channel (he has 3.5M subs), 2026-08-06
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-105 · Chasing off-ICP outlier topics suppresses the videos you need to perform; YouTube fills demand voids
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Shiver's off-the-cuff AI videos hit 70K+ views because AI demand outstripped supply, but the viewers were not his avatar. Colin's rule: two or three more such videos in a row can tank the channel because the algorithm re-profiles the audience, so the on-offer videos never reach their potential and the freeloader share grows with each chase.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-106 · Three outlier videos permanently reset a channel's view baseline
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The stepping pattern: at a 1K average, one 10K video lifts the next videos to 2-4K; after a 20K video the average sits 5-7K; a third outlier and the baseline is 10K+. Each outlier raises the floor rather than being an isolated spike. The catch: the three outliers must be on the right topics or the baseline resets toward the wrong audience.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-107 · Subscriber count no longer drives YouTube views; serving is fully click-prediction based
Tier: T3 · Status: active
YouTube serves each video to whoever it predicts will click, subscribed or not; legacy channels with millions of subscribers routinely get 30K views per video. Topic consistency and packaging matter, accumulated subscribers do not, and topic switches are better handled with a second channel than by confusing one algorithmic profile.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-108 · YouTube Shorts viewers and long-form viewers are separate populations
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A ~1M-subscriber client gets ~100,000 views on shorts and ~150 views on long-form. Shorts behave like a different app (podcast clips, reactions, comedy; talking-head content that works on Instagram flops on Shorts), and shorts views rarely convert into long-form viewers, so a shorts-heavy channel can look like an asset while its nurture engine is dead. Exception: pure-volume brand plays like Hormozi. Related valuation heuristic: one long-form view is worth roughly 10 short-form views, and displayed views undercount reach since CTR runs 2-10% of impressions.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-109 · Verbatim-scripted YouTube videos flop even with perfect research and packaging; long-form punishes prompter-reading
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Colin ran verbatim scripts for clients with fully researched topics, titles and thumbnails and the videos absolutely flopped at zero views. Anyone can perform for a 30-second ad but not for 30 minutes: viewers detect prompter-reading, bounce, and the video stops being pushed. Film in conditions the creator genuinely enjoys; retention follows authentic delivery, not information quality.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-110 · YouTube in 2026 is a mid-funnel nurture platform, not top-of-funnel; guaranteed discovery now requires paid ads
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Content supply outgrew demand, collapsing organic discoverability for niche offers (40 creators with 100K-sub channels all chasing the same Airbnb automation leads). The working system: paid Meta/TikTok ads for discovery, then prospects self-navigate to the channel where long-form converts them to fans then buyers; a smaller channel can even add trust for high-ticket. Paid ads also drive unattributed conversions through owned content: well into six figures of attributed revenue landed on sub-500-view videos from buyers who saw an ad, searched the creator, and converted off-track.
Ezra Firestone reaches the same conclusion from 20 years of ecommerce and attaches a minimum cadence: at least one communication a week, in email and across social, designed to educate, entertain, intrigue or interest, with NO sell in it, so the audience is primed to say yes when an offer does arrive. He is explicit that purely organic distribution now delivers very little visibility, so his teams put paid amplification behind the content system rather than relying on reach. He runs it alongside brand ambassadors, influencers, paid creators and in-house content, and treats every brand as a media company. Asserted, no data. The operating point that transfers is the pairing: a content cadence without a paid amplification line is a publishing habit, not a distribution channel.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23; Andrew Faris, Ezra Firestone: Nothing Has Changed In Ecom In 20 Years, 2026-05-15
Last touched: 2026-08-19

### CR-111 · Define an ideal viewer profile: YouTube skews ~70% male by default while Instagram/TikTok roughly invert
Tier: T3 · Status: active
YouTube's default audience is mostly male (podcasts, workouts, long-form); Instagram and TikTok skew female. Beyond the buyer avatar, define what else the ideal viewer watches and which thumbnail and editing styles they are habituated to, because a video outside a viewer's familiar format pattern will not get clicked regardless of quality. The 70% figure is stated from experience without a source.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23
Last touched: 2026-08-18

### CR-112 · Chasing views only pays for low-ticket subscription offers that extend the content; for client acquisition, a 500-view video producing 2 booked calls is the win condition
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Mass tutorial traffic converts well into cheap sticky subscriptions on the same topic (the AI-tools niche's $47/month school groups) because the product extends the video. The same view-chasing brings zero clients for consulting/service offers and fails outside AI even for subscriptions. For client-getting channels, a 500-view video producing 2 booked calls and 1 sale is the correct win condition.
The first matched pair on this claim arrived 2026-08-20, from one account inside one week: a post with 3,000 views produced 3 booked calls, and a post with 100,000 views two days earlier produced none. Same creator, same offers, same period. Until now this claim carried a stated win condition and no side-by-side. His own conclusion from it is a distribution one, that repetition in front of the same faces beats reach, with Apple's iPhone launch cadence as the stated analogy and "find what works and just reiterate it" as the instruction. Asserted, with no attribution method given for how the 3 calls were traced to the post.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How He Made $15M From YouTube (Full Breakdown), 2026-07-23; Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-166 · A call to action in every organic post cut daily lead volume from about 30 to 12, and the fix was one CTA in four posts with none on the strongest posts
Tier: T3 · Status: active
The operator was averaging about 30 quiz-funnel opt-ins a day, each carrying name, email and phone. He added a standing call to action to every post and volume fell to 12, roughly a 60% drop, at which point he pulled it back to about one post in four.
The refinement is the interesting part, because it runs against the instinct to monetise the best piece. He withholds the call to action from posts he expects to perform, which he calls a goodwill post: "on some of my best posts that I know this is a banger... I'm not going to do a call to action. I'm not going to ask you for anything. I just want you to trust me more." The stated mechanism is reader-side rather than algorithmic, that a permanent ask reads as a permanent pitch.
GUARDS, and they are heavy. One account, one operator, self-reported, no window on either number, no control for anything else that changed in the same period, and no reach or view figures reported alongside the lead figures, so a distribution shift cannot be ruled out as the cause of the drop.
Do not transfer this to paid without testing it. A paid placement exists to make the ask, and the frequency logic that governs a feed of people who chose to follow you does not obviously govern an auction where every impression is bought. The nearest thing the codex holds on the paid side is offer fatigue, which is a different mechanism from ask fatigue.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, How to Build a Quiz Funnel That Signs Clients (with Justin Mahaley), 2026-08-20
Last touched: 2026-08-21

### CR-175 · Renaming the lead magnet, with the creative held identical, cut cost per lead 45% and lifted CTR 79%
Tier: T2 · Status: active
The cleanest shown creative result in the codex for lead gen, and the variable is genuinely isolated. Matt Shiver screen-shares a client account running one static under three different lead-magnet names, with the ad itself unchanged: "The ad is the exact same, no difference, just called it something different that's clearer."
Cost per lead: "Get the blueprint" $6.10, "Get a 10-page blueprint" $4.14, "Get a 10-page cheat sheet" $3.33. Cost per click: about $2.00, $1.42, $1.00. Click-through rate: 1.50%, 2.14%, 2.68%. All read off the account on screen.
The transferable mechanism is SPECIFICITY plus a FORMAT NOUN. Adding the page count moved it once; swapping "blueprint" for "cheat sheet", which promises less work, moved it again by a similar margin.
**Two honest limits.** Spend per variant was only $30 to $50 across 3 to 4 days, so this is directional rather than a powered test. And the landing pages were NOT matched to the names, which he separately says they should be, so part of the CPL gain is click-side and he never decomposes it. CPL fell 45% while CTR rose 79%, which is consistent with most of the movement happening before the form.
**Why it matters to our book: this is the cheapest test we are not running.** Every lead-gen client we operate has a named offer, and none of them has ever had the NAME tested against a matched control. It costs one duplicate ad and a weekend.
Sources: Dr. Matt Shiver, The Fastest Way to Double Your Leads, 2026-03-31
Last touched: 2026-08-23


### CR-176 - Hook rate does NOT reliably predict cost per result, shown on screen: a 15.55% hook rate delivered 11.02 pounds per booked call while a 27% hook rate cost over 30 pounds in the same account
Tier: T2 - Status: active
The strongest single piece of shown creative evidence banked this pass, and it cuts against a metric our own work leans on.
Ben Heath builds hook rate as a custom metric live, and the definition matters because the denominator is impressions: "the first thing I want is going to be um 3-second video plays. And then what we're going to do is divide that by uh number of impressions." Meta does not provide it natively.
The spread inside one account runs about 3.5x: "our best performer in this campaign is just under 27%... we look at our worst performers when measured by hook rate, we're down at sort of like 7, 8, 9%".
**The anomaly is the finding, and it has conversion counts behind it.** "we've got 33 website schedules, so a reasonable sample size. The hook rate is 15.55% so way lower than our best performing ads... But look at this, the cost per website schedule is particularly good at 11 pounds and 2 p." The 27% hook-rate ad sat above 30 pounds per booked call on 64 conversions.
He states the correlation honestly rather than denying it: "you will often see that there's a correlation between better hook rates and better results, return on ad spend, cost per result." The correlation is real and it is loose enough that ranking creative by hook rate picks the wrong ad.
**The diagnostic that survives, and it is genuinely useful: high hook rate plus bad cost per result reads as a good hook bolted to a weak body.** "this one's got a 25.62% hook rate but a website schedule that's way above the average... that's a good hook but the rest of that video ad didn't do as good a job of convincing people to take the action that you want them to take."
**Separate the diagnostic from the prescription, because they are different tiers.** The diagnostic is T2 with rows on screen. His prescription, splicing the best hook onto the best-converting body, is T3 asserted from repeated practice with **no before-and-after ever shown for a spliced ad**: "we've done that so many times, so often that ad is a really high performer". That places him alongside Fraser Cottrell at [[Creative Science#CR-124|CR-124]] as a second operator claiming a re-cut revival and showing nothing.
Directly relevant to the folklore guard in law 4b: the 80% and 90%-never-past-the-hook figures still have no retention curve behind them anywhere, and this is the first account data suggesting the hook is not where the money is decided.
Sources: Ben Heath, Copy This EXACT Facebook Ad Itll Blow Up Your Results, 2026-03-10
Last touched: 2026-08-24

### CR-177 - The fatigue metric operators actually use is FREQUENCY above 2.0 at ad level over 14 days on cold, and it arrives with zero shown data and a self-undercutting mechanism
Tier: T3 - Status: contested
The codex has never held a settled creative-fatigue metric. This is the fullest answer on file and it should be banked with its weaknesses attached, because the source supplies most of them himself.
The rule, from Blue Sense: "at the ad level on a 14-day basis, your frequency should never be greater than a two... There is likely either creative fatigue or that ad is sitting at bottom of funnel and actually just doing a bunch of retargeting." He adds that most ads pass: "90% of the time when you do this exercise, frequency is probably going to be under a two and you're fine, and you can move on."
**Four unit disciplines travel with it and they are the most valuable part.** A frequency number without a date range is meaningless, "If you go and open up the account and you look at the last 14 days, you will have a different frequency number to if you look at the last 7 days or the last 30 days." It rises mechanically up the account structure, "frequency will always increase as you go up the account structure because it's encapsulating more audience." It must be read on the new-audience segment only, because "engaged and existing will skew it up." And it scales with time to purchase, so "frequency shouldn't be compared brand to brand."
**Why this is contested rather than active, and the objection is his own.** Reported frequency is a mean over a heavily right-skewed distribution whose shape Meta stopped exposing: "you'll end up with an average somewhere here, which might be like a 2.4. But, then the reality is is that 10% of users are actually getting targeted like 11 plus times", and "you can't actually get visibility into the distribution curve because Meta removed that feature like six years ago." A threshold on a mean whose distribution you cannot see is a weak trigger, and he says so while still selling the threshold.
He also gives a universal hard rule at ad level and then abandons universality one level up, naming happy clients at 5.0 and unhappy ones above 2.7, without explaining why time-to-purchase rescales ad-set frequency but not ad-level frequency.
**Zero data is shown. Not one frequency value, spend figure, CPA or before-and-after appears in the transcript.** He gestures at two account screenshots and reads no number off either. It cannot be promoted to T2.
**The prescription is the part worth operating on, and it is consistent with law 1: fix frequency in the creative, never in the ad set.** "you always want to be fixing frequency at the creative level, not the ad set level... The frequency isn't the issue, the creative is the issue, and the frequency is the product of it." Adding more ads that speak to the same person does not lower it. Angle diversity does.
Counter-position on file the same week: Charley T reads frequency as funnel position rather than fatigue, "Lower frequency usually means the ad is being used to find new people. Higher frequency usually means Meta is using that to retarget individuals", and treats high frequency with low spend as the signature of an operator who keeps resetting the system. Both men are asserting.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, The Most Important Metric for Creative Fatigue, 2026-04-20; Charley T, The NEW BEST Way to Analyze Facebook Ads in 2026 4Pi after Andromeda, 2026-06-23
Last touched: 2026-08-24

### CR-178 - Two operators independently define "a different ad" as a three-variable change, and the two definitions are the same shape
Tier: T3 - Status: active
Blue Sense: "we define concept as persona, angle, offer. And so if you change one of these variables, it changes the concept." His structural consequence is that a new concept earns a new ad set.
Nick Theriot, from the copy side, uses the same arity with one term swapped: "you basically ask yourself, does this hit on the same awareness, avatar, desire as the ads already live inside the ad account?" His consequence is a reuse rule rather than a structure rule: if all three match, copy the account's best primary text verbatim; if any one changes, write fresh.
**Persona and avatar are the same variable. Offer and desire are close. Angle and awareness stage are not the same thing, and that is the one real difference between them.**
Why it is worth banking: the codex has carried "concept-level diversity, angle x offer x persona" since law 2 without a second independent source for the arity. Two practitioners with unrelated books landing on a three-variable test, one for structure and one for copy reuse, is convergence worth recording. It is still two assertions and neither has run a test.
Practical use for us: this is a cheap decision rule for the copy-craft lane. Same awareness, avatar and desire means reuse the winning primary text rather than write new. Our own batches routinely write fresh copy for creatives that differ on none of the three, which is work spent for no expected gain.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How To Structure Meta For High Creative Volume, 2026-03-24; Nick Theriot, How To Write Facebook Ads Primary Text That CONVERTS in 2026, 2026-07-06

### CR-179 - AI creative is STILL a production-economics story, now across four more transcripts, and the first named boundary condition arrived
Tier: T3 - Status: active
[[Creative Science#CR-169|CR-169]] found that every AI-creative claim in the codex is about speed, cost or volume, and that not one runs a comparison against non-AI creative. **This pass read today's fresh AI tutorial plus three creative-heavy transcripts and the pattern held.**
The 2026-08-23 harvest is a sponsored software tutorial. The ad demonstrated in it was built during filming and never ran: no impression, no click, no CPA, no spend, no before, no after. It is thinner than what we already hold, because it gives no credit cost and no dollar figure at all, only "It probably took me like 5 minutes or so" for 13 start frames and a 15-minute claim in the title. The word viral in the title refers to the format, not to this ad's results.
**The one non-production claim in it is T4 with nothing behind it and it runs against our own house line.** "I'm going to show you exactly how you can make AI videos that are so good and so obviously AI that people don't feel bad about watching them." Our own aesthetic rule is documentary realism. Log it as an open hypothesis, never as evidence, and do not let it into a client recommendation.
**The genuinely new item is a boundary condition, and it comes from Theriot rather than from the AI tutorial.** "They do struggle a bit though when you start to sell to people that need some type of proof, like weight loss or hair loss or something related to skin, where people get proof from real people using the product. So, more technical products and feature focused products, they work really well." That is the first claim in the corpus naming where AI creative LOSES rather than what it saves. Still T3, still no comparison, and it is directly relevant because chiropractic pain relief is a proof-dependent category.
**The non-AI production baseline our codex was missing, also from Theriot:** human UGC at "$200 to $300 per video" running "three to four weeks sometimes depending on shipping times", and influencer creative at "a minimum of $1,000 per video". Those are the numbers an AI production saving should be measured against.
**Ranking discipline unchanged: label these production-economics claims when repeating them, and never tell a client AI creative performs better.** We ship both kinds on five accounts and remain the best-positioned party to settle it with a matched pair.
Not banked: the "450 million in tracked ad spend" and "30% reduction in their CPA" figures from the same tutorial. They sit in a mid-roll course pitch, attach to nothing in the video, and are not an AI-creative result.
Sources: Fraser Cottrell, I Made This VIRAL AI Ad in 15 Min Claude Arcads Tutorial, 2026-08-23; Nick Theriot, The 7 Levels of Facebook Ad Creatives THAT CONVERT, 2026-06-17

### CR-180 - The spend ceiling of an ad is set by the SIZE OF THE DESIRE its hook names, and you can pre-size that desire before writing
Tier: T3 - Status: active
The most operationally usable claim in this pass, and the first mechanism anyone has offered for why a well-made ad refuses to scale.
Nick Theriot walks a spend ladder across four ads for the same brand, figures read off Ads Manager: one "spent well over $100,000 driving over 5,000 purchases", one "spent eighty-eight hundred dollars driving over four hundred purchases for this product with a twenty-one dollar CPA", one "spent $1,000", one "only spent $200". **Observational ladder across four ads with no controlled variable, so T3 and not T2 despite the numbers being on screen.**
His attribution is the desire's addressable size, not the craft: the $200 ad's hook, "how to romanticize your life", failed because "this desire here is not a strong desire. It doesn't have a large TAM compared to other desires like how to have a fun summer".
**The pre-flight test, which costs minutes and nobody on our book does it:** "I would actually go look for yourself and look at the number of people that are searching how to romanticize your life on like Tik Tok and Instagram and YouTube, and you'll know there's very few videos that actually talk about this particular desire."
Two supporting rules from the same file. **A demographic callout alone is not a hook**, said of an ad that correctly named its avatar ("Everyone always talks about how amazing being retired is") and still failed: "the hook lacks, what I would say, um emotional pull and like a reason to watch this." And **recurring-occasion desires scale because the need re-fires**, which is why "girls night" outruns a one-off.
**This agrees with our own house rule from the opposite direction.** We say lead with the problem, never a demographic. His evidence is an ad that led with the demographic, named the avatar accurately, and could not spend.
Sources: Nick Theriot, How to Create Facebook Ads that Scale to 100000 after creating 3000 ads, 2026-08-24; Nick Theriot, The Psychology of Facebook Ads that Scale, 2026-06-29

### CR-181 - Charley T counts delivery COMBINATIONS as "ads", and a 3x2x2 is 12 of them, which finally closes the unit question under law 4a
Tier: T3 - Status: active
[[Scaling Models#SC-136|SC-136]] retracted our invented "roughly 60 combinations" and left his real unit unstated. He states it himself, unprompted and three times, on 4 June 2026.
"This is a 322 ad. Three creatives, two headlines, two primary texts." Then: "**That's 12 possible combinations.**" Then, scaling it: "if you want to test more than three creatives, you don't stack them into one ad, you build multiple 322s. If you have six creatives, that's just two 322 ads, which is **already 24 ads**."
**So when he says "24 ads" he means 24 delivery combinations carried by TWO ad objects. Any ledger row quoting an ad count from him has to be labelled combinations or it is wrong by an order of magnitude.** That is a third distinct unit in law 4a, alongside live-concurrent and launched-per-period at [[Creative Science#CR-154|CR-154]], and it is the one most likely to be mistaken for the others.
Two operating rules attach. **Never mix images and videos inside one 3x2x2:** "Three videos or three images, not a mix. We want Meta comparing like for like." And **never overload past three creatives**, because the combination count explodes and an early-lucky combination captures everything: "a 322 is already 12 ads. So, if you load five or 10 creatives, that could potentially be hundreds whatever gets lucky early is going to dominate everything. Only because at your warmest audience, it was the thing people clicked on first. And this is why a lot of folks would say flex ads don't work."
**Correction to our own record: the "control ad set of 4 to 8 ads" figure at SC-136 does NOT appear in any of these five transcripts.** The control ad set itself is confirmed and named as the destination for harvested winners. The 4-to-8 count is sourced elsewhere and should not be re-quoted from this batch.

### CR-182 - Three more ad-count units landed on 2026-08-25, and for the first time the LIVE-CONCURRENT question has a real answer from two operators
Tier: T3 - Status: contested
Feeds law 4a. Every figure below carries its unit, which is the only way these numbers have ever been comparable.
**Sam Piliero, LIVE CONCURRENT per ad set: 4 to 8, hard range 2 to 10.** "Inside those, there could be anywhere from two creatives in a pack to 10. Really, the sweet spot tends to be four to eight ads in a pack." **His counting unit is now pinned beyond doubt**, because in a separate file he states Dynamic Creative and Flex Media are both off: "We are not selecting dynamic creative, we're not selecting flexible media, we're literally running normal ads." So these are 4 to 8 discrete ad objects. This supersedes the "4 to 6" previously on file for him and is a slight widening, not a conflict.
**Sam Piliero, LAUNCHED per week, the Rule of 10,000:** "For every $10,000 per month in ad spend that is equivalent to how many ads you want to upload per week with a minimum threshold of two. That means if you spend $100,000, divide that by 10,000, getting you 10, you should be uploading 10 ads per week." **This is the first launch rate on file that is keyed to spend rather than to headcount or to a flat weekly habit**, and it is directly usable on our own book: every client we run sits under $30k/month, so the rule prescribes 2 to 3 new ads a week.
**Jon Loomer, LIVE at launch: 1 to 2 ad objects, and creatives inside an ad object are explicitly NOT counted separately.** "Truthfully, you can launch with just one or two... If you have the new creative workflow, you could technically create one ad with at least one static image and one video. Otherwise, create two ads. One with an image and one with a video. In each case, use five primary text and headline options." He gives the mechanism for why launching more is pointless: "You certainly can start with five or 10 ads, but only if you're okay with what happens next. Meta may choose to run with only one or two of those ads."
**Jon Loomer, CUMULATIVE live in one ad set: 10 to 15 is where he considers creative volume exhausted** and starts looking at non-creative levers. "You now have 10 to 15 ads, and you're still not getting the results that you want in aggregate."
**Fraser Cottrell, LAUNCHED per week at a stated budget:** "let's say you're at $50 a day. At that point, I'd probably be launching maybe one or two brand new ads every week." Dynamic Creative is never mentioned in any of his four files, so his unit is unverifiable.
**⚠ Nick Theriot still states NO live count, after EIGHT transcripts read in full.** Two more were read today. His only figures remain cumulative production and agency spend. This has now been an open question for four consecutive passes and the honest answer is that he has never given one.
**What the spread actually is, once units are held fixed: Loomer 1 to 2 live at launch against Piliero 4 to 8 against Heath 20-plus, all per ad set, all live-concurrent, all discrete ad objects. That is a genuine 10x disagreement between three operators with real books and zero comparative tests.** It is smaller than the raw numbers used to suggest, and it is real.

**⚠ 2026-08-26 adds a FIFTH operator and, more importantly, a variable that none of the four above controlled for. The live-concurrent count depends on the CAMPAIGN TYPE, and one operator gives two different numbers in one video for exactly that reason.**
Blue Sense, 2025-03-31, states both halves in consecutive breaths. **In ABO or CBO: 3 to 6 live per ad set, hard bounds.** "this goes against every SOP that we've ever had with regular ABOS or regular CBOS, which is that **we always want to restrict ads to three to six ads in an adset. Six is the absolute maximum. Three is really the absolute minimum.**" **In Advantage+: as many as possible, floor of 10.** "we were launching advantage plus campaigns when they first rolled out just like we would with ABOS's three four five ads in advantage plus and then we started doing some tests introducing **30 40 50 ads in advantage plus and performance only improved and so you want as many ads in here as possible**", with "you really want to have at a base minimum within an advantage plus campaign 10 plus active ads."
**So the same operator, on the same day, runs 3 to 6 and 30 to 50 depending on which campaign type the ad set sits in. That is a 10x spread inside ONE operator, which is the same size as the spread between Loomer and Heath above.** Before this, law 4a's disagreement had three candidate explanations: live versus launched, ad objects versus creative assets, and Dynamic Creative on or off. **Campaign type is a fourth, and it is the first one that can produce a 10x gap without anybody changing their counting unit.**
**Ask which campaign type before comparing any two live-ad counts.** Piliero's 4 to 8 is stated for ad packs in a normal campaign with Dynamic Creative and Flex Media explicitly off. Loomer's 1 to 2 is at launch. Heath's 20-plus has never had a campaign type attached to it in any transcript, and on this reading his number is only reconcilable with the others if he is describing Advantage+.
**The 30-40-50 figure is a described TEST that reports no numbers, so tier it honestly.** "performance only improved" is the entire result: no CPA, no ROAS, no spend, no before and after. It would be T2 if he had shown the deltas. He did not. Same shape as [[Creative Science#CR-183|CR-183]].
**One further boundary from the same operator, filed at [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-137|MD-137]] and load-bearing here: how many ads Meta FUNDS is a function of account spend, roughly 20 at $200k+/month and roughly one at $20-30k/month.** So part of this law's 10x disagreement may be four operators describing four different account sizes rather than disagreeing about a rule.

### CR-183 - A head-to-head structure shootout was described in full and reported with ZERO figures, and it must be logged T4 precisely because it sounds like T2
Tier: T4 - Status: active
**Banked as a guard, not as evidence.** This engine has retracted three invented numbers in three days, and every one of them started as a plausible figure attached to a real operator. This is the shape of the next one, caught before it hardened.
Charley T sets up what is, in structure, exactly the controlled test this codex has been asking for on CBO structure: "Here is a real world example. This is the same ad account in a direct shootout with another strategy you may recognize. **Same product, same audience, same ads. The only thing that changed was the structure.** The one campaign or Andromeda 1 running correctly versus everything else. We started at the same daily budget, but the outcomes were dramatically different. And that's not luck. That's not a good month."
**Same product, same audience, same ads, same starting budget, one variable changed. Then no ROAS, no CPA, no spend, no duration, no lift, no screen. Nothing.** The entire result is carried by the phrase "dramatically different".
**Rule attached: any future note that assigns a number to this comparison is inventing it.** If a figure for this shootout ever appears in this codex, it did not come from this transcript.
The surrounding framework is separately bankable as T3 and does not depend on the missing numbers: a CBO holding one control ad set plus at most two single-ad test ad sets, each test aimed at beating the worst ad in the control rather than the campaign as a whole ("To improve the single worst ad in the control ad set, not to beat the whole campaign, not to find a unicorn, just to improve upon the weakest player on the team"); a two-step read order of delivery first then profit volume ("Step one, delivery. Did the test ads earn spend? If no, the ads aren't competitive enough and the test is dead on arrival"); and the sharpest idea in the file, that a winning test converts into a scale test rather than into more testing ("Your creative test just became a stress test... You keep adding budget until you can't").
**One internal inconsistency to carry: the two metrics driving his system use different definitions of profit and he never reconciles them.** His pass/fail test is "profit volume, total revenue minus total ad spend", which excludes COGS. His replacement-target test is gross profit per transaction, which includes margin.
**A useful reframe survives independent of the missing data**, and it opposes the usual budget-tier advice: complexity should be gated by conversion volume, not by spend. "I've run $1,000 a day budget campaigns that only get 30 sales a week and $200 a day budget campaigns to get more than 65. $1,000 a day isn't a low budget, but 30 sales a week is a low number of conversions." His ladder is gated on ad sets you can hold out of learning, and he caps himself: "even when I'm spending a million a month, I'm not getting more complicated than that." Also note this file never defines "322" anywhere, so do not expand the acronym from this source; the definition is banked separately at [[Creative Science#CR-181|CR-181]].
Sources: Professor Charley T, The SIMPLE Facebook Ads Strategy Dominating in 2026, 2026-08-25

### CR-184 - The most useful thing an operator published this pass is a list of SEVEN things he refuses to spend time on, and the reasoning is scope rather than evidence
Tier: T4 - Status: active
Jon Loomer names seven levers he ignores outright. Nothing is shown, no account, no figures, so tier it T4 and use it as a prior on where operator attention is wasted, never as proof any lever is inert.
1. **Placements.** "If you're optimizing for any type of conversion, I spend little to no time worrying about placements... Yes, placements are a source of low-quality clicks and impressions, but only if your goal is clicks or impressions. When you want conversions, Meta will spend very little of your budget there."
2. **Detailed targeting and lookalikes.** Note the careful form: he claims unmeasurability, not inertness. "we have no idea if Meta leans into those inputs in some cases or ignores them in others. They're unlikely to hurt results, but any evidence of their impact is thin and difficult to prove." This is the same position as law 1a from the other direction.
3. **Age and gender**, with a named alternative that matches [[Auction Mechanics & Bidding#AU-019|AU-019]]: "Shrinking my potential audience won't optimize performance. It only drives up costs... And when that problem exists, I use value rules instead."
4. **Location inconsistency**, and he scopes himself OUT of our situation while saying it, which is the part that matters for our book: "granted, I have very little reason to worry about this cuz I'm not running ads as a local business." **Every client we run is local. Do not import item 4.**
5. **Which ad gets the delivery**, with the combinatorics argument underneath it: "If you have the new creative workflow, one ad works much the same way as one ad set once did. It can include up to 10 images and videos... up to five primary text and headline options... we're dealing with potentially millions of combinations." **That mechanism, not any number, is the real support for his small ad counts at [[Creative Science#CR-182|CR-182]].** The two product limits are recited from memory with no documentation shown, so verify before quoting them as T1.
6. **Enhancements degrading creative.** Read this one precisely, because it is weaker than it looks and it collides with [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-128|MD-128]]: he does NOT say ignore them. "I customize enhancements where possible. I turn off those that are completely irrelevant. But I otherwise don't care if a handful of people saw this version." Our Flex Media audit still stands.
7. **Reconciling Ads Manager against other analytics**, with four structural reasons the gap exists: click-through does not require an immediate purchase, engage-through and view-through involve no site click, incremental attribution is modelled, and third parties cannot fill the tracking holes. "It doesn't mean that Meta is lying or making up conversions." The residue is actionable: "value the clicks most. Make sure the events are set up properly and deduplicated when sent from multiple sources."
Bonus item, relevant to our exclusion practice and to [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-123|MD-123]]: "I don't care that custom audience exclusions are incomplete." That is a third independent voice reporting exclusion leakage as normal.
**Quote his own caveat with any item taken from this list, because he states the exceptions exist and does not enumerate them:** "I care very little about these things because I realize they're outside of my control... I understand there are exceptions when these things matter more, and sometimes these exceptions apply to me."

### CR-185 · Two spend-keyed creative-volume rules now exist and they disagree by 2.3x, and the operator who states the tighter one refutes it with his own two accounts
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
Feeds law 4a. Until 2026-08-25 the codex had exactly one launch rate keyed to spend rather than to headcount or habit. It now has two, and they do not agree.
**Blue Sense, one net new ad per $1,000 of monthly spend.** Stated three times with the arithmetic consistent at every point: "If you're spending 50k a month on ads, generally you want at least 50 net new concepts or net new ads within the account", "If you're spending 100k a month, we'd like a 100", "If you go down to 20K, 20 net new ads a month is a good ballpark", and the ratio spoken outright, "**But for every thousand, one net new ad.**" *Arithmetic check: 20,000/1,000 = 20, 50,000/1,000 = 50, 100,000/1,000 = 100. All three stated points reconcile exactly with the stated ratio.*
**Sam Piliero's Rule of 10,000 at [[Creative Science#CR-182|CR-182]], converted to the same unit: one ad per week per $10,000 of monthly spend is roughly 4.3 ads a month per $10,000, or one ad per ~$2,300 of monthly spend.** So Blue Sense prescribes about 2.3x more new creative per dollar than Piliero. At the $20,000/month level Blue Sense says 20 new ads a month and Piliero says about 9.
**The exclusion Blue Sense attaches is what makes his number bigger than it looks, and it should travel with the figure: hook variants of existing winners do not count.** "So, I'm not talking about 50 slight variations, taking a couple high performers and throwing a bunch of hooks on there. Generally, we'd like 50 **proper new ads** within the account." Under Piliero's unit a re-cut may well count. **The two rules may therefore be closer than 2.3x, or further apart, and nobody has stated the other side's inclusion rule.**
**⚠ THE STRONGEST EVIDENCE IN THIS CLAIM ARGUES AGAINST BOTH RULES, and it comes from the operator stating one of them.** "We have brands spending $100,000 launching 200 ads per month. We have brands spending $100,000 launching 30 ads per month **with very similar efficiency levels.**" *Arithmetic: 200 ads at $100k is one per $500; 30 ads at $100k is one per $3,333; his own rule predicts 100. Neither real account matches it, and they differ from each other by 6.7x at identical spend with efficiency he calls similar.* He concedes the point himself in the same breath, "that is the reality of this recommendation is that relatively subjective", and labels the whole framework "I'm going to give you subjective numbers right now".
**A fixed ads-per-spend ratio is therefore not supported by the only paired real-account evidence anybody has offered for one.** Use these as order-of-magnitude anchors, never as targets, and never quote either without the 6.7x counterexample attached.
**A stated exception to the ratio, which is a second regime rather than a contradiction:** once a winner bank exists, the requirement decays at flat spend. "if you're doing 50 net new ads per month, after about six to 12 months of doing this, you'll have so many winners pre-existing within the account that you actually don't need to continue to introduce 50 new ads a month. You could drop this down to 10, maybe 20." That is one ad per $2,500 to $5,000 at the same $50,000, so an account holding spend steady with an established bank needs 2.5x to 5x less new creative than one trying to scale.
**Scaling behaviour, and the absence is the finding: he says the ratio is non-linear and gives no exponent.** "you can actually scale spend more exponentially than ad volume requirements increase um if you're global. If you're in Australia, it actually becomes very tough. It's actually the opposite way. You actually need exponentially more ads because the TAM is so small." **"Exponentially" appears twice with zero figures attached. Do not supply one.** The TAM mechanism behind it is at [[Scaling Models#SC-144|SC-144]].
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, How to Audit Your Ad Account for Scale Step-by-Step Guide, 2025-12-10
Last touched: 2026-08-26

### CR-186 · Creative hit rate is claimed to cap at 13 to 14% of all ads ever introduced, with a stated statistical definition and a self-contradiction in the next sentence
Tier: T3 · Status: contested
The most instrumentable creative-volume claim in the codex, because it is the only one that ships with an explicit definition, and also the one most likely to be an artefact of that definition.
The claim: "if you ever look at average hit rates throughout an account, **you'll really never see an account with a hit rate above 13 to 14%.** Which means that of all ads introduced into an account over time, average high performers **measured by above one standard deviation above the mean performance of an ad**, it's rarely ever above 15%."
**Two things to fix before this is ever quoted.** First, *arithmetic check: he states a hard ceiling of 13 to 14% and then restates the same quantity as "rarely ever above 15%", joined by "which means that", which asserts they are one claim. They are two ceilings one to two points apart.* Second, and more serious, **the definition may manufacture the number.** "More than one standard deviation above the mean" is a floating threshold, and on a right-skewed distribution, which ad performance always is, the share of observations above one SD lands in roughly this range by construction. **So the 13 to 15% may be a property of the statistic rather than a fact about advertising.** Nobody has tested that, and testing it needs only one account's ad-level export.
**A second, incompatible figure from the same operator 49 days earlier: "if you have a 5 or a 10% hit rate in the account, if you go and launch 10 to 20 ugly ads that you can just quickly whip up on your phone, um you will see performance out of one of them."** *Arithmetic check, and it fails at the low end: 5% x 10 ads = 0.5 expected winners, which is a coin flip, not "you will see performance out of one of them". 10% x 20 = 2.0. The claim only holds at the top of both ranges.* A typical rate can legitimately sit below a ceiling, so 5-10% and 13-15% are not strictly contradictory, but they are two framings of one quantity with no data behind either. **Do not average them into a single figure.**
**Related, and a warning label rather than a claim: the 5 to 10x expected-value ratio of a winner over a non-winner** that he reports across "every single data set I've ever seen" is computed by his own creative calculator, which takes an assumed hit rate and an assumed spend-per-winner as inputs. **The output is partly definitional, not an independent measurement.** Same caution as the hit-rate ceiling: check what the number is made of before treating it as evidence.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Is Creative Velocity A Vanity Metric, 2025-05-08; Blue Sense Digital, 1 vs 1000 Creatives in Meta Ads, 2025-03-20
Last touched: 2026-08-26

### CR-187 · GUARD: the best-known "traffic campaigns destroy your account" argument contains no test, no data, and an on-camera refusal to run one
Tier: T4 · Status: active
Banked as a ceiling on a piece of folklore we are likely to meet in a client conversation, not as a finding about traffic campaigns.
Blue Sense published a video titled "Traffic Campaigns Are Killing Your Meta Results (Here's Why)" and then says, in it: "Traffic campaigns though don't hold up in any way, shape, or form. **And I've never seen any data set that actually proves it.**" He goes further and rules out ever producing one: "I don't want to test it on our clients cuz I don't think it's going to work. I think it's guaranteed going to um fail." And he asks the audience to supply what he lacks: "I actually really want people to run them because I want some data on this so I don't just have to make theoretical videos."
**His mechanism is worth recording because it is falsifiable and because it is NOT the one the folklore usually gives.** He claims Meta sorts USERS into objective pools by their historic ad behaviour and prices CPM to their purchase intent, so a traffic campaign can only buy from people who click and never buy. **The word "pixel" does not appear anywhere in the transcript. He never claims the pixel is poisoned, corrupted or mis-trained.** The whole argument rests on one premise he flags as unsourced twice in three sentences: "You cannot simultaneously coexist in the traffic objective and the sales objective from what I'm aware. **Unless there is documentation out there that says otherwise, please comment it below and let me know.**"
**⚠ THE MOST IMPORTANT NEGATIVE RESULT: neither this file nor its companion claims cross-campaign contamination.** No claim that running a traffic campaign degrades conversion campaigns elsewhere in the account, no claim that damage persists after the campaign stops, no account-level learning-degradation claim. **If the codex or a client deck ever carries "traffic campaigns poison the account", this source does not support it.**
**One genuinely useful diagnostic survives, and it is cheap:** segment landing-page sessions by referrer UTM and compare dwell time. "Average time on site if users to a traffic campaign is pretty much always sub 1 second. Whereas on a sales campaign, you can have a look as well, it'll probably be somewhere 7 8 seconds depending on the quality of the landing page." T3, repeated observation, no dataset shown, and both figures hedged.
**Two numbers in this file must never be repeated as findings.** *Arithmetic failure:* he states sales CPM about $25 versus traffic CPM about $10 and calls it "about 70% cheaper"; the components give 60%. *Quarantined figure:* a "27% better performance" result he attributes to Meta and then discredits himself in the same sentence, "but you don't know the brands, you don't know the test design, etc." It has no provenance beyond an unnamed Meta claim introduced in order to be rejected.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Traffic Campaigns Are Killing Your Meta Results Heres Why, 2025-10-01

### CR-188 · Theriot deliberately runs three NEAR-IDENTICAL creatives in one ad set at $6M/30-day scale, which is the anti-collapse pole law 2 lost yesterday
Tier: T3 · Status: active
[[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-003|MD-003]] lost its anti-collapse witness on 2026-08-26 when Blue Sense turned out to hold both positions five days apart. This is the replacement, and it is better sourced than the one it replaces, because it is a standing practice rather than an opinion.
Nick Theriot, 2026-08-26, opening on scale: "Over the last 30 days, we spent over $6 million on ads at my ad agency, specifically for our clients."
**His standard testing unit is three creatives per ad set that differ in exactly one element.** Images: "The three images are going to have the same text hook, just a different background on each of those images." Videos, and this is as near-duplicate as it gets: "this is the same exact video three times, all have the same body, same length, same everything, but the visual hook is different."
**Why this is evidence.** If Meta bundled near-duplicates into one entity with zero unique reach, this practice would be self-defeating, and it is not a stray test, it is how every ad set in the book is built. That is an operational bet placed with $6,000,000 over 30 days against the collapse thesis. Note the boundary it respects: the varied element is the VISUAL in both cases, which is consistent with the scope already recorded at MD-003, where the rebuttal is limited to swaps that change the visual.
**What has NOT changed, and this is the ninth month of it.** He shows no entity ID, no similarity score and no unique-reach figure, exactly like everyone else on both sides. **The evidentiary hole is unchanged; only the roster of the anti-collapse side changed.**
**A second Theriot practice cuts the other way and belongs in the same entry.** He also expands by swapping the person: "we'll test different ethnicities of people uh with our winning messaging and our winning creative to be able to appeal to more people, so we can spend more and scale", and reports finding a winner "by creating the exact same ad but a female version". That corroborates [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-001|MD-001]]'s avatar mechanism from a third operator. **NO NUMBER GIVEN for either the female version or the bag-avatar win.**
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Lower Facebook Ads CPA in 2026 4 Things I Fix First, 2026-08-26

### CR-189 · A landing page beat a product page by roughly $20 CPA, and the operator states the winning number two incompatible ways
Tier: T2 for the test, with an arithmetic conflict attached · Status: active
Banked with the failure attached rather than smoothed, per the check added 2026-08-26.
Nick Theriot ran the clean version of this test, one ad launched twice against the same audience with only the destination changed: "we launched this ad twice, one with a PDP and one with a landing page."
**Statement one, mid-video:** "the landing page spent 10 times more compared to the PDP with a nearly $20, $22 cheaper cost per purchase compared to um, the PDP itself with an $85 cost per purchase." That puts the PDP at $85 and the landing page at roughly $63 to $65.
**Statement two, in his own recap minutes later:** "spent 10 times more with a $20 cost per purchase." That puts the landing page at $20.
**These cannot both be true. $20 CHEAPER than $85 is $65, not $20.** The recap understates the landing page's cost by roughly 3x. A third statement, "we lowered cost per acquisition by $20 by doing this", agrees with statement one, so the weight of his own testimony favours $65 and the recap is the error. **Quote the delta, never the level: a roughly $20 improvement on an $85 baseline is what this test supports.**
Supporting components he reports moving the right way: cheaper cost per link click and better CPMs, with "CVR roughly stayed the same but the traffic was cheaper."
**Scope guards.** One ad, one client, one product, no date range, no spend figure, no significance test, and the 10x spend difference means the two arms were never equally funded. His explanation for WHY it worked is a separate and unsupported claim, quarantined at [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-144|MD-144]]. **A future run must not merge the two: the result is T2 and the mechanism is T4.**
Sources: Nick Theriot, How To Lower Facebook Ads CPA in 2026 4 Things I Fix First, 2026-08-26

### CR-190 · The Ads Library now sorts by IMPRESSIONS and shows inactive ads, which repairs the broken "long-running equals winner" heuristic
Tier: T1, read off the product · Status: active
The cheapest competitor-research upgrade in the codex, and it comes with a correction to a rule this codex has repeated.
Ben Heath, 2026-04-14, walking the Library. Two capabilities that did not previously exist: "That is the ability to sort by impressions high to low", and "you can look at inactive ads as well as active ads. That's not something you used to be able to do", reached via "Just make sure in filters, you've got active status as active and inactive."
**The correction.** The old rule was runtime: "And if an ad has been active for more than 6 months a year, it probably is a best performer." He keeps it and breaks it in the same breath, because zombie ads sit active on zero spend, a problem he attributes to delivery changes: "a problem that was getting worse given how Meta Ads delivery system has updated". **The repaired signal is the intersection, not either alone:** "lots of impressions plus um been running for a long time is as close to a guarantee".
**A load-bearing filter note that will cause misreads if missed.** The Library's location filter is where the ad is DELIVERED, not where the advertiser sits: "the location you set is not where you are, or even where the competitor is based." Every geo-scoped competitor pull we run depends on that.
The detail panel also exposes the exact run window and the variation count under one ad entry, e.g. "We can see there's the three variations of this ad that was run", which is a free read on the ad-object-versus-creative-asset counting problem at [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-124|MD-124]].
Sources: Ben Heath, How To Spy On Your Competitors Meta Ads for FREE, 2026-04-14

### CR-191 · Creative production budget as a share of media spend: 10%+ above $100k/mo, 25% at $30k/mo, and the worked model's numbers are self-declared FAKE
Tier: T3 for the allocation bands, T4 for the model · Status: active
Blue Sense, 2025-10-14, on a real starved account: "We have an ad account right now that's spending 300K per month on Meta."
**The bands, stated as anecdote and labelled as such by him:** "those that allocate about 10% or more of their budget to creative production end up seeing really good results", explicitly "based on anecdotal experience from the clients that we've worked with". The share must rise as spend falls: "When you're spending only 30k a month, you really want to be looking at something like 25% allocated to production costs." Below $5k a month there is no external budget at all and "the founder needs to be involved in content creation".
**Read the bands against our own book before using them. Every client we run sits under $30k a month, so the 25% end applies, not the 10% end.**
**The critical guard, and he supplies it himself on the record: every figure in the worked model is invented.** "I'm going to start making up numbers, so I'm not actually giving away the real numbers of this business." **So the $50 CPA, the $20K reallocation and the 6,000-conversion baseline are illustrative and must never be quoted as an account result.**
**The arithmetic inside the fake model is nonetheless correct, which is worth stating because it is the rarer outcome in this corpus.** $300K at $50 CPA is 6,000 conversions; moving $20K to production leaves $280K, which must beat 6,000 conversions, requiring a CPA at or under $46.67. His sweep lands exactly there, rejecting $48 and $47 and accepting $46, and his stated threshold "we need to see a drop in CPA by at least $4" is the correct conservative rounding.
**What is NOT in the file: any outcome.** The reallocation is a forecast, stated as a certainty it has not earned, "we will guaranteed to get at least a $4 drop in CPA by doing this over a 60-day time period". **NO OUTCOME NUMBER GIVEN.** Production rate at $20K/month is given as "about 60 new video assets per month", which computes to $333 per video.
Sources: Blue Sense Digital, Heres How Much You Should Be Spending On Creative, 2025-10-14

### CR-192 · Consolidating 36 ads into 6 is reported with no performance number at all
Tier: T3 · Status: active
A no-number guard, banked because the ad-count debate at law 4a keeps acquiring figures that were never measured.
Jon Loomer, 2026-05-04, using the new multimedia workflow: "I took what was previously 36 ads and consolidated it into six." **NO PERFORMANCE NUMBER GIVEN.** No CPA, no ROAS, no delivery change, no before and after of any kind. The entire justification is structural: "These new six ads are truly diverse, and I eliminated the repetition that Meta discourages."
**He then states the neutrality rule that his own episode argues against:** "Now, fewer ads isn't necessarily better, just as more ads isn't necessarily worse." The reconciliation he implies is that the variable is creative DIVERSITY and the count is a side effect. **Anyone citing 36-to-6 as evidence that consolidation improves results is citing a structural preference as a result.**
Note the mechanical cause of the 6x collapse: the workflow at [[Meta Delivery & Andromeda#MD-124|MD-124]] holds up to 10 assets in one ad object, so 36 assets fit in 6 objects with no creative removed. **The asset count did not fall. Only the object count fell.** This is the counting trap at MD-124 happening in the wild, in a published note, by a careful operator.
Sources: Jon Loomer, Dont Get Attached to Your Ad Process, 2026-05-04

### CR-193 · Putting the exact clicked video at the top of the landing page: three accounts, and not one performance number
Tier: T3 · Status: active
Pairs directly with the LP-versus-PDP result at [[Creative Science#CR-189|CR-189]], and it is the mechanism-free version of the same idea: make the page continuous with the ad.
**The spec, which is concrete enough to build from.** Above the fold sits "a full 4x5 view of the video that they just clicked on playing in real time in the background with no noise", with a sticky add-to-cart and price laid over it, and listicle-style education below. The stated failure it replaces is one page serving every angle: "they all land on the same landing page, which then has 5,000 benefits listed".
**⚠ The evidence is a spend level, not a result, and this is the whole guard.** The only figure in the file is "We took them from spending pretty much nothing to $1,000 a day within about a 7-day period". **Spend is an INPUT.** There is no ROAS, no CPA, no conversion rate, no AOV and no click-through rate anywhere in the transcript. Breadth is asserted without measurement too: "We've now tested this on three separate accounts and seen really good success across all of them", with no test design, control or metric for any of the three.
**So the honest statement is that a named operator scaled an account to $1,000/day within a week of shipping this page and attributes it to the page. That is a hypothesis worth testing, not a result.**
He predicts his own tactic decays once page generation is automated: "I think that this opportunity will probably just be arbitrageed out of the market." He also names the reason nobody tests upper-funnel page strategies, which is a measurement problem rather than a creative one: you "just can't run strategies like that without being able to have clarity over the incremental impact".
